  • AUDIT ADDITION — brief US counsel on FIN-2019-G001, not only FIN-2013-G001

    ISSUER
    FinCEN (US Treasury)
    COST
    $0
    LEAD TIME
    immediate
    APPLIES TO
    A (clearance), B (MSB determination)

    Recorded as a discrete action because the original brief built BOTH the Model A 'no money transmission' clearance AND the Model B administrator/MSB determination on 2013 guidance while never mentioning the 2019 consolidation — superseded authority on the most consequential legal question in the lane. FIN-2019-G001, 'Application of FinCEN's Regulations to Certain Business Models Involving Convertible Virtual Currencies', 9 May 2019, states it 'consolidates current FinCEN regulations, and related administrative rulings and guidance issued since 2011, and then applies these rules and interpretations to obligations under the BSA as they relate to current and emerging business models'. It addresses models the 2013 guidance does not. Give counsel the 2019 document as the primary reference and the 2013 one as the source of the underlying definitions.

United States

Model B (buy coins -> stake player-v-player -> redeem for cash or prizes) is unlawful as designed, and no commercially viable licensed path exists: about ten states criminalise wagering on contests of SKILL by statute or judicial construction regardless of the skill/chance debate (AZ, AR, CA, DE, FL, IL, LA, NV, SC, SD, plus TN and WA through near-any-chance and material-element tests) — MONTANA IS REMOVED FROM THAT LIST, the statute the brief cited for it does not say what the brief said; and roughly TEN states enacted express dual-currency 'online sweepstakes' prohibitions in 2025-26 that reach payment processors, geolocation vendors and media affiliates as well as the operator (CA, CT, IN, LA, ME, MT, NJ, NY, OK, TN), with TWO MORE (IA, NV) granting regulators open-ended enforcement authority rather than defining the offence — a materially different compliance question. Licensure is not unavailable in principle: Nevada NRS 463.160 licenses any game played for money with no skill/chance distinction, and New Jersey's DGE licenses esports event wagering. The accurate statement is that NO STATE HAS AN OFF-THE-SHELF LICENCE CATEGORY THAT FITS A PEER-TO-PEER MOBILE TRIVIA APP AT VIABLE COST OR LEAD TIME. Model C (fixed, pre-announced, operator-funded prize pool, neutral operator, no randomisation on the paid path) is the only real-money design with a defensible state-law story, and it still needs geofencing of roughly eleven states plus a per-state opinion. Models A and D are lawful subject to three things the original brief under-weighted: the Washington/New York 'privilege of playing' trap, unclaimed-property duties on stored coin balances, and automatic-renewal law.

  • A CLEAR

    Closed-loop cosmetic coins are not a 'bet or wager' under 31 U.S.C. § 5362(1)(A): the user stakes nothing on the outcome of 'a contest of others, a sporting event, or a game subject to chance', and receives nothing of value on any outcome. No prize of value means no lottery (no prize/chance/consideration triad), so 18 U.S.C. §§ 1301-1304 are not engaged. The Wire Act reaches only 'bets or wagers' and (per the First Circuit) only sporting events — not engaged. 18 U.S.C. § 1955 requires an underlying state gambling-law violation and a 'gambling business' — not engaged. CORRECTED (audit defect 7): the § 5362(1)(E)(viii) exclusion does NOT support purchased coins. Clause (viii) covers participation where participants 'do not stake or risk anything of value other than — (I) personal efforts ... or (II) points or credits that the sponsor of the game or contest provides to participants FREE OF CHARGE'. Model A coins are bought for dollars, so (viii) is unavailable the moment coins are sold; it continues to cover only the existing free demo-coin game. Model A's real federal argument is the § 5362(1)(A) 'nothing of value staked, no prize of value' point, standing alone. On money transmission, the coin is NOT a convertible virtual currency: FinCEN FIN-2019-G001 § 1.3 defines CVC as virtual currency that 'either has an equivalent value as currency, or acts as a substitute for currency', and a coin that can never be redeemed for money or a prize of value is neither. Independently, the operator accepts funds only integral to the sale of its own (non-money-transmission) service and so falls in the exemption at 31 C.F.R. § 1010.100(ff)(5)(ii)(F). If instead characterised as prepaid access, a closed-loop arrangement limited to $2,000 of value per device per day is not a 'prepaid program' (FinCEN Prepaid Access Final Rule, 76 FR 45403–45420, Vol. 76 No. 146, 29 July 2011 [CORRECTED date, audit defect 8]; threshold at 31 C.F.R. § 1010.100(ff)(4)(iii)). No FinCEN registration. Residual federal exposure is consumer-protection only: FTC Act § 5 dark-pattern liability over confusing multi-step currency conversions and purchase flows (U.S. v. Epic Games, $245m order finalised March 2023; U.S. v. Cognosphere LLC, $20m, complaint filed 17 Jan 2025, which also barred loot-box sales to under-16s without parental consent), plus COPPA. CAUTION: FinCEN states it 'interprets these exemptions strictly' (FIN-2019-G001 at 9) — the moment a single coin can be cashed out, traded between users, or redeemed for a prize with real value, Model A collapses into Model B.

  • B CLOSED

    Consideration (real money for coins) plus prize (cash or prizes of value) is present in all 51 jurisdictions; the only live question is chance, and for Model B that question does not save the design. THREE INDEPENDENT KILL-SHOTS. (1) STATUTES THAT REACH SKILL WAGERING EXPRESSLY, so the trivia-is-skill argument is irrelevant: Arizona A.R.S. s.13-3301(4) (gambling includes risking value for benefit from 'a game or contest of chance OR SKILL'); California Penal Code s.337a(a)(6) (any bet 'upon the result ... of any trial ... or contest ... of skill, speed or power of endurance of person or animal, OR BETWEEN PERSONS'); Florida Stat. s.849.14 ('Unlawful to bet on result of trial or contest of skill'); Delaware 11 Del. C. s.1403 (registering 'bets or wagers upon the result of any trial or contest of skill, speed or power of endurance'); Illinois 720 ILCS 5/28-1(a)(1) (playing 'a game of chance OR SKILL for money'); Louisiana R.S. 14:90 (no chance element at all — 'any game, contest, lottery, or contrivance whereby a person risks the loss of anything of value in order to realize a profit'); Nevada NRS 463.0152 ('gambling game' is any game played 'for money ... or any representative of value', no skill/chance distinction); South Dakota SDCL s.22-25A-1 ('bet or wager' by reference to 'the result of a race, contest, or game', no chance element); South Carolina S.C. Code s.16-19-40 as construed in Town of Mt. Pleasant v. Chimento (S.C. 2012) (reaches games even where skill predominates); Arkansas Code s.5-66-104 (any device 'at which any money or property may be won or lost'). CORRECTED: MONTANA IS REMOVED from this list. The brief's claim that Mont. Code Ann. s.23-5-110 defines a lawful 'contest of skill' as one where 'the contestant is not required to pay anything of value ... to participate' is withdrawn. Verified at mca.legmt.gov on 14 Sep 2026: s.23-5-110 is titled 'Public policy of state concerning gambling' and is a legislative-findings and licensing-privilege section with no definition of 'contest of skill' and no paid-entry disqualifier; the definitions section s.23-5-112 defines 44 terms and 'contest of skill' is not among them, and its gambling definition at s.23-5-112(14)(a) is chance-based ('a gain that is contingent in whole or in part upon lot, chance, or the operation of a gambling device or gambling enterprise'), which cuts AGAINST the brief's original reading. Montana's real Model B exposure is S.B. 555, not a skill-wagering statute. (2) CHANCE TESTS THAT CATCH A RANDOMISED TRIVIA DRAW ANYWAY: Tennessee Code s.39-17-501(1) ('to any degree contingent on chance'); Washington, New York, Alabama, Kentucky, Missouri, Ohio and Oregon apply a material-element test ('depends in a material degree upon an element of chance, notwithstanding that skill of the contestants may also be a factor' — in New York that is Penal Law s.225.00(1), CORRECTED from the brief's s.225.00(2)). Randomised question selection, a 5-second timer against an unseen opponent, and matchmaking variance are all chance inputs a prosecutor will point at. (3) EXPRESS 2025-26 DUAL-CURRENCY BANS that criminalise the sweepstakes-casino structure and extend liability to payment processors, geolocation vendors, media affiliates and platform suppliers. CORRECTED COUNT: roughly TEN express bans, not twelve — California Penal Code s.337o (AB 831, signed 11 Oct 2025, Ch. 623, eff. 1 Jan 2026, misdemeanour, $1,000-$25,000 per violation; independently confirmed); New York Rac. Pari-Mut. Wag. & Breeding L. s.912 (S5935A, Ch. 605 of 2025, signed 5 Dec 2025, $10,000-$100,000 per violation; independently confirmed); Oklahoma S.B. 1589 (vetoed 7 May 2026, overridden 14 May 2026, Class C2 felony, eff. 1 Nov 2026; independently confirmed); Connecticut P.A. 25-112 / S.B. 1235 (eff. 1 Oct 2025, Class D felony); Montana S.B. 555 (eff. 1 Oct 2025); plus five whose enactment particulars are UNVERIFIED and must be confirmed before they go in a launch timeline — New Jersey A5447 (text confirmed to exist and to carry $100,000/$250,000 civil penalties, but enactment and the '15 Aug 2025' signing date NOT confirmed), Indiana H.B. 1052 / P.L. 153-2026, Maine L.D. 2007, Tennessee S.B. 2136, Louisiana H.B. 53 (Act 48) and H.B. 883 (Act 182). IOWA AND NEVADA ARE NOT EXPRESS DUAL-CURRENCY BANS and must not be counted as such: Iowa S.F. 2289 is a broad enforcement grant that does not define dual-currency systems or create a new offence, and Nevada S.B. 256 added enforcement tools against unlicensed online operators. In IA and NV the compliance question turns on REGULATOR DISCRETION, not on a statutory definition the founder can read against the product — which is worse to plan around, not better. Most of these bans require the game to 'simulate casino-style gambling', which a trivia duel arguably does not, but that gap is narrow, untested, and California s.337a(a)(6) and Florida s.849.14 close it independently. ON LICENSURE — CORRECTED: it is NOT true that no state licenses this. Nevada NRS 463.0152 defines a gambling game with no skill/chance distinction and NRS 463.160 licenses exactly that; New Jersey's DGE licenses esports event wagering. Those are licensed paths. The accurate claim is that no state has an off-the-shelf licence category that fits a peer-to-peer mobile trivia app at commercially viable cost and lead time — a Nevada non-restricted licence is a multi-year, multi-million-dollar suitability process. [AUDIT ADDITION] TRIBAL GAMING COMPACTS are a separate state-layer risk the brief never mentioned. Exclusivity provisions in California, Florida, Oklahoma and Connecticut give tribes independent standing and enforcement leverage against unlicensed real-money gaming, and tribal objection has killed products with a clean reading of the criminal code. Specific compact provisions UNVERIFIED. [AUDIT ADDITION] PLAYER-SIDE VERSUS OPERATOR-SIDE LIABILITY: several of these statutes reach the person who plays or bets, not only the house — 720 ILCS 5/28-1(a)(1) ('plays a game of chance or skill for money') and Cal. Penal Code s.337a (which reaches making and ACCEPTING a bet) among them. Geofencing protects the company from the operator-side offence; it does not retroactively protect a user who played from a prohibited state, and that drives terms-of-service drafting, refund posture, and whether you owe a user their balance back. Scope of player-side liability per state: UNVERIFIED. Verdict: unlawful.

  • C UNSETTLED

    The intended shelter is 31 U.S.C. § 5362(1)(E)(ix), and it DOES NOT WORK for trivia — this is the most commonly misread provision in the statute. Clause (ix) opens 'participation in any fantasy or simulation sports game or educational game or contest', and founders read 'educational game or contest' as a standalone carve-out. It is not: (ix) imposes three cumulative conditions, and condition (II) requires that 'All winning outcomes reflect the relative knowledge and skill of the participants and are determined predominantly by accumulated statistical results of the performance of individuals (athletes in the case of sports events) in multiple real-world sporting or other events.' A knowledge duel or expedition is not determined by accumulated statistical results of real-world events, so it fails (II) and falls outside (ix) — even though the fixed, pre-announced, entrant-count-independent prize pool satisfies condition (I) perfectly. The stronger federal argument is the threshold one: a paid-entry knowledge contest is arguably not a 'bet or wager' under § 5362(1)(A) at all, because the entrant stakes on their own performance, not on 'a contest of others', not on a sporting event, and — if outcomes are genuinely knowledge-determined — not on 'a game subject to chance'. There is no federal case law resolving this for online trivia. WIRE ACT: § 1084(a) reaches bets 'on any sporting event or contest'; the First Circuit read that phrase as a sports-limited unit (986 F.3d 38), but no other circuit has ruled and the unrescinded 2018 OLC opinion would read 'contest' broadly; the § 1084(b) safe harbour is a further threshold defence (AUDIT ADDITION). IGBA/Travel Act: derivative of state law, i.e. it turns on each state's predominance / material-element / any-chance test — outside this lane. MONEY TRANSMISSION: better than Model B but design-dependent. If the announced prize pool is the operator's own money and is NOT funded by entry fees, the operator is arguably selling a service and paying its own promotional expense, so 31 C.F.R. § 1010.100(ff)(5)(ii)(F) is available — the position DFS operators take. CORRECTED AUTHORITY (audit defect 5): if you hold a WITHDRAWABLE DOLLAR BALANCE for users, the governing provision is 31 C.F.R. § 1010.100(ff)(5)(i)(A) (acceptance of currency, funds or other value that substitutes for currency from one person and transmission to another location or person) — NOT FIN-2019-G001 § 4.7, which is a convertible-virtual-currency section and does not reach a dollar balance. Resting the trigger analysis on § 4.7 would misdirect the counsel opinion. Design lever: one-way payouts, no withdrawable stored value. FEDERAL EXCISE TAX — DIRECTION REVERSED BY AUDIT (defects 1 and 2). The original brief called this 'the under-appreciated killer', said § 4421 'draws no skill/chance distinction at all', said the IRS 'has already applied' the tax to paid-entry skill contests, and said the fixed-operator-funded-prize defence was untested with no authority in existence. All four statements are wrong. (a) Rev. Rul. 57-521, 1957-2 C.B. 779 — discussed at length inside AM 2020-009 itself — held that 'contesting for a prize offered by another in a contest of mental or physical skill of the contestant, which the one offering the prize must award in any event, is not gaming; and, the fact that each contestant is required to pay an entrance fee does not make the payment a bet or gaming transaction unless the entrance fees alone comprise the prize'. The ruling concerned a PUZZLE CONTEST with entry fees and guaranteed payments, where 'the operator has obligated itself to pay a sum certain in prizes which is not contingent on the number or amount of entrance fees', and concluded the puzzle game was not a wagering pool or lottery within IRC § 4421. That is directly on point for Model C and is the single most favourable authority in existence for it. (b) AM 2020-009 does say the statutory language 'does not differentiate whether an activity involves skill, chance, or some combination of the two', but the very next sentence reads 'Still, the presence or absence of skill in a contest may help determine whether an entry fee constitutes a taxable wager, or not', and the memo then distinguishes the puzzle contest from DFS: 'In the revenue ruling, the contest participant's own skill was the only factor involved in winning the puzzle game and there was no chance element at all. In contrast, DFS participants merely select a lineup ... and have no ability to exercise control or influence over the actions of the players.' The IRS applied the tax to DFS BECAUSE the entrant does not control the outcome. A trivia duel, where the entrant's own knowledge determines the result, sits on the Rev. Rul. 57-521 side of the line the memo itself draws. (c) The correct residual risk is therefore: the operator-funded, fixed, pre-announced, entrant-count-independent prize is supported by a published revenue ruling that the IRS's own most recent advice endorses; the exposure is that FACT//DUEL's chance elements (question draw, matchmaking, reaction-time scoring) could be argued to pull it toward DFS. AM 2020-009 remains non-precedential (26 U.S.C. § 6110(k)(3)); Rev. Rul. 57-521 is a published revenue ruling and is citable. ADVERTISING / PRIVATE LITIGATION — CORRECTED (audit defect 6): Skillz Platform Inc. v. Papaya Gaming, Ltd., No. 1:24-cv-01646 (S.D.N.Y., Cote, J.) was brought by a COMPETITOR under the Lanham Act and New York General Business Law. FTC Act § 5 has no private right of action and the FTC was not a party. An April 2026 jury returned a $420m verdict; judgment entered 28 July 2026 separately awarded $719 million in disgorgement as an ALTERNATIVE REMEDY THE PLAINTIFF MAY ELECT IN LIEU OF DAMAGES, plus $10.1m in fees — these are alternative, not cumulative. It is a district-court judgment subject to appeal to the Second Circuit, it is not final, and it is NOT precedent and binds no court. The fact pattern — marketing real-money head-to-head 'skill' tournaments while filling them with undisclosed bots — is nonetheless exactly FACT//DUEL's risk if a paying player is ever matched against an AI without unmissable disclosure. Net: genuinely unsettled federally, materially safer than Model B, and the safest of the money-taking models IF the prize is operator-funded, fixed and announced, there is no withdrawable balance, and every opponent is a disclosed human or a disclosed bot.

  • D CLEAR

    A recurring fee for features or cosmetics with no stake and no prize of value engages no federal gambling statute: no 'bet or wager' under 31 U.S.C. § 5362(1)(A), no lottery under 18 U.S.C. §§ 1301-1304, no 'sporting event or contest' wager under 18 U.S.C. § 1084, and nothing for 18 U.S.C. § 1955 to attach to. Not money transmission — the operator accepts funds integral to the sale of its own services (31 C.F.R. § 1010.100(ff)(5)(ii)(F)). The live federal law is consumer protection. ROSCA (Restore Online Shoppers' Confidence Act), 15 U.S.C. §§ 8401-8405, Pub. L. 111-345, requires, for any negative-option feature sold online: clear and conspicuous disclosure of all material terms before obtaining billing information, express informed consent, and simple mechanisms to stop recurring charges (§ 8403). CORRECTED (audit defect 4): the FTC's 2024 AMENDED Negative Option Rule — the 'click-to-cancel' regime — was vacated in its entirety by the Eighth Circuit in Custom Communications, Inc. v. FTC, No. 24-3137 (8th Cir. 8 July 2025), on Magnuson-Moss procedural grounds (failure to conduct a preliminary regulatory analysis after finding annual costs above $100m), days before the 14 July 2025 compliance date. IT IS WRONG TO SAY THERE IS 'NO FTC NEGATIVE OPTION RULE'. The vacatur of the 2024 amendments restored the pre-existing 1973 Prenotification Negative Option Plan rule, still codified at 16 C.F.R. Part 425. The correct statement is that there is currently no click-to-cancel rule, while the narrower 1973 prenotification rule remains on the books — check whether your offer is a prenotification plan within Part 425 before assuming nothing applies. The FTC restarted the rulemaking: a draft ANPRM went to OIRA on 30 January 2026 and the ANPRM WAS PUBLISHED 11 MARCH 2026 with comments due 13 April 2026; per the law-firm account the brief itself relies on, it asks whether to amend the EXISTING Negative Option Rule and separately about certain provisions of the vacated Click-to-Cancel Rule — two distinct instruments. AUDIT ADDITION (missing item 6): STATE AUTOMATIC-RENEWAL LAWS are binding law, not prudence. California's Automatic Renewal Law and New York's and other states' ARLs impose click-to-cancel-equivalent obligations independently of any FTC rule; building to click-to-cancel standards is therefore a compliance requirement in those states, not merely a hedge against the rule returning. Specific state section numbers, effective dates and the current California ARL amendments are UNVERIFIED in this lane and must be confirmed by the state lane or counsel. Also applies: Epic Games/Cognosphere-style dark-pattern liability on the purchase flow, and COPPA if any subscriber could be under 13.

States and the District of Columbia 55

UNSETTLED 28   UNANALYSED IN THIS LANE 1   CLOSED 21   NOTE 1   FAVOURABLE CIRCUIT AUTHORITY 1   PROHIBITED FOR MODEL B (S.B. 555); UNRESOLVED FOR MODEL C — CORRECTED 1   LICENCE 1   WATCH 1

  • AlabamaUNSETTLED

    Material-element test: a 'contest of chance' is one whose outcome 'depends in a material degree upon an element of chance, notwithstanding that skill of the contestants may also be a factor'. A skill-dominant trivia contest with no randomisation is defensible (Model C); Model B is unsettled and exposed to Alabama's gambling-loss-recovery statute, which was used in the 2026 prediction-market class actions. No sweepstakes ban enacted. Constitutional ban on lotteries (Ala. Const. art. IV s.65) is a background risk for anything pot-like. [AUDIT ADDITION] AGE OF MAJORITY IN ALABAMA IS 19, not 18. A flat 18+ gate leaves 18-year-old Alabama users able to disaffirm the contract, which undermines the terms of service, the arbitration clause and the class-action waiver against them, and sharpens the minors' in-app-purchase exposure. Statutory cite UNVERIFIED.

    Ala. Code s.13A-12-20(4) ('contest of chance'), s.13A-12-21 (simple gambling) — text via FindLaw secondary. Alabama loss-recovery statute section UNVERIFIED.

  • AlaskaUNSETTLED

    Gambling is defined by reference to a contest of chance or a future contingent event not under the participant's control; no enacted sweepstakes ban and no express skill-wagering prohibition found. Model C plausibly permitted, Model B unsettled. Alaska has no commercial gaming regime, so there is no licence to obtain.

    AS 11.66.280 (gambling definitions) — section text UNVERIFIED in this pass.

  • All other states and territoriesUNANALYSED IN THIS LANE

    Not analysed. Every remaining state applies its own predominance / material-element / any-chance test to decide whether a paid trivia duel is gambling, its own money transmitter licensing regime, its own minimum contest age, and (for Model D) its own automatic-renewal law. Federal clearance tells you almost nothing about any of them. Route to the state lane.

    31 U.S.C. § 5361(b) (express no-preemption rule of construction)

  • ArizonaCLOSED

    THE CLEAREST PROHIBITION IN THE COUNTRY. 'Gambling' expressly includes 'one act of risking or giving something of value for the opportunity to obtain a benefit from a game or contest of chance OR SKILL'. The skill/chance debate is legislated away. The only exceptions are 'amusement gambling' — merchandise prizes under $10 wholesale, coupons capped at $550 aggregate, or an intellectual contest where the payment is part of an established purchase price with no increment added. A cash-prize trivia duel or paid-entry contest fits none of them. A.R.S. s.13-3311 provides an AG registration route for amusement-gambling intellectual contests but only inside those prize caps. Geofence. Models A and D unaffected.

    A.R.S. s.13-3301(4) (definition of 'gambling'), s.13-3301 (amusement gambling), s.13-3311 (registration, filing of rules, sworn statement, exceptions) — current Arizona Revised Statutes, azleg.gov.

  • ArkansasCLOSED

    Broad gaming-device prohibition reaching any device 'at which any money or property may be won or lost', with no chance element required. Narrow safe harbour: paid ESPORTS tournaments are expressly not gambling if entry fees are paid in cash, prizes are disclosed in advance, and outcomes reflect the skill and performance of competitors (eff. 31 Jul 2023). A mobile trivia app is very unlikely to be an 'esports tournament' as defined. Geofence unless counsel can bring the product inside ch. 23-118. Blocked by Skillz.

    Ark. Code s.5-66-104 (gaming devices — prohibition); Ark. Code ss.23-118-101 to 23-118-103 (Paid Esports Tournaments, HB 1349 of 2023, eff. 31 Jul 2023).

  • AUDIT ADDITION — scope note on these rowsNOTE

    The original brief had ZERO subdivision rows and carried the state dual-currency/sweepstakes ban list only inside the Model B narrative, where audit defects 9 and 10 found date and bill-number errors. The rows below exist so the geo-block can be sequenced from a list rather than from prose. This lane is federal; these states matter here ONLY because 31 U.S.C. § 5362(10)(A) and 18 U.S.C. § 1955 take their unlawfulness from state law. Every row is secondary-sourced unless marked otherwise.

    31 U.S.C. § 5361(b); § 5362(10)(A); 18 U.S.C. § 1955(b)(1)(i)

  • CaliforniaCLOSED

    TWO SEPARATE PROHIBITIONS. (1) Penal Code s.337a(a)(6) criminalises laying, making, offering or accepting any bet 'upon the result, or purported result, of any trial ... or contest ... of skill, speed or power of endurance of person or animal, OR BETWEEN PERSONS' — a player-v-player trivia stake is squarely within it; subdivisions (2), (3) and (4) reach operating a place for such wagers, RECEIVING OR HOLDING the money staked, and recording the wagers. First offence up to one year and/or $5,000. (2) Penal Code s.337o (added by AB 831, signed 11 Oct 2025, eff. 1 Jan 2026) bans operating an 'online sweepstakes game' — a dual-currency internet game that simulates gambling and awards cash or cash equivalents — and extends liability to financial institutions, payment processors, geolocation providers, content suppliers and media affiliates who knowingly and willfully support it; misdemeanour, $1,000-$25,000 per violation, up to one year. A trivia duel arguably does not 'simulate gambling' for s.337o purposes, but s.337a(a)(6) closes that gap. California applies the predominance test generally (In re Allen, 59 Cal.2d 5 (1962)), so Model C with an operator-funded fixed prize and no player-v-player pot is the only structure with a case — get a California opinion. [AUDIT ADDITION] TRIBAL COMPACT EXPOSURE: state-tribal compact exclusivity gives tribes independent standing and enforcement leverage against unlicensed real-money gaming here, separate from the criminal code. Clearing the penal analysis does not clear a compact objection. Compact provisions UNVERIFIED. California's tribal exclusivity is among the most consequential in the country. [AUDIT ADDITION] PLAYER-SIDE LIABILITY: Cal. Penal Code s.337a reaches MAKING and ACCEPTING a bet, not only operating a place or holding stakes, so the individual player is within the statute too. Scope UNVERIFIED; put it to California counsel.

    Cal. Penal Code s.337a(a)(2),(3),(4),(6); Cal. Penal Code s.337o (AB 831, eff. 1 Jan 2026); Cal. Bus. & Prof. Code s.17539.1 (as amended by AB 831). Regulator: California Attorney General / Department of Justice Bureau of Gambling Control.

  • ColoradoUNSETTLED

    Colorado's gambling definition is generally understood to exclude 'bona fide contests of skill, speed, strength or endurance in which awards are made only to entrants or the owners of entries', which would support Model C and possibly Model B. But promotions-law compliance guides list Colorado as a state that does not permit entry fees in contests. The two framings conflict and I could not resolve them from a primary source in this pass. No sweepstakes ban enacted. Treat as unresolved and get a Colorado opinion before taking paid entries.

    C.R.S. s.18-10-102 (gambling definitions) — skill-contest exclusion text UNVERIFIED. Conflicting secondary source: Sweeppea state compliance guide.

  • ConnecticutCLOSED

    Cuts both ways, so treat it as prohibited. The gambling definition DOES exclude 'legal contests of skill, speed, strength or endurance in which awards are made only to entrants or the owners of entries' (verbatim), which on its face supports Model C and even Model B. But S.B. 1235 / Public Act 25-112 (signed 11 Jun 2025, eff. 1 Oct 2025) makes operating an online sweepstakes casino a CLASS D FELONY, and the entire real-money skill industry (including Skillz) blocks Connecticut, which tells you how the Department of Consumer Protection reads the 'legal contests' circularity. Geofence unless you are willing to fund a test case. [AUDIT ADDITION] TRIBAL COMPACT EXPOSURE: state-tribal compact exclusivity gives tribes independent standing and enforcement leverage against unlicensed real-money gaming here, separate from the criminal code. Clearing the penal analysis does not clear a compact objection. Compact provisions UNVERIFIED.

    Conn. Gen. Stat. s.53-278a(2) (definition of 'gambling', skill-contest exclusion, 2024 revision in force); Conn. Public Act 25-112 (S.B. 1235), eff. 1 Oct 2025. Regulator: Department of Consumer Protection, Gaming Division.

  • DelawareCLOSED

    Constitutional ban: all forms of gambling are prohibited except state-controlled lotteries, s.17B lotteries, pari-mutuel horse wagering and bingo. Separately, 'advancing gambling in the first degree' covers keeping or using any book, device or apparatus for receiving, recording or registering 'bets or wagers upon the result of any trial or contest of SKILL, speed or power of endurance of human or beast' — the 19th-century formula that catches player-v-player skill wagering explicitly. Class A misdemeanour. Blocked by Skillz. Geofence.

    Del. Const. art. II s.17 (Lotteries and Other Gambling); 11 Del. C. s.1403 (advancing gambling in the first degree); 11 Del. C. s.1401 (second degree); 11 Del. C. s.1432 (gambling definitions).

  • District of ColumbiaUNSETTLED

    No express skill-wagering prohibition identified and no enacted sweepstakes ban, but B26-0656 (Internet Gaming and Consumer Protection Act of 2026, introduced 9 Apr 2026, under Council review as of mid-2026) would legalise regulated online gaming while banning sweepstakes casinos. DC's sporting-event definition treats esports as games of skill, which is mildly helpful for Model C. Operator-level restrictions and scrutiny already reported. Monitor closely — DC could flip within one Council period.

    D.C. Code s.22-1701 et seq. (gambling) — section text UNVERIFIED; D.C. Code s.36-601.01 (sporting events, esports as skill) via secondary source; DC Council B26-0656 (pending).

  • First Circuit (Maine, Massachusetts, New Hampshire, Rhode Island, Puerto Rico)FAVOURABLE CIRCUIT AUTHORITY

    Only jurisdictions bound by N.H. Lottery Comm'n v. Rosen, 986 F.3d 38 (1st Cir. 20 Jan. 2021), which limits 18 U.S.C. § 1084 to sporting events. Elsewhere there is no controlling authority and the unrescinded 2018 OLC opinion would read 'contest' broadly. This is a federal, not state, subdivision — included because the Wire Act answer literally changes with geography.

    N.H. Lottery Comm'n v. Rosen, 986 F.3d 38 (1st Cir. 2021)

  • FloridaCLOSED

    Fla. Stat. s.849.14 is titled 'Unlawful to bet on result of trial or contest of skill, speed or power or endurance' and makes it a second-degree misdemeanour (up to 60 days, $500) to stake, bet or wager any money or thing of value on such a result. The legislature had to enact a SPECIFIC carve-out for bowling tournaments with entry fees and a purse (s.849.141), which demonstrates that entry-fee-funded prizes would otherwise be caught. Separately, s.849.094 requires filing rules and prize lists with FDACS at least 7 days out and a trust account or bond where total prize value exceeds $5,000. Florida also has no explicit agent-of-payee money-transmitter exemption. 2026 sweepstakes bills SB 1580 and HB 1467 both died. Geofence for paid play. [AUDIT ADDITION] TRIBAL COMPACT EXPOSURE: state-tribal compact exclusivity gives tribes independent standing and enforcement leverage against unlicensed real-money gaming here, separate from the criminal code. Clearing the penal analysis does not clear a compact objection. Compact provisions UNVERIFIED. Florida's compact with the Seminole Tribe is broad and actively enforced.

    Fla. Stat. s.849.14 (in force, 2025 Florida Statutes); Fla. Stat. s.849.141 (bowling exception); Fla. Stat. s.849.094 (game promotions). Regulators: Florida Attorney General; Florida Department of Agriculture and Consumer Services; Florida Gaming Control Commission.

  • GeorgiaUNSETTLED

    No enacted sweepstakes ban and no verified skill-wagering statute, but Georgia's gambling-loss-recovery statute is live and was used in the 2026 wave of class actions against prediction-market operators and their brokers. Georgia also has an aggressive history on 'coin-operated amusement machines' with no-cash-prize rules. Model C plausibly defensible under a predominance analysis; Model B carries real private-litigation risk independent of any prosecution.

    O.C.G.A. s.16-12-21 (gambling) and s.16-12-35 (bona fide skill contest / amusement machine rules) — section text UNVERIFIED; O.C.G.A. s.13-8-3 (gambling contracts void; recovery) cited in 2026 class actions (secondary: Illinois Answers Project, Mar 2026).

  • HawaiiUNSETTLED

    Hawaii has NO legal gambling of any kind — no lottery, no casinos, no sports betting — and its gambling offence tracks the model-penal-code 'contest of chance or a future contingent event not under his control' formula. SB 3281 (2026), which would have criminalised promoting illegal electronic gambling, was deferred. [CONTESTED — audit flagged at "possible" confidence] The brief reported, from promotions-law compliance guides only, that contests requiring an entry fee with prizes of $5,000 or more must be registered and bonded in Hawaii. THE AUDIT RECOMMENDS STRIKING THAT LINE: no Hawaii prize-promotion registration statute is identifiable, and it sits badly against the fact — which the brief itself states correctly — that Hawaii has no legal gambling and NO GAMING REGULATOR, so there is no agency to register with. It is retained here only as a contested claim, flagged. THE OPERATIONAL POINT IS UNAMBIGUOUS EITHER WAY: GEOFENCE HAWAII for any paid stake. Do not treat paid entry in Hawaii as curable by filing a registration; the AG has no tolerance and there is no licensing route.

    HRS s.712-1220 (gambling definitions) and s.712-1223 (gambling) — section text UNVERIFIED; [CONTESTED — audit flagged at "possible" confidence] Hawaii contest registration/bonding threshold from secondary promotions-law sources only, no primary statute located — audit recommends striking it.

  • IdahoCLOSED

    The Idaho Constitution bans gambling broadly and expressly prohibits 'any electronic or electromechanical imitation or simulation of any form of casino gambling'. Sweepstakes-industry trackers list Idaho alongside Washington as a state where pre-existing constitutional and statutory law already prohibits the dual-currency model without new legislation. Idaho's statutory gambling definition follows the contest-of-chance formula. Geofence Model B; Model C needs an Idaho opinion.

    Idaho Const. art. III s.20 (gambling prohibited, including simulations of casino gambling); Idaho Code s.18-3801 (gambling defined) — subsection detail UNVERIFIED.

  • IllinoisCLOSED

    Illinois criminalises playing 'a game of chance OR SKILL for money or other thing of value' — the statute names skill expressly. There is a participant-side exception for a bona fide contest for the determination of skill, speed, strength or endurance where awards are made only to entrants, but it does not shelter the OPERATOR, who remains exposed under the keeping-a-gambling-place and operating provisions. Illinois also has the most usable loss-recovery statute in the country: anyone who loses $50 or more by gambling may sue the winner in the circuit court. The Illinois Gaming Board issued cease-and-desist letters to 60+ sweepstakes operators on 3 Mar 2026. Geofence Model B; Model C needs an Illinois opinion focused on operator liability. [AUDIT ADDITION] PLAYER-SIDE LIABILITY: 720 ILCS 5/28-1(a)(1) reaches the person who PLAYS, and the bona fide contest exception at 5/28-1(b)(2) is participant-side. So in Illinois the geofence protects the company from the operator-side offence while an Illinois user who plays is separately exposed — which drives the terms of service and the return-of-balance posture, not just the block list.

    720 ILCS 5/28-1(a)(1) ('plays a game of chance or skill for money'); 720 ILCS 5/28-1(b)(2) (bona fide contest exception, participant-side); 720 ILCS 5/28-3 (keeping a gambling place); 720 ILCS 5/28-8 (loss recovery, $50 threshold). Regulator: Illinois Gaming Board; Illinois Attorney General.

  • IndianaCLOSED

    [CONTESTED — audit flagged at "possible" confidence] DATES UNVERIFIED FROM A PRIMARY SOURCE. H.B. 1052, reported signed 12 Mar 2026 as Public Law 153-2026, prohibits online sweepstakes games built on dual- or multi-currency systems that simulate lottery games or casino-style gaming, reported effective 1 Jul 2026, with civil fines up to $100,000 per violation. The Indiana General Assembly source could not be reached to confirm the signing date, effective date or penalty figure — confirm from the enrolled act before these enter a launch timeline.   • IowaCLOSED

    CORRECTED CHARACTERISATION: Iowa is NOT one of the express dual-currency sweepstakes bans and must not be counted as one. S.F. 2289 (reported signed by Gov. Reynolds 15 May 2026, eff. 1 Jul 2026 — [CONTESTED — audit flagged at "possible" confidence] both dates UNVERIFIED from a primary legislative source) expands the Iowa Racing and Gaming Commission's enforcement authority and expressly authorises cease-and-desist orders against operators offering games of chance, sports wagering, gambling OR SWEEPSTAKES GAMING in Iowa without a licence. As the brief itself noted, it does NOT define dual-currency systems and does NOT create a new criminal offence — it is a broad enforcement grant. That makes it MORE dangerous for a novel product than a narrow casino-simulation ban, because the Commission decides what counts and there is no definitional element to argue against; but it is a different compliance question from CA, NY or OK, and the brief's headline count of twelve bans conflated the two. Iowa Code ch. 99B restricts games between individuals to bona fide social relationships. Real-money skill apps block Iowa. Geofence.

    Iowa S.F. 2289 — ENFORCEMENT-AUTHORITY GRANT, amending Iowa Code ch. 99F; signing and effective dates UNVERIFIED; Iowa Code s.99B.12 (games between individuals). Regulator: Iowa Racing and Gaming Commission.

  • KansasUNSETTLED

  • KentuckyUNSETTLED

    HIGHEST PRIVATE-LITIGATION RISK IN THE UNITED STATES even though there is no sweepstakes ban. CORRECTED — THE CITE IS TWO SECTIONS, NOT ONE. KRS 372.020 is the LOSER'S OWN action: a person who loses $5 or more in a 24-hour period through illegal gambling may sue to recover the amount lost. KRS 372.040 is the SEPARATE provision that carries the six-month window, the 'ANY OTHER PERSON' standing and TREBLE damages — if the loser does not sue within six months, any other person may sue for treble the value of the loss. The brief packed both into 'KRS 372.020'. The split matters because s.372.040 is the provision that actually creates the outsized exposure: it converts a private remedy into a bounty available to strangers and litigation funders. Kentucky used this machinery to obtain a nine-figure judgment against PokerStars and the AG has active litigation against VGW. Kentucky's gambling definition uses the material-degree test, with a reported exception where results are determined solely by skill and prizes are predetermined — which, if correct, is a precise fit for Model C and a precise mismatch for Model B. Kentucky was also a venue in the 2026 prediction-market class actions. Model C: arguable. Model B: do not.

    KRS s.372.020 (loser's action to recover the amount lost, $5 threshold) and KRS s.372.040 (six-month window, 'any other person' standing, TREBLE damages) — CORRECTED, the brief attributed both mechanisms to s.372.020; KRS s.528.010(3) (gambling, material degree) and s.528.010(5) (skill exception, predetermined prizes) — s.528.010(5) via secondary source (Legal Moves Law Firm esports guide), text UNVERIFIED. Regulator: Kentucky Attorney General.

  • LouisianaCLOSED

    Louisiana's gambling statute has NO CHANCE ELEMENT AT ALL: gambling is 'the intentional conducting, or directly assisting in the conducting, as a business, of any game, contest, lottery, or contrivance whereby a person risks the loss of anything of value in order to realize a profit'. That definition catches a paid-entry skill contest run as a business on its face. On top of that, H.B. 53 (Act 48) and H.B. 883 (Act 182), signed 25 May 2026 and effective 1 Aug 2026, ban dual-currency online sweepstakes, treat them as 'gambling by computer', raise the maximum fine for illegal online gambling operations from $20,000 to $100,000 with up to five years, and bring racketeering exposure. Absolute geofence.

    La. R.S. 14:90 (Gambling, in force); La. Acts 2026, No. 48 (H.B. 53) and No. 182 (H.B. 883), eff. 1 Aug 2026. Regulator: Louisiana Gaming Control Board; Louisiana State Police Gaming Enforcement Division; Louisiana Attorney General.

  • MarylandUNSETTLED

A trivia app is not obviously within the listed genres. Maryland's general gambling provisions reach keeping or having an interest in a gaming device or place used for gambling (6 months to 1 year, $500). Promotions-law guides report that Maryland does not permit consideration in skill contests. Three 2026 sweepstakes bills (HB 1226, HB 295, SB 652) were introduced; none enacted. Unresolved — get a Maryland opinion, and consider whether the product can be characterised as an esport under s.12-114.

  • MontanaPROHIBITED FOR MODEL B (S.B. 555); UNRESOLVED FOR MODEL C — CORRECTED

    CORRECTED — THE BRIEF'S PRIMARY MONTANA CLAIM IS WITHDRAWN. The brief said Mont. Code Ann. s.23-5-110 defines a lawful 'contest of skill' as one in which skill predominates AND 'the contestant is not required to pay anything of value or purchase anything in order to participate', and used that to make Montana an absolute geofence with 'TWO INDEPENDENT PROHIBITIONS'. Verified directly at mca.legmt.gov on 14 Sep 2026: (a) s.23-5-110 is titled 'Public policy of state concerning gambling' — it is a legislative-findings and licensing-privilege section, it defines no terms and contains no paid-entry disqualifier; (b) the definitions section is s.23-5-112, which defines 44 terms and 'contest of skill' IS NOT AMONG THEM (the list runs Antique gambling device, Applicant, Application, Associated gambling business, Authorized equipment, Bingo ... through Video gambling machine); (c) s.23-5-112(14)(a) defines gambling as 'risking any money, credit, deposit, check, property, or other thing of value for a gain that is contingent in whole or in part upon lot, chance, or the operation of a gambling device or gambling enterprise' — a CHANCE-BASED definition that cuts AGAINST the brief's reading, not for it. A 'contest of skill' definition with a paid-entry bar is repeated in several promotions-law guides for Montana but NO PRIMARY CITATION COULD BE LOCATED: treat it as UNVERIFIED and do not rely on it. CONSEQUENCE: Montana comes OUT of the list of states that criminalise wagering on contests of skill by statute. WHAT STILL STANDS: S.B. 555, effective 1 Oct 2025, amended Title 23 ch. 5 to add a definition of 'online sweepstakes gambling' capturing the dual-currency model — reportedly the first dedicated dual-currency ban in the country — with felony penalties. MODEL B: geofence Montana on S.B. 555. MODEL C: now UNRESOLVED rather than absolutely barred — a paid-entry, operator-funded, fixed-prize knowledge contest with randomisation stripped from the paid path has a real argument under the chance-based s.23-5-112(14)(a), and a Montana opinion could remove Montana from the Model C block list. Keep it geofenced until that opinion exists. Footnote: Montana is the only state with no money transmitter licence requirement.

    Mont. Code Ann. s.23-5-110 ('Public policy of state concerning gambling' — verified 14 Sep 2026 at mca.legmt.gov; the brief's 'contest of skill' cite to this section is WITHDRAWN); Mont. Code Ann. s.23-5-112 (Definitions — 44 defined terms, no 'contest of skill'; gambling defined at (14)(a), chance-based, verified verbatim 14 Sep 2026); Mont. S.B. 555 (2025), eff. 1 Oct 2025, amending Mont. Code Ann. tit. 23 ch. 5. Regulator: Montana Department of Justice, Gambling Control Division.

  • NevadaLICENCE

    PRACTICALLY PROHIBITED, BUT A LICENSED PATH DOES EXIST IN PRINCIPLE. 'Game' or 'gambling game' means any game played with cards, dice, equipment or any mechanical or electronic device or machine FOR MONEY, property, checks, credit or any representative of value — the statute draws NO distinction between games of skill and games of chance. Any game that lets a player walk away with real value is a gambling game requiring a full Nevada gaming licence under NRS 463.160. CORRECTED: the brief elsewhere asserted that 'no state licenses this' and that 'lawful-only-with-licence is not available as an answer'. That is contradicted by this very entry. NRS 463.160 IS a licensed path — it is simply prohibitive: a Nevada non-restricted licence is a multi-year, multi-million-dollar suitability process with no product-appropriate category for a peer-to-peer mobile trivia app. The accurate statement is 'no off-the-shelf licence category at viable cost and lead time', not 'no licence exists'. The Gaming Control Board does not recognise the dual-currency sweepstakes model as exempt. S.B. 256 (2025) added ENFORCEMENT TOOLS against unlicensed online operators including freezing earnings — CORRECTED: it is NOT an express dual-currency ban and must not be counted as one; the Nevada question turns on Board discretion, not on a statutory dual-currency definition you can read against the product. Geofence.

    NRS s.463.0152 ('Game' and 'gambling game' defined); NRS s.463.160 (licence required — a real, if commercially unavailable, licensed path); Nev. S.B. 256 (2025) — enforcement-authority grant, not an express dual-currency ban. Regulator: Nevada Gaming Control Board; Nevada Gaming Commission.

  • New YorkCLOSED

    THE MOST IMPORTANT STATE TO GET RIGHT, AND THE MOST INSTRUCTIVE. (1) MODEL B IS BANNED OUTRIGHT: Racing, Pari-Mutuel Wagering and Breeding Law s.912 (added by S5935A, ch. 605 of 2025, signed by Gov. Hochul 5 Dec 2025, EFFECTIVE IMMEDIATELY WITH NO WIND-DOWN) prohibits dual-currency online sweepstakes platforms that let players exchange game currency for cash prizes or cash equivalents and that mimic casino gaming, sports betting, lottery products or bingo — and reaches technology and game suppliers, platform providers, PAYMENT PROCESSORS AND FINANCIAL INSTITUTIONS, geolocation firms and MEDIA AFFILIATES. $10,000-$100,000 per violation plus licence revocation or ineligibility. AG Letitia James had already sent cease-and-desist letters to 26 platforms in March 2025. (2) MODEL A HAS A TRAP — CITATION CORRECTED: N.Y. Penal Law s.225.00(6), 'Something of value', includes 'any form of credit or promise' involving 'extension of a service, entertainment or a PRIVILEGE OF PLAYING AT A GAME OR SCHEME WITHOUT CHARGE' — the same wording the Ninth Circuit relied on in Kater under Washington law. The brief cited this to s.225.00(1), which is wrong. Verified subdivision-by-subdivision at nysenate.gov on 14 Sep 2026: (1) 'Contest of chance', (2) 'Gambling', (3) 'Player', (4) 'Advance gambling activity', (5) 'Profit from gambling activity', (6) 'Something of value', (7) 'Gambling device', (8) 'Slot machine', (9) 'Bookmaking', (10) 'Lottery', (11) 'Policy or the numbers game', (12) 'Unlawful'. THE SUBSTANTIVE ANALYSIS IS UNCHANGED: a purchased coin that is staked and lost is a thing of value in New York even with no cash-out. (3) MODEL C — MATERIALLY QUALIFIED. The brief called White v. Cuomo, 38 N.Y.3d 209 (2022) 'THE BEST AUTHORITY IN THE COUNTRY' and said 'Build to that sentence.' That is overstated and the overstatement is operationally dangerous. White v. Cuomo was a holding on Article I s.9 of the NEW YORK CONSTITUTION, concerning interactive fantasy sports contests that the legislature had AFFIRMATIVELY AUTHORIZED in Racing Law article 14 and expressly exempted from Penal Law article 225; the court held that ample support existed for the legislature's determination that those authorized contests were lawful skill-based competitions. It does NOT hold that an UNAUTHORIZED paid-entry trivia contest escapes the material-degree contest-of-chance test at Penal Law s.225.00(1), and it has no precedential force in any other state. The reported 4-3 split is UNVERIFIED — a secondary source describes a DiFiore majority and a Wilson dissent joined by two judges, consistent with 4-3 but not confirmed from the opinion; do not state the split. WHAT SURVIVES AND IS WORTH KEEPING: the three attributes the Court treated as characteristic of lawful skill competition — substantial player influence, predetermined fixed prizes, a neutral operator — are a sound DESIGN TARGET that Model C meets and Model B does not. Treat them as a design heuristic with New York constitutional support, not as clearance, and get a New York penal-code opinion separately. (4) Note the material-degree test at Penal Law s.225.00(1) (CORRECTED from the brief's s.225.00(2), which is 'Gambling') and the GBL art. 24-A s.369-e registration and bond for prize pools over $5,000 (30 days' notice, $100 fee, bond equal to total prize value — figures from law-firm guides, UNVERIFIED).

    N.Y. Rac. Pari-Mut. Wag. & Breeding L. s.912 (ch. 605 of 2025, eff. 5 Dec 2025, independently confirmed); N.Y. Penal Law s.225.00(1) ('Contest of chance', material degree) and s.225.00(6) ('Something of value') — CORRECTED numbering, verified at nysenate.gov 14 Sep 2026; N.Y. Penal Law s.225.05 (promoting gambling 2d); White v. Cuomo, 38 N.Y.3d 209 (N.Y. 2022) — NY Const. art. I s.9 only, IFS authorized by Racing Law art. 14 and exempted from Penal Law art. 225, vote split UNVERIFIED; N.Y. Gen. Bus. Law art. 24-A s.369-e. Regulators: New York State Gaming Commission; New York Attorney General; New York Department of State.

  • Second Circuit (Connecticut, New York, Vermont) — Papaya appellate forumWATCH

The 28 July 2026 judgment is NOT FINAL and binds no court. Whether the disgorgement theory and the Lanham Act framing survive appeal is the single most decision-relevant open item for anyone sizing bot-disclosure risk.

    Skillz Platform Inc. v. Papaya Gaming, Ltd., No. 1:24-cv-01646 (S.D.N.Y.)

  • South CarolinaCLOSED

    South Carolina's unlawful-games-and-betting statute has been construed by the South Carolina Supreme Court in Town of Mt. Pleasant v. Chimento (2012) to reach games EVEN WHERE SKILL PREDOMINATES — the statutory meaning of 'gambling' in South Carolina includes games in which skill outweighs chance. H.4129 would amend the law to provide that paying a fee to play a skill-predominant game for a performance-proportionate prize is not gambling, but it has not passed. South Carolina also has a loss-recovery statute and was a venue in the 2026 prediction-market class actions. Blocked by Skillz. Absolute geofence. Footnote: South Carolina does not require a state money transmitter licence for most money-transmission activity.

    S.C. Code s.16-19-40 (unlawful games and betting); Town of Mt. Pleasant v. Chimento, 401 S.C. 522 (S.C. 2012) (citation partly UNVERIFIED); S.C. Code s.32-1-10 (loss recovery) — section UNVERIFIED; S.C. H.4129 (pending). Regulator: South Carolina Attorney General; SLED.

  • South DakotaCLOSED

    South Dakota's internet-gambling chapter defines 'bet or wager' as taking, receiving or accepting money or any valuable thing on the understanding that it will be paid to a person if payment is contingent upon 'the result of a RACE, CONTEST, OR GAME or upon the happening of an event not known to be certain' — NO CHANCE ELEMENT IS REQUIRED, so a skill contest is squarely inside it. Establishing a location or site in the state from which to conduct a gambling business over the internet is a Class 6 FELONY (up to 2 years, $4,000), rising to Class 5 for repeat offences, and EACH INDIVIDUAL BET IS A SEPARATE VIOLATION. Blocked by Skillz. Absolute geofence. South Dakota adopted the MTMA.

    SDCL s.22-25A-1 ('bet or wager' defined); SDCL s.22-25A-8 (establishment of internet gambling business prohibited); SDCL s.22-25A-9 (each bet a separate violation). Regulator: South Dakota Commission on Gaming; South Dakota Attorney General.

  • TennesseeCLOSED

    TWO INDEPENDENT PROHIBITIONS. (1) Tennessee defines gambling as 'risking anything of value for a profit whose return is TO ANY DEGREE CONTINGENT ON CHANCE' — effectively an any-chance test, with NO express skill exception in the definitions section. Randomised question selection alone is enough to engage it. This is the verified limb and it is sufficient on its own. (2) [CONTESTED — audit flagged at "possible" confidence] DATE UNVERIFIED: S.B. 2136, reported signed by Gov. Lee 22 May 2026 with immediate effect and no wind-down, prohibits online sweepstakes games using a virtual-, dual- or multi-currency system exchangeable for cash, prizes or anything of value that simulate casino-style gambling; violations are unfair or deceptive acts under the Tennessee Consumer Protection Act with AG investigative authority. The Act also targets prediction markets. Confirm the signing date and immediate-effect characterisation from the enrolled bill. Blocked by Skillz. Absolute geofence — limb (1) alone justifies it.

    Tenn. Code Ann. s.39-17-501(1) (definition of 'gambling', 2024 Tennessee Code in force — VERIFIED limb); Tenn. S.B. 2136 / H.B. 1885 — signing date and immediate effect UNVERIFIED. Regulator: Tennessee Attorney General; Tennessee Sports Wagering Council.

  • TexasUNSETTLED

    Texas criminalises making a bet on the partial or final result of a game or contest or on the performance of a participant. There is a social-gambling defence — private place, no economic benefit other than personal winnings, equal odds for all participants — but it is UNAVAILABLE TO AN OPERATOR that takes a fee or rake, which is the whole business model. SB 517 (redefining gambling devices) failed in 2026. Texas has no gaming regulator and enforcement is by district attorneys, which makes exposure unpredictable rather than absent. Model B: geofence on a conservative read. Model C: Texas opinion needed, focused on whether an operator-funded fixed prize is a 'bet'.

    Tex. Penal Code s.47.01(1) ('bet') and s.47.02 (gambling; social-gambling defence at s.47.02(b)) — text UNVERIFIED. Regulator: Texas Attorney General; local district attorneys.

  • UtahCLOSED

    Utah is the most absolutist gambling state in the country. 'Gambling' means 'RISKING ANYTHING OF VALUE FOR A RETURN or risking anything of value upon the outcome of a contest, game, gaming scheme, or gaming device when the return or outcome is based on an element of chance' — note that the FIRST clause carries no chance qualifier at all. Combined with the constitutional prohibition, there is no lawful gambling of any kind in Utah and no regulator to licence you. Utah is also one of the eight states with NO explicit agent-of-payee money-transmitter exemption. Operator-level restrictions on sweepstakes platforms already reported. Absolute geofence for Models B and C.

    Utah Code Ann. s.76-10-1101(2) (definition of 'gambling', 2024 Utah Code in force); Utah Const. art. VI s.27. Regulator: Utah Attorney General.

  • VermontCLOSED

    Vermont permits persons to organise, execute or participate in contests or sweepstakes PROVIDED THAT persons who enter are NOT REQUIRED TO VENTURE MONEY OR OTHER VALUABLE THINGS. On that wording, paid entry disqualifies the contest — the same structural bar as Montana. Vermont is easy to miss because it appears on no sweepstakes-ban list. No enacted online sweepstakes ban. Geofence Models B and C pending verification.

    13 V.S.A. s.2143b (contests and sweepstakes) — quoted text obtained from a Justia/Vermont Legislature listing summary; DIRECT FETCH FAILED, so treat the quotation as strong but RE-VERIFY before relying. 13 V.S.A. ch. 51 (gambling and lotteries). Regulator: Vermont Attorney General; Vermont Department of Liquor and Lottery.

  • VirginiaUNSETTLED

    Virginia's illegal-gambling definition turns on an outcome dependent on chance and is generally understood to carry an exception for a contest of speed or skill between men, animals, fowl or vehicles where participants may receive prizes — which would support Model C. Gov. Spanberger VETOED legislation to legalise electronic skill gaming devices in April 2026, and HB 161 (iGaming plus sweepstakes ban) failed in the 2026 session; SB 579 would categorise unlicensed online sweepstakes games as illegal gambling with civil penalties of $10,000-$100,000 per violation. Separately relevant to Model D: Virginia's automatic-renewal amendments take effect 1 Jul 2026, requiring an online cancel path at least as easy as sign-up. Monitor — Virginia is likely to enact a sweepstakes ban.

    Va. Code s.18.2-325 (definitions) and s.18.2-334 (exception for contests of speed or skill) — text UNVERIFIED; Va. SB 579 (2026, pending); Virginia automatic renewal law amendments eff. 1 Jul 2026 (bill number UNVERIFIED). Regulator: Virginia Lottery Board / Virginia Gaming Commission; Virginia Attorney General.

  • WashingtonCLOSED

    THE MOST DANGEROUS STATE IN THE UNION FOR THIS PRODUCT, AND THE ONE THAT REACHES MODEL A. (1) Operating internet gambling — knowingly transmitting or receiving gambling information over the internet — is a CLASS C FELONY. (2) 'Thing of value' expressly includes 'any form of credit or promise ... involving extension of a service, entertainment or a privilege of playing at a game or scheme without charge'. In Kater v. Churchill Downs Inc., 886 F.3d 784 (9th Cir. 28 Mar 2018), the Ninth Circuit held Big Fish Casino's virtual chips were a 'thing of value' on exactly that basis, because without chips a user cannot play — making the free-to-play social casino illegal gambling under Washington law. The related claims settled for a reported $155m. (3) The 'contest of chance' test is material-degree, so randomised question draws are enough. (4) Washington's Recovery of Money Lost at Gambling Act gives every Washington player a private right to recover losses. (5) Sweepstakes trackers list Washington, with Idaho, as already prohibiting the dual-currency model under pre-existing law. GEOFENCE WASHINGTON FOR EVERY MODEL THAT INVOLVES STAKING PURCHASED COINS, INCLUDING MODEL A. Footnote: Washington's money-transmitter surety bond minimum is among the lowest at $10,000.

    Wash. Rev. Code s.9.46.240 (internet gambling, Class C felony); s.9.46.0285 ('thing of value'); s.9.46.0237 ('gambling'); s.9.46.0225 ('contest of chance', material degree); RCW s.4.24.070 (recovery of money lost at gambling); Kater v. Churchill Downs Inc., 886 F.3d 784 (9th Cir. 2018). Regulator: Washington State Gambling Commission.

  • West VirginiaUNSETTLED

    No enacted sweepstakes ban and no verified skill-wagering prohibition. West Virginia has a full iGaming regulator and permits esports wagering, which signals that paid-entry skill contests are regulable rather than per se criminal. Model C plausibly defensible; Model B unsettled.

    W. Va. Code s.61-10-1 et seq. (gaming) — text UNVERIFIED. Regulator: West Virginia Lottery Commission.

  • WisconsinUNSETTLED

    Wisconsin's 'bet' definition is chance-based with exclusions for bona fide business transactions and, on the usual model-penal-code pattern, for bona fide contests of skill, speed, strength or endurance — but I could not verify the exclusion text. No enacted sweepstakes ban. Wisconsin adopted the MTMA. Model C plausibly defensible; verify before launch.

    Wis. Stat. s.945.01(1) ('bet') and exclusions — text UNVERIFIED. Regulator: Wisconsin Department of Administration, Division of Gaming.

  • WyomingUNSETTLED

    Wyoming's gambling definition turns on gain 'contingent in whole or in part upon lot, chance, the operation of a gambling device' and is generally understood to exclude bona fide contests of skill, speed, strength or endurance in which awards are made only to entrants or the owners of entries — one of the more permissive framings. No enacted sweepstakes ban. Wyoming is one of the eight states with NO explicit agent-of-payee money-transmitter exemption, but its money-transmitter surety bond minimum is among the lowest at $10,000. Model C plausibly the most permissive state; verify the skill exclusion text.

    Wyo. Stat. s.6-7-101(a)(iii) (definition of 'gambling' and skill-contest exclusion) — text UNVERIFIED. Regulator: Wyoming Gaming Commission.

    The original brief never mentioned tribal gaming. Exclusivity provisions in state-tribal compacts in California, Florida, Oklahoma and Connecticut give tribes independent standing and enforcement leverage against unlicensed real-money gaming, and tribal objection has killed products that had a clean reading of the state criminal code. Stage 2 — Licences, registrations, notifications 14

  • Register as a Money Services Business with FinCEN (FinCEN Form 107, 'Registration of Money Services Business'), filed electronically through the BSA E-Filing System

    ISSUER
    FinCEN (Financial Crimes Enforcement Network), U.S. Department of the Treasury
    COST
    UNVERIFIED
    LEAD TIME
    UNVERIFIED (statutory window is 180 days from establishment, not a processing time)
    APPLIES TO
    B mandatory; C only if you hold withdrawable cash balances for users or the prize pool is entrant-funded; A and D not required

    NEEDS FIRST US legal entity with EIN; named owner/controlling person to sign; BSA E-Filing account

    31 C.F.R. § 1022.380: file within 180 days after the date the MSB is established; renew every two years on or before 31 December of the second calendar year of each registration period. Re-registration is separately triggered by a change of ownership/control requiring state-law re-registration, a transfer of more than 10% of voting power or equity (for non-SEC reporters), or a >50% increase in the number of agents. CORRECTED TRIGGER AUTHORITY (audit defect 5): the original brief rested the trigger analysis on FinCEN Guidance FIN-2019-G001 § 4.7. That section is titled 'CVC Money Transmission Performed by Internet Casinos' and reaches only a person who 'accepts and transmits value denominated in CVC'. A dollar-denominated withdrawable user balance is plain 'funds', and the governing provision for it is 31 C.F.R. § 1010.100(ff)(5)(i)(A). Use § 4.7 only for the CVC-denominated variant; do not let counsel be briefed on the wrong provision. NOT LEGAL ADVICE — get a written BSA analysis from US counsel before deciding you are outside this.

  • Register for the federal wagering occupational tax before accepting any wagers — IRS Form 11-C, 'Occupational Tax and Registration Return for Wagering'

    ISSUER
    Internal Revenue Service
    COST
    $50 per year where the wager is authorised under the law of the state in which accepted; $500 per year otherwise (26 U.S.C. § 4411)
    LEAD TIME
    UNVERIFIED — must be filed before wagers are accepted
    APPLIES TO
    B (likely); C only if counsel concludes entry fees are 'wagers' — see corrected analysis, which is now materially FAVOURABLE for an operator-funded fixed-prize contest

    NEEDS FIRST Determination (with counsel) whether entry fees are 'wagers' under 26 U.S.C. § 4421, AND a Fifth Amendment analysis — see below

    26 U.S.C. §§ 4411, 4412. CORRECTED (audit defects 1 and 2): the original brief said 'skill-vs-chance is irrelevant here' and that the fixed-operator-funded-prize defence was untested with no authority in existence. Both are wrong. Rev. Rul. 57-521, 1957-2 C.B. 779 holds that contesting for a prize offered by another in a contest of mental or physical skill, which the offeror must award in any event, is not gaming, and that an entry fee is not a wager 'unless the entrance fees alone comprise the prize'. AM 2020-009 (23 July 2020) discusses and endorses that ruling, and applied the tax to DFS specifically BECAUSE DFS entrants 'have no ability to exercise control or influence over the actions of the players' — the opposite of a knowledge duel. AM 2020-009 is non-precedential (26 U.S.C. § 6110(k)(3)); Rev. Rul. 57-521 is a published revenue ruling and is citable. AUDIT ADDITION — FIFTH AMENDMENT (missing item 2): registering on Form 11-C at the $500 UNAUTHORISED rate is a public admission of accepting wagers not authorised under the law of the state where accepted, i.e. potential evidence of an 18 U.S.C. § 1955 / state gambling offence. Marchetti v. United States, 390 U.S. 39 (1968) and Grosso v. United States held the self-incrimination privilege a complete defence to prosecution for failure to register/pay the wagering taxes on the then-current scheme; the CURRENT scope of that privilege after subsequent statutory amendments is UNVERIFIED and must be settled with counsel BEFORE anyone files at the unauthorised rate. Do not file blind.

  • Monthly federal excise tax on wagers — IRS Form 730, 'Monthly Tax Return for Wagers'

    ISSUER
    Internal Revenue Service
    COST
    0.25% of the amount wagered if authorised under the law of the state in which accepted; 2% if not (26 U.S.C. § 4401(a))
    LEAD TIME
    Monthly return
    APPLIES TO
    B if entry/stake money is treated as wagers; C only if the Rev. Rul. 57-521 position fails

    26 U.S.C. § 4401. The 2% rate on unauthorised wagers is applied to HANDLE, not revenue — at scale it can exceed gross margin. Model this before launch. CORRECTED FRAMING: this is a live and severe risk for MODEL B (player-v-player staking with a rake is the paradigm 'wagering pool ... conducted for profit', and the stake money alone comprises the prize — the exact Rev. Rul. 57-521 trigger). For MODEL C with an operator-funded, fixed, pre-announced prize NOT contingent on entrant numbers, the cited authority points the other way. The original brief's 'under-appreciated killer' framing was directionally wrong for Model C.

Separately, the same Act amended 26 U.S.C. § 165(d) to cap wagering-loss deductions at 90% of losses from the 2026 tax year.     AUDIT ADDITION (missing item 8). The original brief discussed this only in the risks narrative, never as a checklist action, even while calling it 'the real cost driver for Models B and C'. FinCEN Form 107 is a federal REGISTRATION with no approval step and authorises money transmission in NO state. Roughly every state requires its own money transmitter licence. This is outside the federal lane for substance but belongs on the action list because a founder working the checklist top-to-bottom would otherwise never see it — and because it, not federal law, is what determines whether Models B and C are financeable at all. All state-specific figures UNVERIFIED here.

  • Decide whether you will HOLD player funds at all — and then answer the THRESHOLD question the brief skipped: are you transmitting money TO ANOTHER PERSON? CORRECTED: state money transmitter licensing is not an automatic consequence of holding player funds.

    ISSUER
    State banking/financial-institution departments, applied for through NMLS (Nationwide Multistate Licensing System)
    COST
    IF required: application fees commonly $500-$10,000 per state plus investigation, fingerprint and NMLS processing fees; surety bonds from $10,000 (WA, WY) to $500,000 (NY), California scaling to $7,000,000 on volume; tangible net worth the greater of $100,000 or 3% of total assets up to $100m under the MTMA formula. All figures are aggregated from licensing-consultancy and law-firm sources, NOT from state fee schedules — re-price from current NMLS state-specific requirements before budgeting.
    LEAD TIME
    3-12 months per state for a complete application; New York and California commonly 12-18 months or longer
    APPLIES TO
    B (squarely). C only if a transmission-to-another-person analysis says so — do not assume it.

    NEEDS FIRST Audited financials, business plan, BSA/AML programme, background checks on control persons, FinCEN MSB registration (federal — out of this lane)

    CORRECTED, and this matters more than any other line in the checklist. Most state MTL regimes — and the CSBS MTMA definition this brief relies on elsewhere — turn on RECEIVING MONEY FOR TRANSMISSION TO ANOTHER PERSON. An operator that takes deposits and pays its OWN prize obligations out of its OWN funds is frequently not transmitting to a third party and may fall outside the definition entirely in many states. That is precisely MODEL C: operator-funded, fixed, entrant-count-independent prizes. MODEL B — where you hold one player's stake for payment to another player — squarely raises money transmission. The brief's flat '49 states plus DC, the single largest cost and time line item in the whole plan' materially OVERSTATES the burden for the very design it recommends. Correct posture: assume a full 49-state-plus-DC programme for Model B (multi-year, seven-figure, effectively disqualifying for a startup); for Model C, get a money-transmission opinion on the transmission-to-another-person question FIRST, because the answer may be that no licence is required at all. Which states depart from the transmission-to-another-person formulation is UNVERIFIED and is the specific question to put to counsel.

  • Understand that the Money Transmission Modernization Act does NOT give you one licence. CORRECTED: the brief's '31 states have enacted the MTMA ... Massachusetts became the 26th adopter' is internally inconsistent — 31 enacted and Massachusetts as the 26th cannot both be right in the same sentence. TREAT THE ADOPTER COUNT AS UNVERIFIED.

    ISSUER
    Conference of State Bank Supervisors (model law); adopted state by state
    COST
    n/a — it harmonises net worth, surety bond and permissible-investment standards, not fees
    LEAD TIME
    n/a
    APPLIES TO
    B, C

    The operative conclusion does not depend on the count and should be stated without false precision: PLAN FOR 49 APPLICATIONS, NOT ONE. CSBS says MTMA states cover the large majority of reported money transmission activity, but Cooley is explicit that 'managing compliance with money transmission laws generally remains a state-by-state proposition' — states added bespoke provisions, excluded payroll processors, and diverged on bonds (Maine set a fixed bond instead of the model formula). Massachusetts (eff. 1 Jan 2026) and Virginia (eff. 1 Jul 2026) are reported adopters; the roster and the total are UNVERIFIED.

    The original brief cleared Models A and D as 'lawful' with no caveat beyond in-app-purchase disclosure. Both accumulate STORED VALUE.   • COPPA compliance programme: neutral age screen before any personal information is collected, direct notice and verifiable parental consent for under-13s, COPPA-compliant privacy policy, data retention/deletion policy, strengthened security requirements

    ISSUER
    Federal Trade Commission
    COST
    UNVERIFIED (VPC vendor fees)
    LEAD TIME
    UNVERIFIED
    APPLIES TO
    A, B, C, D — all models; unavoidable for a trivia product, which reads as child-attractive

    NEEDS FIRST Decide and document whether the service is child-directed, mixed-audience, or general-audience

    15 U.S.C. §§ 6501-6506; 16 C.F.R. Part 312. Amended Rule published 22 April 2025, effective 23 June 2025, FULL COMPLIANCE REQUIRED SINCE 22 APRIL 2026 — this is live law today, not a future deadline. Mixed-audience services must age-screen BEFORE collecting personal information, in a neutral manner that does not default to a set age or encourage falsification ('What is your date of birth?', never 'Are you over 13?'). The FTC's 'Enforcement Policy Statement Promoting the Adoption of Age-Verification Technology' of 25 February 2026 is confirmed to exist and is said to give limited relief for data collected solely to run age-verification technology — BUT ITS CONDITIONS AND SCOPE ARE UNVERIFIED IN THIS LANE (audit addition, missing item 5). A money model that hard-blocks under-18s depends on exactly that collection, so read the policy statement in full and confirm the conditions (permitted data categories, retention limits, whether it covers third-party vendors) before relying on it. Practical answer for any money model: hard-block under-18s entirely and document it — state minimum gambling/contest ages make under-18 money play indefensible regardless of COPPA.

  • FTC Act § 5 and Lanham Act disclosure and purchase-flow review: single-step purchase confirmation, no dark patterns, plain-language coin pricing, disclosure of any AI/bot opponents, disclosure of matchmaking logic, no account lockout for chargeback disputes

    15 U.S.C. § 45. Regulator benchmarks: U.S. v. Epic Games (order finalised March 2023, $245m refunds; bars charging via dark patterns and bars locking accounts over disputed charges) and U.S. v. Cognosphere LLC (complaint filed 17 January 2025, C.D. Cal., $20m; bars loot-box sales to under-16s without parental consent and targets confusing multi-step currency conversion). CORRECTED (audit defect 6): Skillz Platform Inc. v. Papaya Gaming, Ltd., No. 1:24-cv-01646 (S.D.N.Y.), Judge Denise Cote, is NOT an FTC Act § 5 or COPPA case — FTC Act § 5 has no private right of action and the FTC was not a party. It was a competitor suit under the LANHAM ACT and NEW YORK GENERAL BUSINESS LAW. An April 2026 jury returned a $420m verdict; the 28 July 2026 judgment separately awarded $719m in disgorgement as an ALTERNATIVE remedy the plaintiff may ELECT IN LIEU OF damages, plus $10.1m in fees — alternative, not cumulative, so '$719m plus $10.1m' as a running total is wrong. It is a district-court judgment, subject to appeal to the Second Circuit, not final, and NOT PRECEDENT anywhere (audit addition, missing item 3). The operational lesson survives all of that: FACT//DUEL's duel mode must never pit a paying player against an AI without unmissable disclosure, and a competitor can bring this claim with no regulator involved at all.

CORRECTED (audit defect 4): the FTC's 2024 AMENDED Negative Option Rule (the click-to-cancel regime) was vacated in its entirety by the Eighth Circuit in Custom Communications, Inc. v. FTC, No. 24-3137 (8th Cir. 8 July 2025). That vacatur restored the pre-existing 1973 PRENOTIFICATION NEGATIVE OPTION RULE, which REMAINS IN FORCE at 16 C.F.R. Part 425 (audit addition, missing item 4). It is wrong to say 'there is currently no FTC negative option rule' — there is currently no CLICK-TO-CANCEL rule. Check whether your offer is a prenotification plan within Part 425. The FTC restarted the rulemaking: draft ANPRM submitted to OIRA 30 January 2026, ANPRM PUBLISHED 11 MARCH 2026, comments due 13 April 2026; it asks both whether to amend the EXISTING rule and about certain provisions of the vacated click-to-cancel rule. ROSCA and § 5 enforcement continues throughout. Build to click-to-cancel standards regardless — and note that in several states it is not optional (see the state automatic-renewal item below).

    B

    NEEDS FIRST Legal determination of whether FACT//DUEL 'simulates casino-style gambling' — most of the ten express bans require that element and a trivia duel arguably does not, but CA Penal Code s.337a(a)(6) and FL s.849.14 catch skill wagering independently.     CA Penal Code s.337o, NY Rac. Pari-Mut. Wag. & Breeding L. s.912 and OK S.B. 1589 all extend liability to payment processors, financial institutions, geolocation providers, content suppliers and MEDIA AFFILIATES who knowingly support the platform. That means your Stripe/Adyen equivalent, your geolocation vendor and your affiliate marketers can be prosecuted — in practice they will refuse to onboard you.

  • Restructure the paid product to Model C: fixed prize pool, announced BEFORE entries open, funded by the operator, NOT scaled to entrant count, with the operator as a neutral non-participant taking no rake from a player-funded pot.

    ISSUER
    n/a — design control
    COST
    Operator-funded prize liability (a real P&L cost, unlike a rake)
    LEAD TIME
    Design/build
    APPLIES TO
    C

    NEEDS FIRST Kill player-v-player escrow entirely on the paid path

    This tracks the design attributes the New York Court of Appeals treated as characteristic of lawful skill competition in White v. Cuomo, 38 N.Y.3d 209 (2022): 'skill-based competitions in which participants who exercise substantial influence over the outcome of the contest are awarded predetermined fixed prizes by a neutral operator'. CORRECTED — DO NOT TREAT THIS AS CLEARANCE. White v. Cuomo was decided under Article I s.9 of the NEW YORK CONSTITUTION, about interactive fantasy sports that the legislature had affirmatively authorized in Racing Law article 14 AND expressly exempted from Penal Law article 225. It has no precedential force outside New York, and it does NOT hold that an unauthorized paid-entry trivia contest escapes the material-degree contest-of-chance test at N.Y. Penal Law s.225.00(1). The brief's instruction to 'build to that sentence' would let a founder conclude New York is cleared by design compliance alone. It is not. Use the three attributes as a design target; get a New York opinion separately. The reported 4-3 vote split is UNVERIFIED — do not cite it. An entrant-count-dependent pot converts the structure back into a wager and loses even the design argument.

    LEAD TIME

    B, C

Code s.39-17-501(1), 'to any degree contingent on chance'). FACT//DUEL's Expedition mechanic — staking points at three confidence tiers on rotating question sets — is the highest-risk surface here and must not carry real money.
