  • [AUDIT ADDITION] Cost the free-entry / alternative-method-of-entry (AMOE) option before committing to Model C, and understand that it is the most legislatively targeted structure in the country right now.

    ISSUER
    n/a — design control, with counsel
    COST
    Engineering plus fulfilment of free entries; prize liability unchanged
    LEAD TIME
    Design/build
    APPLIES TO
    B, C

    NEEDS FIRST A per-state opinion on whether removing consideration actually opens any state you care about

    The brief jumped from Model B to Model C without evaluating a genuine AMOE. A free entry of equal dignity (no purchase necessary, equal odds, equal prize eligibility, a real and usable free path) removes the CONSIDERATION element and can open states whose bar is consideration-based. It does NOT help where the statute does not turn on consideration in that way — Arizona, Florida, Delaware, South Dakota and Louisiana among them. And the decisive caution: THE ENTIRE 2025-26 DUAL-CURRENCY BAN WAVE CATALOGUED IN THIS LANE IS THE LEGISLATIVE RESPONSE TO EXACTLY THIS STRUCTURE — sweepstakes operators used an AMOE to argue they were not gambling, and ten states legislated against it. An AMOE is a legitimate option for a promotional contest; it is not a safe harbour in 2026. Which states it opens: UNVERIFIED.

    APPLIES TO
    B

    [CONTESTED — audit flagged at "possible" confidence] This is a verification flag, not an allegation of fabrication. Every 2025-26 enactment that COULD be reached checked out exactly: CA AB 831 (signed 11 Oct 2025, Ch. 623, adds Penal Code s.337o); NY S5935A (signed 5 Dec 2025, Ch. 605, adds Rac. Pari-Mut. Wag. & Breeding L. s.912, $10,000-$100,000 per violation); OK S.B. 1589 (vetoed 7 May 2026, overridden 14 May 2026, Class C2 felony, eff. 1 Nov 2026, with the dual-currency 'representative of value' definition and geolocation/platform/media-affiliate liability verbatim as described). For the other five, primary legislative sources were blocked, JS-rendered or empty. NJ A5447's TEXT is confirmed to exist and to target dual-currency sweepstakes with civil penalties of $100,000 first / $250,000 subsequent, but its ENACTMENT and the 15 Aug 2025 signing date are not confirmed. Confirm all five before relying on the specific dates.

Risks specific to United States

  • STATE-LAW PREEMPTION REALITY — THERE IS NONE. 31 U.S.C. § 5361(b) is an express rule of construction: 'No provision of this subchapter shall be construed as altering, limiting, or extending any Federal or State law or Tribal-State compact prohibiting, permitting, or regulating gambling' in the United States. PASPA was struck down in Murphy v. NCAA (2018), removing the only modern federal attempt to constrain state choice. UIGEA (§ 5362(10)(A)), the Illegal Gambling Business Act (18 U.S.C. § 1955(b)(1)(i)) and the Travel Act (18 U.S.C. § 1952) all take their unlawfulness FROM state law. Clearing the federal layer tells you almost nothing about whether you can operate in any given state.
  • THE UIGEA 'EDUCATIONAL GAME OR CONTEST' TRAP. § 5362(1)(E)(ix) reads 'participation in any fantasy or simulation sports game or educational game or contest...' and is routinely quoted as if those opening words were a self-contained carve-out for quiz products. They are not. Clause (ix) imposes three cumulative conditions, and (ix)(II) requires outcomes 'determined predominantly by accumulated statistical results of the performance of individuals ... in multiple real-world sporting or other events'. No trivia game can satisfy that. If any adviser tells the founder that FACT//DUEL is a UIGEA-exempt 'educational contest', that adviser has misread the statute.
  • CORRECTED RISK — THE FEDERAL WAGERING EXCISE TAX IS A MODEL B PROBLEM, NOT A MODEL C KILLER. The original brief said this was 'the most likely way this product loses money quietly', that § 4421 'makes no skill/chance distinction', and that AM 2020-009 applied §§ 4401/4411 to DFS 'on exactly that basis'. The audit refuted all three at 'certain' confidence and this pass adopts the refutation. AM 2020-009 states the statute 'does not differentiate whether an activity involves skill, chance, or some combination of the two' and then immediately says 'Still, the presence or absence of skill in a contest may help determine whether an entry fee constitutes a taxable wager, or not', distinguishing DFS from Rev. Rul. 57-521's puzzle contest on the ground that the DFS entrant 'has no ability to exercise control or influence over the actions of the players'. Rev. Rul. 57-521, 1957-2 C.B. 779 holds that an entry fee in a contest of mental skill with a guaranteed operator-funded prize is not a wager 'unless the entrance fees alone comprise the prize'. WHAT REMAINS TRUE: the 2% unauthorised rate is levied on HANDLE, not net revenue, so IF the analysis goes against you the tax can exceed gross margin — model it. But for Model C with an operator-funded fixed prize the leading authority is favourable, and for Model B (stake money alone comprising the prize) it is adverse. The risk is Model B's, and the residual Model C risk is that chance elements — question draw, matchmaking, reaction-time scoring — pull the product toward the DFS side of the line the IRS itself drew.
  • AUDIT ADDITION — DO NOT FILE FORM 11-C WITHOUT A FIFTH AMENDMENT ANALYSIS. The checklist tells the founder to register before accepting any wagers, and the $500 rate applies where wagers are NOT authorised under the law of the state where accepted. Filing at that rate is a signed, dated federal admission of accepting unauthorised wagers — i.e. potential evidence in a 18 U.S.C. § 1955 or state gambling prosecution. Marchetti v. United States, 390 U.S. 39 (1968) and Grosso v. United States held the self-incrimination privilege a complete defence to failure-to-register/pay prosecutions under the then-existing wagering tax scheme. The CURRENT scope of the privilege after later amendments to §§ 4411/4412 is UNVERIFIED here. Settle it with counsel before filing. A compliance step that manufactures the evidence for a criminal case is not a compliance step.
  • MODEL A DEGRADES INTO MODEL B ON A SINGLE PRODUCT DECISION. One cash-out path, one peer-to-peer coin transfer, one 'redeem coins for a gift card' feature, or one tournament that pays a prize of value, and the coin becomes convertible virtual currency, FinCEN money-transmitter obligations attach, and the state sweepstakes bans engage. FinCEN 'interprets these exemptions strictly' (FIN-2019-G001 at 9). Put a hard architectural boundary — not a policy note — between the closed-loop coin and anything redeemable. Note also the corrected authority position: once coins are SOLD, § 5362(1)(E)(viii) stops protecting them, so Model A's only remaining federal argument is 'no prize of value' under § 5362(1)(A). That argument fails the instant anything of value is paid out.
  • THE WIRE ACT IS NARROW BUT NOT SETTLED NATIONWIDE. N.H. Lottery Comm'n v. Rosen, 986 F.3d 38 (1st Cir. 20 Jan. 2021) limits 18 U.S.C. § 1084 to sporting events, and DOJ did not seek certiorari. But the 2018 OLC opinion reading § 1084 to cover all interstate wagering has never been formally withdrawn, and the First Circuit's holding binds only Maine, Massachusetts, New Hampshire, Rhode Island and Puerto Rico. In the other circuits there is no controlling authority. AUDIT ADDITION: the § 1084(b) safe harbour (transmission of information for use in news reporting, and transmission between jurisdictions where the betting is legal) is a threshold defence the original analysis skipped. Treat the Wire Act as low-probability, high-severity tail risk rather than a resolved question.
  • CORRECTED — BOTS AND MATCHMAKING ARE A NINE-FIGURE PRIVATE-LITIGATION RISK, BROUGHT BY COMPETITORS, NOT REGULATORS, AND NOT YET FINAL. Skillz Platform Inc. v. Papaya Gaming, Ltd., No. 1:24-cv-01646 (S.D.N.Y., Cote, J.) produced a $420m April 2026 jury verdict; the 28 July 2026 judgment separately awarded $719m in DISGORGEMENT as an ALTERNATIVE remedy the plaintiff may elect IN LIEU OF damages, plus $10.1m in fees. The figures are alternative, not cumulative — the original brief's headline '$719m' and checklist '$719m plus $10.1m' read as a running total and overstate the award. The claims were under the LANHAM ACT and NEW YORK GENERAL BUSINESS LAW, not FTC Act § 5 or COPPA; the FTC was not a party and FTC Act § 5 has no private right of action. It is a district-court judgment, subject to appeal to the Second Circuit, NOT FINAL, and NOT PRECEDENT anywhere. What survives every one of those corrections: the conduct found — marketing real-money 'skill-based head-to-head' tournaments while secretly filling them with bots — is precisely FACT//DUEL's exposure. The product currently has no server and no real opponents; whatever fills a duel must be audited and disclosed before a single dollar is staked. A competitor can bring this claim with no regulator interest at all.
  • MSB REGISTRATION IS NOT A LICENCE AND WILL NOT SATISFY THE STATES. FinCEN Form 107 is a federal registration with no approval step. It does not authorise money transmission in any state. Roughly every state requires its own money transmitter licence with bonding and net-worth requirements — a multi-year, multi-million-dollar programme that is outside this federal lane but is the real cost driver for Models B and C. It is now a checklist item (audit addition), not merely a narrative aside.
  • HOLDING USER BALANCES IS THE SINGLE DESIGN CHOICE THAT MOST CHANGES THE LEGAL ANSWER. A withdrawable cash balance makes the operator look like a money transmitter under 31 C.F.R. § 1010.100(ff)(5)(i)(A) — which is the correct provision for dollar-denominated value, not FIN-2019-G001 § 4.7 — even where the game itself is defensible. One-way payouts (money in, contest entered, prize paid out directly to the winner's funding instrument, no stored redeemable value) preserve the 'integral to the sale of services' exemption at § 1010.100(ff)(5)(ii)(F). This is worth designing around from day one and is the highest-leverage single decision in the whole file.
  • COPPA IS ALREADY IN FULL FORCE, NOT PENDING. The amended Rule's compliance date passed on 22 April 2026. A trivia product with cartoon-adjacent presentation, no accounts and no age gate is a plausible FTC target on child-directedness alone, entirely independently of anything to do with money. The FTC's 25 February 2026 age-verification enforcement policy statement is confirmed to exist but its CONDITIONS ARE UNVERIFIED — a hard under-18 block depends on collecting exactly the data that statement addresses, so read it before designing the gate.
  • AUDIT ADDITION — MODEL D IS NOT A CONSUMER-PROTECTION FREE PASS. Two corrections compound here: the 1973 Prenotification Negative Option Rule survived the click-to-cancel vacatur and remains at 16 C.F.R. Part 425, and state automatic-renewal laws (California, New York and others) impose click-to-cancel-equivalent duties as binding law. 'Federally lawful' plus 'build to click-to-cancel standards anyway' understated both. Model D is the cheapest model to launch and the easiest one to get sued over for a bad cancellation flow.
  • FEDERAL CLEARANCE ON MODEL A OR D DOES NOT MAKE THE CURRENT PRODUCT FUTURE-PROOF. The existing FREE demo-coin game sits squarely inside 31 U.S.C. § 5362(1)(E)(viii)(II) — 'points or credits that the sponsor of the game or contest provides to participants free of charge and that can be used or redeemed only for participation in games or contests offered by the sponsor'. That exclusion is written around free credits and it stops applying the moment coins are sold for money. It therefore protects today's product and none of the four proposed models.
  • AUDIT ADDITION — § 5363 HAS A THRESHOLD THE ORIGINAL ANALYSIS ASSUMED AWAY. 31 U.S.C. § 5363 prohibits acceptance of payment by 'a person engaged in the business of betting or wagering' in connection with unlawful internet gambling. Whether FACT//DUEL is such a person is a contestable element, not a given, particularly for Model C. Do not concede it. Argue the threshold before arguing the exclusions.
  • THE SKILL ARGUMENT DOES NOT SAVE MODEL B. CORRECTED COUNT: TEN states, not eleven, legislate around the skill/chance question — Arizona (gambling includes contests 'of chance or skill'), California Penal Code s.337a(a)(6) (bets on contests of skill BETWEEN PERSONS), Florida s.849.14, Delaware 11 Del. C. s.1403, Illinois 720 ILCS 5/28-1(a)(1) ('game of chance or skill'), Louisiana R.S. 14:90 (no chance element at all), Nevada NRS 463.0152 (no skill/chance distinction), South Dakota SDCL s.22-25A-1 (contest or game, no chance element), South Carolina (Chimento), Arkansas s.5-66-104. MONTANA IS REMOVED: the brief's Mont. Code Ann. s.23-5-110 'paid entry disqualifies a skill contest' claim is withdrawn — that section is 'Public policy of state concerning gambling' and defines nothing, and Montana's actual gambling definition at s.23-5-112(14)(a) is chance-based (both verified at mca.legmt.gov, 14 Sep 2026). Any pitch deck or investor memo that says 'trivia is skill so we are fine' is still wrong on the law in the remaining ten.
  • YOUR VENDORS GO DOWN WITH YOU. California Penal Code s.337o, New York Rac. Pari-Mut. Wag. & Breeding L. s.912 and Oklahoma S.B. 1589 all extend criminal or civil liability to payment processors, financial institutions, geolocation providers, content and technology suppliers, platform providers, promoters and MEDIA AFFILIATES who knowingly support a prohibited operator. The practical consequence is not that you get prosecuted first — it is that no reputable PSP, geolocation vendor or affiliate network will onboard you, and any that does will terminate on first notice, stranding player balances mid-contest.
  • PRIVATE LOSS-RECOVERY LITIGATION IS A BIGGER THREAT THAN PROSECUTION. Illinois 720 ILCS 5/28-8 (recover losses of $50+); KENTUCKY — CORRECTED — KRS 372.020 (the loser's own action, $5+) AND THE SEPARATE KRS 372.040 (after six months, ANY OTHER PERSON may sue for TREBLE the loss); Washington RCW 4.24.070; plus analogues in Georgia, South Carolina, Massachusetts and Ohio. s.372.040 is the provision that creates the real exposure: it turns a private remedy into a bounty available to strangers and litigation funders. These need no conviction, no regulator and no intent — just a player who lost money on an unlicensed game. The Kater line settled for a reported $155m. The 2026 class actions against Kalshi, Robinhood and Webull used exactly this machinery across five states at once, funded by a litigation-finance firm. [AUDIT ADDITION] An arbitration clause and class-action waiver are the standard mitigation but do not reliably bind users below the age of majority — 19 in Alabama and Nebraska, 21 in Mississippi.
  • WASHINGTON AND NEW YORK REACH MODEL A. RCW 9.46.0285 and N.Y. Penal Law s.225.00(6) ('Something of value' — CORRECTED from the brief's s.225.00(1), which is 'Contest of chance'; verified at nysenate.gov 14 Sep 2026) both define a thing of value to include the privilege of playing a game without charge. Kater v. Churchill Downs (9th Cir. 2018) applied that to virtual chips. FACT//DUEL's existing demo-coin escrow — coins staked into escrow, winner takes the escrow — is structurally the same mechanic. If those coins ever become purchasable, the product is arguably illegal gambling in Washington with no cash-out feature at all. The fix is architectural and must be designed in now: purchased coins must be spend-only and structurally incapable of being staked.
  • THE 2026 LEGISLATIVE WAVE IS NOT OVER. CORRECTED: about TEN states enacted express dual-currency prohibitions between June 2025 and November 2026 (CA, CT, IN, LA, ME, MT, NJ, NY, OK, TN), and TWO more (IA, NV) granted regulators open-ended enforcement authority without defining the offence. Bills failed but will return in Minnesota, Florida, Massachusetts, Mississippi, Hawaii, Texas and Arkansas; bills are live in DC, Ohio, Virginia, Maryland and Missouri. A compliance posture built on today's map will be out of date within one legislative session. Budget for ongoing monitoring, and build the geofence as a configuration table with a per-state effective-date column, not as hard-coded logic. [AUDIT ADDITION] As at 14 Sep 2026 Oklahoma (1 Nov 2026) is the ONLY listed prohibition not yet operative — do not stage a launch into that seven-week window.
  • MOST SWEEPSTAKES BANS REQUIRE THE GAME TO 'SIMULATE CASINO-STYLE GAMBLING', AND A TRIVIA DUEL PROBABLY DOES NOT — BUT DO NOT BUILD ON THAT GAP. It is untested, it is a question of characterisation that a hostile AG will resolve against you, Oklahoma's 'representative of value' definition is drafted broadly enough to reach any dual-currency exchangeable for any chance to win, Iowa's S.F. 2289 is a pure enforcement grant with no definitional limit at all, and California s.337a(a)(6) and Florida s.849.14 close the gap independently of any sweepstakes ban.
  • MONEY TRANSMISSION IS THE HIDDEN COST CENTRE — BUT CORRECTED, AND THE CORRECTION CUTS IN THE FOUNDER'S FAVOUR FOR MODEL C. State MTL regimes, and the CSBS MTMA definition, generally turn on receiving money FOR TRANSMISSION TO ANOTHER PERSON. Model B — holding one player's stake for payment to another player — squarely raises money transmission and, if required across the country, means licences in 49 states plus DC: fees $500-$10,000 per state, bonds from $10,000 to $500,000 (California scaling to $7m), tangible net worth the greater of $100,000 or 3% of assets, 3-12 months per state and 12-18+ for New York and California. That is a multi-year, seven-figure programme and it is effectively disqualifying for a startup. Model C — operator-funded, fixed, entrant-count-independent prizes paid from the operator's own funds — is frequently NOT transmission to a third party and may fall outside the definition entirely in many states. The brief collapsed the two and called a 49-state programme 'the single largest cost and time line item in the whole plan' for both, which materially overstates the burden for the design it recommends. Get a money-transmission opinion on the threshold question before assuming the programme exists. The MTMA harmonises standards, not licences, and its adopter count as stated in the brief was internally inconsistent and is UNVERIFIED. The safest structure remains never taking custody, via a licensed PSP or sponsor bank.
  • APP STORE POLICY MAY BE A HARDER GATE THAN STATE LAW. Both Apple and Google require real-money gaming apps to be submitted by the licensed entity and geo-restricted to licensed territories. CORRECTED: the reason is not that no licence exists anywhere — Nevada NRS 463.160 and New Jersey's DGE esports event wagering licence are real categories — but that none of them fits a peer-to-peer mobile trivia app at viable cost or lead time, so there is no licence you can actually show the stores. Model B may simply be unshippable on either store regardless of how the state analysis comes out. Confirm current store policy directly before spending on legal opinions.
  • THE PRODUCT'S CURRENT MECHANICS ARE THE WORST-CASE FACTS. A 5/7/10-second timer compresses the skill window and amplifies reaction-time variance; randomised question sets introduce a draw-luck element; Expeditions stake at three confidence tiers on rotating question sets, which reads as a wager on an uncertain outcome; and matchmaking determines your opponent. Each of those is a chance input a prosecutor or plaintiff will point to under a material-element test. If real money is ever introduced, the paid path must be redesigned: identical question sets for all entrants, no confidence-tier variance, and a fixed operator-funded prize.
  • NO STATE OFFERS A COMMERCIALLY VIABLE LICENCE FOR THIS — CORRECTED FROM 'NO STATE LICENSES THIS'. The brief stated a categorical 51-jurisdiction absolute that its own Nevada entry contradicted. Licensure exists in principle: Nevada NRS 463.0152 defines a gambling game as any game played for money with NO skill/chance distinction and NRS 463.160 licenses exactly that, and New Jersey's DGE licenses esports event wagering. What is true is that no state has an OFF-THE-SHELF licence category that fits a peer-to-peer mobile trivia app at viable cost and lead time: the categories that exist are casino, sports wagering, fantasy sports, lottery, charitable gaming and, in a handful of states, esports wagering, and a Nevada non-restricted licence is a multi-year, multi-million-dollar suitability process. The practical conclusion for a startup is unchanged — 'lawful-only-with-licence' is not a path you can fund — but the claim must be stated accurately, because an investor or counsel will find the Nevada contradiction immediately.
  • [AUDIT ADDITION] TRIBAL GAMING COMPACTS ARE A STATE-LAYER RISK THE ORIGINAL BRIEF NEVER MENTIONED. Exclusivity provisions in state-tribal compacts in California, Florida, Oklahoma and Connecticut give tribes independent standing and enforcement leverage against unlicensed real-money gaming. Tribal objection has killed products that had a clean reading of the state criminal code, and it arrives through a different channel — compact dispute mechanisms, federal court, and political pressure on the state regulator — so a clean penal-code opinion does not dispose of it. Oklahoma additionally grants tribal operators exclusive online gambling rights. Compact provisions and their reach to a non-casino skill product: UNVERIFIED. Scope the gaming-counsel engagement to cover it.
  • [AUDIT ADDITION] UNCLAIMED PROPERTY IS A LIVE OBLIGATION FOR THE TWO MODELS THE BRIEF CLEARED AS 'LAWFUL'. Models A and D both accumulate stored value in unredeemed coin and credit balances. Several states treat unredeemed prepaid balances as ABANDONED PROPERTY with reporting and remittance duties to the state treasurer after a dormancy period. The brief cleared A and D with no caveat beyond in-app-purchase disclosure. The engineering consequence is immediate even though the legal one is not: you need a server-authoritative per-user balance ledger with last-activity timestamps, which FACT//DUEL's local-only IndexedDB store cannot provide. Per-state thresholds, dormancy periods and whether closed-loop non-redeemable game currency is exempt: UNVERIFIED.
  • [AUDIT ADDITION] GEOFENCING THE COMPANY IS NOT THE SAME AS PROTECTING THE USER. Several statutes this lane relies on reach the person who PLAYS OR BETS, not only the operator — 720 ILCS 5/28-1(a)(1) and Cal. Penal Code s.337a among them. The brief disentangled the two only for Illinois. Whoever writes the terms of service needs to know, per state, whether the block list protects the company, the user, or both, because it determines the refund posture, whether a balance must be returned to a user who played from a prohibited state, and what the product may truthfully tell users about their own exposure. Per-state scope: UNVERIFIED.
  • [AUDIT ADDITION] THE FREE-ENTRY (AMOE) ROUTE IS THE MOST LEGISLATIVELY TARGETED STRUCTURE IN THE COUNTRY RIGHT NOW. The brief jumped from Model B to Model C without evaluating whether a genuine alternative method of entry would remove the consideration element and open more states — the structure the sweepstakes industry actually uses. It is a real option and leaving it unmapped leaves the strategy space incomplete. But the decisive fact is that the ENTIRE 2025-26 dual-currency ban wave catalogued in this lane exists precisely BECAUSE operators used an AMOE to argue they were not gambling. An AMOE is legitimate for a promotional contest; it is not a safe harbour in 2026, and it does not help at all in states whose prohibition does not turn on consideration (AZ, FL, DE, SD, LA). Cost it before committing; do not adopt it without a per-state opinion. UNVERIFIED as to which states it opens.

European Union

There is no EU gambling licence and no EU gambling regulator — the Commission states flatly that there is no sector-specific EU legislation in the field of gambling services — so FACT//DUEL's money product must be licensed (or lawfully structured) state by state across 27 regimes. Model B (staking redeemable for cash) is not launchable as a pan-EU proposition: the EU definitions turn on stake + chance + prize, no member state accepts a dual paid-plus-free entry route as cleansing the paid route, and in the largest markets there is no licence class that fits a player-v-player knowledge stake. Model C (paid-entry knowledge contest) splits the Union three ways, but NOT along the lines the uncorrected brief drew: outside gambling law in Germany and Austria ONLY IF chance does not predominate — and in Germany a paid skill contest that escapes the GlueStV lands in § 33d GewO, which requires its own trade permit (AUDIT ADDITION; online application contested); contested-but-adverse in the Netherlands, where appellate authority has run against skill claims; LICENSABLE, NOT EXEMPT, in Sweden (Spellagen's kombinationsspel expressly covers skill-plus-chance games — the uncorrected brief's placement of Sweden outside gambling law was wrong), Ireland, Italy, Spain, Denmark and Malta; and caught in France because L320-1 CSI bites wherever ANY part of the gain is due to chance — France does not prohibit skill contests as a class, but FACT//DUEL's randomly drawn questions and reflex timers almost certainly put it inside the prohibition. Models A and D need no gambling licence anywhere, CONDITIONALLY: Model A becomes criminal exposure in Belgium if coins buy randomised rewards, and any competitive advantage bought by a Model D subscription converts it into Model C.

  • A CLEAR

    MODEL A is lawful under every horizontal EU instrument in this lane, and the reason is cleaner than the original brief stated. (1) E-MONEY — CORRECTED. Coins are not electronic money because EMD2 Art 2(2) limb (ii) fails: only FACT//DUEL accepts them, no third party does, and they are not transferable as a separate monetary asset (EBA Q&A 2022_6336). Limb (i) also fails because they are not issued for the purpose of making payment transactions. The invariant to protect is NON-TRANSFERABILITY AND NO THIRD-PARTY ACCEPTANCE — no player-to-player gifting or trading, no priceable secondary market, no acceptance of your coin by any other merchant. The original brief told you that a goodwill cash refund or an account-closure balance return could make you an e-money issuer; the auditor is right that this is wrong, because a refund from you to your own customer involves no third party accepting anything. Consumer-friendly refunds are lawful and in fact partly mandatory. (2) VAT — CORRECTED AND STILL BINDING. A non-EU seller of a digital service to an EU consumer owes VAT at the consumer's member-state rate from the first euro: VAT Directive Art 58 (place of supply), Art 73 (taxable amount), and Art 59c's EUR 10,000 micro-threshold applies only to EU-ESTABLISHED sellers. That conclusion rests on the directive, NOT on CJEU C-472/24. C-472/24 MB 'Zaidimu valiuta' (judgment 5 March 2026) concerned a THIRD-PARTY TRADER reselling RuneScape gold on a secondary market, not a publisher issuing its own currency; the Court held that exchanging real currency for units usable only inside an online game is not exempt under Art 135(1)(e) and is not a voucher under Art 30a, so Art 73 general rules apply. Extending that to a first-party issuer's own coin sale is a reasonable inference, not a holding, and the single-purpose voucher analysis under Art 30a(2) for a first-party issuer was NOT before the Court. Take the conservative position (VAT at sale of coins) but know it is inference. (3) WITHDRAWAL — RE-LABELLED. CRD Art 9 gives a 14-day withdrawal right on the coin purchase contract. The CPC Network's further position — that in-game virtual currency is NOT itself digital content, so the Art 16(1)(m) waiver cannot extinguish that right, and unspent coins must be cash-refundable — is SOFT LAW that Video Games Europe and EGDF dispute and on which German courts have gone the other way (properly-run waivers upheld). Build the refund path because it is cheap and because it is the stated enforcement posture; do not describe it as settled law. (4) PRICING / UCPD — RE-LABELLED. Real-money price display at the undiscounted bundle rate, exact-amount coin purchase, no stacked currencies, and spending-off-by-default parental controls are CPC Key Principles positions built on UCPD Arts 5-9 and CRD Art 6(1)(e). The underlying UCPD misleading-omission analysis is real law; the specific prescriptions are the Network's reading of it. (5) AUDIT ADDITIONS. Directive (EU) 2019/770 (Digital Content Directive) is the core contract-law instrument here and was missing entirely: Arts 6-8 conformity, Arts 14-18 remedies and reimbursement on termination, and critically Art 19, which governs when you may MODIFY digital content or a digital service during continuous supply and gives the consumer a free termination right where a modification adversely affects access or use — that, not UCTD Art 3 alone, is what constrains post-launch coin rebalancing. CRD Art 8(2)'s 'order with obligation to pay' button labelling applies to EVERY coin-purchase button, not just a subscription confirm; non-compliance means the consumer is NOT BOUND by the contract, i.e. revenue reversal. Directive 2000/31/EC Arts 5, 6 and 10 impose provider-identification and commercial-communication duties alongside CRD Art 6, and its Art 3 country-of-origin principle gives a non-EU-established provider nothing. Rome I Regulation (EC) 593/2008 Art 6(1)-(2) is why all of this binds you at all: a trader directing activities to a member state is subject to the mandatory consumer-protection law of the consumer's habitual residence, and a choice-of-law clause cannot take it away; Brussels Ia Regulation (EU) 1215/2012 Art 18 lets the consumer sue at home. None of this makes Model A unlawful; all of it makes an unmodified launch unlawful.

  • B LICENCE

    MODEL B breaks the horizontal perimeter, but for corrected reasons. (1) E-MONEY — CORRECTED. The question is NOT 'is the balance redeemable'. It is whether EMD2 Art 2(2) limb (ii) is satisfied: does someone other than the issuer accept the instrument, is it transferable, is it voluntarily accepted as a separate monetary asset (EBA Q&A 2022_6336, citing CJEU C-661/22)? In a dual-currency sweepstakes design the coins are staked PLAYER-VERSUS-PLAYER, which is exactly a transfer of stored value between persons other than the issuer, and winnings redeemable for cash or prizes of value supply the payment purpose under limb (i). That combination — not redeemability alone — is what makes Model B presumptively e-money issuance requiring EMI authorisation from a national competent authority (Central Bank of Ireland, BaFin, DNB, CSSF, MFSA and others), with initial capital, own-funds, safeguarding of client funds in segregated accounts, and passporting. A narrowly drafted operator-only redemption with NO player-to-player transfer might stay outside the definition; that is a perimeter question for counsel, not a founder. (2) PAYMENT SERVICES. Taking money from Player 1 into escrow and paying it to Player 2 looks like execution of payment transactions or money remittance (PSD2 Art 4(22), Annex I point 6). CJEU C-51/25 Betaal Garant Nederland (16 July 2026) narrowed this — transfers made only to deliver another, primary, non-payment service are not themselves a payment service, and a 'credit transfer' requires the intermediary to hold the payer's payment account — but commentators stress it is fact-specific and it expressly did NOT address money remittance. Holding named per-player balances is precisely what pushes you back inside. (3) AML — CORRECTED, AND THIS IS THE MOST DANGEROUS ERROR IN THE ORIGINAL BRIEF. AML obligations for gambling service providers are ALREADY LIVE across the EU under national transpositions of Directive (EU) 2015/849 (AMLD4, transposition deadline 26 June 2017) as amended by AMLD5. AMLD4 Art 3(14) already defines 'gambling services' in materially identical words to the AMLR — 'a service which involves wagering a stake with monetary value in games of chance, including those with an element of skill such as lotteries, casino games, poker games and betting transactions... provided at a physical location, or by any means at a distance, by electronic means... and at the individual request of a recipient of services' — and AMLD4 Art 2(1)(3)(f) has made providers of gambling services obliged entities EU-wide since 2017. The AMLR carries that wording into a regulation; it does not invent it. A Model B launch in 2026 with no AML programme would be unlawful in most member states ON DAY ONE. Comply with national AML law NOW; re-paper to Regulation (EU) 2024/1624 (AMLR) by 10 July 2027. CDD trigger for gambling: transactions of EUR 2,000 or more on collection of winnings, wagering of a stake, or both, single or linked (AMLD4 Art 11(d); carried into AMLR Art 19 — exact paragraph number UNVERIFIED). (4) PSD3/EMD2 REPEAL — AUDIT ADDITION, MATERIAL. PSD3 repeals EMD2 outright and merges the EMI and payment-institution regimes. Existing EMI authorisations survive only 24 months after entry into force (extendable to 30) with a MANDATORY RE-APPLICATION. An EMI licence obtained for Model B in 2027 would need re-papering almost immediately. (5) And then 27 national gambling regimes, outside this lane, where sweepstakes-casino structures have been the specific target of 2025-2026 enforcement. Verdict unchanged: not launchable in the EU without regulated-entity status or a licensed partner acting as merchant/issuer of record.

  • C UNSETTLED

    MODEL C is the cleanest money-play structure under horizontal EU law but its characterisation is genuinely open, and the original brief overstated how much of it was settled. WHAT IS REASONABLY SETTLED: (a) if the entry fee is paid by card directly to you through an authorised PSP and the announced prize is paid out of your own treasury, you are not storing transferable third-party-accepted value, so EMD2 does not bite — this is the single design choice that keeps Model C outside e-money; (b) the CRD withdrawal right is manageable because a contest entry is a service the consumer expressly asks to begin immediately, and the Art 16(1)(a)/16(1)(m) waiver flow (separate click, explicit acknowledgement of loss of the right, Art 8(7) durable-medium confirmation) can be run properly; (c) after C-51/25 Betaal Garant a fixed prize paid from operator funds is a strong 'ancillary to the primary service' story for PSD2 purposes. WHAT IS NOT SETTLED — VAT, CORRECTED. The original brief called the VAT treatment of the entry fee 'settled' after testing it only against Art 135(1)(e) (currency transactions). The relevant provision is VAT Directive Art 135(1)(i), which exempts 'betting, lotteries and other forms of gambling, subject to the conditions and limitations laid down by each Member State'. If a member state characterises the paid-entry timed duel as gambling — precisely the question this brief calls unresolved for AML purposes — the entry fee may be VAT-EXEMPT in that state, outside the OSS return, with knock-on input-VAT recovery consequences. You cannot label the gambling characterisation 'unsettled' for AML and 'settled' for VAT when the same characterisation drives both. Related and also missing from the original: the Glawe line (C-38/93 Glawe; C-377/11 International Bingo Technology) can make the taxable amount for a wagering leg the OPERATOR'S RETAINED MARGIN rather than gross stakes. Both materially change the VAT model. WHAT IS NOT SETTLED — AML CHARACTERISATION. The 'games of chance, including those with an element of skill' wording has been EU law since AMLD4 Art 3(14) in 2015, not since the AMLR, so this exposure is PRESENT-TENSE, not a 2027 problem. A 5/7/10-second timed trivia duel over a randomly drawn question set is not obviously a pure skill contest: the question draw is stochastic and the timer converts knowledge into a probabilistic race. Whether such a contest is a gambling service (obliged entity, full CDD at EUR 2,000, FIU reporting) or a skill competition outside gambling entirely has no EU-level answer and no case law. THE ESCAPE HATCH THE ORIGINAL BRIEF OMITTED — AUDIT ADDITION. AMLD4 Art 2(2) expressly permits member states, other than for casinos and following a risk assessment, to 'exempt, in full or in part, providers of certain gambling services' from AML obligations on the basis of proven low risk, with notification to the Commission; the AML package preserves an equivalent low-risk exemption option. So whether a given paid-entry skill contest is an obliged entity remains a PER-MARKET answer both today and after 10 July 2027 — and this is the most plausible route for Model C. Ask, per market, whether the exemption has been exercised and whether your product falls in it. The fixed, pre-announced, entrant-count-independent prize pool remains the single most helpful fact — it removes the pooled-stake character most national regimes key on — but it does not resolve the statutory wording. Requires member-state counsel in each launch market; a favourable answer in one state is not portable.

  • D CLEAR

    MODEL D is lawful under EU horizontal law and remains by a wide margin the lowest-risk monetisation for an EU launch: no stake, no prize, no stored transferable value, therefore no EMD2 perimeter question, no PSD2 issue beyond using an authorised PSP, no AML. What still applies: (1) VAT — a subscription supplied to an EU consumer is taxable at the consumer's rate under Art 58; a non-EU-established seller must register for non-Union OSS before the first sale, with no EUR 10,000 threshold (Art 59c is only for EU-established cross-border sellers). (2) CRD — Art 6 pre-contractual information, Art 8(2) 'order with obligation to pay' labelling on the confirm button, Art 9 14-day withdrawal on the initial subscription, and if you want immediate access the Art 16(1)(m) waiver must be run correctly: express consent plus acknowledgement of loss of the right in a click SEPARATE from the buy click, plus Art 8(7) durable-medium confirmation. (3) UCPD — cancellation must not be harder than sign-up; dark-pattern cancellation flows are the expected target of the forthcoming Digital Fairness Act. (4) Auto-renewal notice and easy-cancellation rules are set NATIONALLY, not by the CRD, and diverge sharply (Germany, France, Italy). (5) Do NOT include the old EU ODR-platform link in your terms: Regulation (EU) No 524/2013 was repealed by Regulation (EU) 2024/3228 with effect from 20 July 2025 and the platform is switched off. ADR information duties under Directive 2013/11/EU survive. AUDIT ADDITIONS THAT LAND HARDEST ON A SUBSCRIPTION: Directive (EU) 2019/770 (DCD) Art 19 governs modification of a continuously-supplied digital service and gives the consumer a free termination right where a modification adversely affects access or use — this is the instrument that constrains changing what the subscription includes mid-term, and it was absent from the original brief; DCD Arts 14-18 govern conformity remedies, price reduction and reimbursement on termination, and the 'legal guarantee of conformity' you must disclose pre-contractually comes from here. Directive (EU) 2020/1828 on representative actions (applicable 25 June 2023) makes a subscription with a defective cancellation flow a collective-redress target, not just a regulator target. The European Accessibility Act (Directive (EU) 2019/882) has APPLIED since 28 June 2025 to consumer-facing e-commerce services; you are exempt only while you are a microenterprise service provider (Art 4(5)) — under 10 staff and under EUR 2m turnover.

This is the most plausible AML escape hatch for Model C.   • [AUDIT ADDITION] All 27 member states — European Accessibility Act national enforcementUNSETTLED

    [AUDIT ADDITION]. Directive (EU) 2019/882 requirements have APPLIED since 28 June 2025 and expressly cover e-commerce and consumer-facing digital services — websites and mobile apps used to conclude consumer contracts, i.e. your coin shop and subscription checkout. Art 4(5) exempts microenterprises PROVIDING SERVICES, which is the only reason FACT//DUEL can ignore it today; the exemption falls away at 10 staff or EUR 2m turnover. Enforcement is by NATIONAL market surveillance authorities with national penalty regimes, so the practical exposure varies by market and the specific national penalty provisions are UNVERIFIED. Design the checkout and the timer/motion/audio-cue vocabulary to accessibility standards now: retrofitting a juice-heavy, timer-driven game UI later is materially more expensive than building it in.

  • All 27 member states — ePrivacy Art 5(3) implementationUNSETTLED

    Because ePrivacy is a directive, consent rules for storing/accessing information on the user's device (your IndexedDB analytics state, not the strictly-necessary game state) are implemented and enforced nationally, with material differences in what counts as valid consent and how aggressively it is policed. France (CNIL) and Germany (TDDDG, DSK guidance) are the practical high-water marks.

    Directive 2002/58/EC Art 5(3) as amended by Directive 2009/136/EC; national implementations

  • All 27 member states — gambling licensingUNSETTLED

    Entirely outside harmonised EU law. Models B and C get a different answer in each state, and a licence or exemption in one confers nothing in another. Member-state counsel required per launch market. Out of this lane; flagged so it is not mistaken for a settled EU-level answer. [CORRECTED EMPHASIS] The same national characterisation also drives two things the original brief treated as EU-uniform: whether you are an AML obliged entity TODAY under national AMLD4/5 law, and whether the VAT Art 135(1)(i) gambling exemption applies.   • All 27 member states — GDPR Article 8 age of consentUNSETTLED

    The age at which a child can consent to an information-society service without parental authorisation is set at 16 by default but member states may lower it to any age down to 13. This is not a uniform number: your parental-consent gate must be country-aware, and a single hardcoded age will be wrong somewhere. Note this only matters where CONSENT is your lawful basis — for contract-based processing (Art 6(1)(b)) the relevant question is national contractual capacity of minors, which also varies.

    Regulation (EU) 2016/679 Art 8(1); national implementing laws

  • All 27 member states — subscription auto-renewal and cancellation (Model D)UNSETTLED

    The CRD does not harmonise auto-renewal notice periods, maximum initial terms, or cancellation mechanics for ongoing subscriptions — these are national rules and they diverge materially, with Germany, France and Italy among the most prescriptive. Several states require a specific easy-cancellation mechanism. Exact national provisions NOT VERIFIED; obtain local counsel before launching Model D in any specific market. [AUDIT ADDITION] Note also that Directive (EU) 2019/770 Art 19 is EU-level hard law on MODIFYING a continuously-supplied digital service mid-term, and Directive (EU) 2020/1828 makes a defective cancellation flow a collective-redress target in any of these states.

    Directive 2011/83/EU does not cover this; national consumer statutes — specific sections UNVERIFIED. Directive (EU) 2019/770 Art 19; Directive (EU) 2020/1828

  • DenmarkLICENCE

  • EU level (horizontal) — AUDIT ADDITIONNO LICENCE, BUT BINDING HORIZONTAL OBLIGATIONS

    There is no EU gambling licence, no EU gambling regulator and no mutual recognition. What IS directly applicable EU-wide: (1) AMLR Regulation (EU) 2024/1624 from 10 July 2027 — providers of gambling services are obliged entities (Art. 3(3)(g)); CDD on collection of winnings, wagering of a stake, or both, at EUR 2,000 or more (ART. 19(5), corrected from the uncorrected brief's Art. 20(5)); Art. 4 lets member states exempt low-risk gambling services, with an exclusion for providers whose principal activity is online gambling or sport betting BUT WITH A CARVE-BACK for state-operated and state-regulated online gambling, and subject to a Member State risk assessment and Commission process; whether a genuinely chance-free Model C is an AMLR 'gambling service' at all is CONTESTED, because Art. 2(1)(12) still requires a game of chance. (4) Digital Content Directive (EU) 2019/770, Consumer Rights Directive withdrawal right, DSA minor-protection provisions.   • FranceCAUGHT — GEO-BLOCK FOR ANY PAID CONTEST WITH A CHANCE ELEMENT (STATUS REASONING CORRECTED: FRANCE DOES NOT PROHIBIT SKILL CONTESTS AS A CLASS)

    AUDIT CORRECTION TO THE HEADLINE CHARACTERISATION. The uncorrected brief called France 'THE CLEAREST NO IN THE EU, AND IT IS SPECIFICALLY A NO TO SKILL GAMES'. That overstates the provision. On the brief's own quoted text, Art. L320-1 CSI prohibits operations offered to the public 'pour faire naitre l'esperance d'un gain qui serait du, MEME PARTIELLEMENT, AU HASARD et pour lesquelles un sacrifice financier est exige', then adds: 'Cette interdiction recouvre les jeux dont le fonctionnement repose sur le savoir-faire des joueurs. Le sacrifice financier est etabli dans les cas ou une avance financiere est exigee de la part des participants, meme si un remboursement ulterieur est rendu possible par le reglement du jeu.' CHANCE REMAINS A CONSTITUENT ELEMENT. The savoir-faire sentence closes the skill-PREDOMINANCE escape for MIXED games; it does not prohibit paid contests decided wholly by knowledge or ability, and paid chess and bridge tournaments and quiz competitions continue lawfully in France. THE CORRECT STATEMENT: France catches any paid contest with ANY chance element — which on FACT//DUEL's own facts (randomly drawn questions, reflex timers) probably does catch it. The second sentence independently defeats Model B's free-alternative-entry structure. The only adjacent carve-out, video-game competitions at Arts. L321-8 to L321-11 CSI (Decret n° 2017-871 of 9 May 2017), applies only where participants are PHYSICALLY PRESENT and caps entry fees at a decreed fraction of total organisation cost (the percentage is UNVERIFIED and has not been guessed) — paid online competitions do not qualify. ANJ licences cover only sports betting, horse betting and online poker; online casino is prohibited. Models A and D are unaffected. PRACTICAL CONCLUSION UNCHANGED: geo-block France for the current product. A fully deterministic fixed-question no-timer format would need a French opinion rather than an assumption.

    Code de la securite interieure Art. L320-1, as amended by Ordonnance n° 2019-1015 du 2 octobre 2019, in force 1 January 2020; Arts. L321-8 to L321-11 CSI; Decret n° 2017-871 du 9 mai 2017; Loi n° 2010-476 du 12 mai 2010. Regulator: Autorite Nationale des Jeux (ANJ). L320-1 WORDING WAS NEVER DIRECTLY FETCHED — Legifrance returned 403 to both the original researcher and the auditor; the quotation rests on a search-engine rendering of the Legifrance/Lexbase text. HIGH CONFIDENCE, NOT PRIMARY.

  • GermanySPLIT BY MODEL — MODEL C OUTSIDE THE GLUESTV IF SKILL PREDOMINATES BUT A § 33D GEWO TRADE PERMIT IS ENGAGED; MODEL B HAS NO FITTING PERMISSION CLASS

    THE LARGEST EU MARKET AND THE MOST CHANGED ENTRY IN THIS CORRECTED EDITION. § 3(1) GlueStV 2021: gambling exists where 'im Rahmen eines Spiels fuer den Erwerb einer Gewinnchance ein Entgelt verlangt wird und die Entscheidung ueber den Gewinn ganz oder ueberwiegend vom Zufall abhaengt'. BOTH elements are needed, so a contest decided by intellectual performance is not Gluecksspiel even with an entry fee and § 284 StGB does not bite. AUDIT ADDITION AND THE DOCUMENT'S BIGGEST OMISSION: that does NOT mean no German permission is required. § 33d GewO requires an Erlaubnis from the competent authority for commercially operating 'andere Spiele mit Gewinnmoeglichkeit' — other games with a chance to win that are NOT Gluecksspiel — which is precisely the category a paid skill contest falls into when it escapes § 3(1) GlueStV; a Bundeskriminalamt Unbedenklichkeitsbescheinigung is a precondition and the permit must be refused where the applicant lacks the required reliability (provision verified at gesetze-im-internet.de, 14 Sep 2026). ITS APPLICATION TO PURELY ONLINE OFFERINGS IS CONTESTED — which is exactly why it must be in the German opinion rather than absent from the analysis. The uncorrected brief's advice to 'run Model C in Germany unlicensed' is WITHDRAWN. MODEL B — AUDIT CORRECTION: the uncorrected brief described the GGL's permission classes as exhaustive (sports betting § 21, online poker § 22b, virtual slot machines § 22a, lotteries, horse betting) and concluded Model B was 'unlicensable'. The GGL's own legal-basis page states GlueStV 2021 made licensable virtuelle Automatenspiele, Online-Poker UND ONLINECASINOSPIELE, and online casino games are authorised by the individual LAENDER through their own concession procedures, not by the GGL. The enumeration was incomplete and the categorical 'unlicensable' is withdrawn — the accurate statement is that no existing class fits a player-v-player knowledge stake, so the product cannot be licensed in its current shape, but restructuring into a licensable class is not categorically foreclosed. THE RISK THAT SURVIVES UNCHANGED: FACT//DUEL's 5/7/10-second timers on randomly drawn questions push the 'ueberwiegend vom Zufall' analysis toward chance. The GGL and the Laender must complete a statutory evaluation of GlueStV 2021 by 31 December 2026 (§ 32, section number SECONDARY); a reformed treaty would not take effect before roughly 2027-2028 (SECONDARY). Trade press reported a 'small adjustment' targeted for spring 2026 ratification — NOT CORROBORATED against any official source and NOT RELIED ON. AUDIT ADDITION: German advertising limits under the GlueStV, and JuSchG youth-protection duties, apply independently — UNVERIFIED as to detail. LICENCE/PERMIT FEES AND LEAD TIMES UNVERIFIED for both the GGL permission and the § 33d GewO Erlaubnis.

  • IrelandLICENCE-REQUIRED (MODEL C AS WELL AS MODEL B) — BUT EVERY DATE AND THRESHOLD IN THIS ENTRY IS UNVERIFIED

    Ireland matters because its definition of gaming expressly catches skill: 'games of skill or chance (or partly of skill and partly of chance) where the participant pays money for the chance to win a prize of money (or money's worth)' — no skill carve-out, so Model C needs a licence just as Model B does. Lotteries are separately defined to include activities involving 'a guess of future events or of past events where the results are not generally known' — phrasing close enough to a trivia product to warrant specific counsel. AUDIT CORRECTION TO THE CONFIDENCE LEVEL, NOT THE CONCLUSION: the uncorrected brief called Ireland 'THE MOST CONSEQUENTIAL NEW REGIME FOR THIS PRODUCT' while resting the entire verdict on a law-firm restatement of the s. 2 definitions and a six-date commencement timeline, none of it primary-sourced. irishstatutebook.ie, oireachtas.ie and grai.ie all returned 403 to both the researcher and the auditor. TREAT AS PROVISIONAL. The reported timeline — Act signed 23 October 2024; commencement order signed 3 February 2026 effective 5 February 2026; remote betting / remote betting intermediary / in-person betting applications opened 9 February 2026 via the GRAI Operator Portal; remote betting licence requirement from 1 July 2026; in-person from 1 December 2026 — is UNVERIFIED, though the betting dates are corroborated across DLA Piper, LexisNexis and gov.ie. The reported exemption thresholds (one-off charitable lotteries with prizes under EUR 2,000; marketing lotteries with a EUR 5,000 prize limit) are UNVERIFIED. REMOTE GAMING (B2C) LICENCES ARE REPORTED AS NOT YET OPEN — variously Q4 2026 or 2027-28; confirm with the GRAI. Reported as already in force: credit-card gambling ban, mandatory customer spending limits for remote gambling, child protection rules, suspicious-behaviour reporting (UNVERIFIED). Administrative sanctions reported up to EUR 20m or 10% of turnover (UNVERIFIED). B2B licensing also reportedly applies to software and hosting suppliers. FEES ARE NOT SET IN THE ACT — they are to be prescribed by the GRAI with ministerial consent, so no fee figure should be budgeted.

    Gambling Regulation Act 2024 (No. 35 of 2024), s. 2 definitions — QUOTED VIA A&L Goodbody's guide (December 2024) and DLA Piper (February 2026), SECONDARY; irishstatutebook.ie returned 403 to both researcher and auditor. Commencement order of 3 February 2026 — UNVERIFIED. Regulator: Gambling Regulatory Authority of Ireland (GRAI).

  • ItalyLICENCE-REQUIRED — AND PRICED OUT OF REACH FOR AN EARLY-STAGE PRODUCT UNTIL THE CONCESSION FEE IS CONFIRMED

    ITALY DOES NOT EXEMPT SKILL — IT LICENSES IT. D.M. 17 settembre 2007 n. 186, 'Regolamento per la disciplina dei giochi di abilita a distanza con vincita in denaro', governs remote skill games with cash prizes and expressly covers games where the result depends PREDOMINANTLY OVER the random element on the skill of the players. ADM authorisation of both the concession and the specific game platform project is required. Proving skill predominance in Italy puts you INSIDE the regime. The remote sector was reordered by D.Lgs. 25 marzo 2024 n. 41 (in force 4 April 2024, implementing Art. 15 of Law 111/2023). AUDIT ADDITIONS, BOTH DECISIVE AND BOTH ABSENT FROM THE UNCORRECTED BRIEF: (1) D.Lgs. 41/2024 sets a multi-million-euro fee per remote concession, widely reported at around EUR 7m for a nine-year concession — FIGURE UNVERIFIED, but the order of magnitude removes Italy from an early-stage footprint, and listing Italy as an action item with cost 'UNVERIFIED' materially understated the barrier. (2) Italy operates a near-total gambling advertising ban under the Decreto Dignita, so a licensed Italian product could be legal to operate and impossible to market. CONTESTED: the reported award of 52 concessions to 46 operators in September 2025 with a 5-concession cap per operator could not be confirmed by the auditor. Regulator: Agenzia delle Dogane e dei Monopoli (ADM).

    D.M. 17 settembre 2007 n. 186 (as modified by the AAMS directive decree of 5 February 2010); D.Lgs. 25 marzo 2024 n. 41, in force 4 April 2024. Concession counts and the ~EUR 7m per-concession fee: SECONDARY AND UNVERIFIED. Decreto Dignita advertising ban: audit addition, detail UNVERIFIED.

  • LuxembourgUNCLEAR — TREAT AS CLOSED

    Reported as the only EU member state with no dedicated commercial online gambling licensing regime — the Loterie Nationale operates the sanctioned offer and there is no route to licence a private online operator.   • MaltaLICENCE-REQUIRED — THE PRAGMATIC ESTABLISHMENT CHOICE, WITH A HARD CEILING AND A CORRECTED LEGAL BASIS

    CITATION CORRECTED — THE UNCORRECTED BRIEF CITED A REPEALED INSTRUMENT AS THE LIVE REGIME. The Skill Games Regulations (S.L. 438.11), in force 24 January 2017 under the Lotteries and Other Games Act (Cap. 438), were repealed by Act XVI of 2018, the Gaming Act (Cap. 583), which replaced Cap. 438 and its subsidiary legislation. DO NOT CITE S.L. 438.11. The current instruments are the Gaming Act Cap. 583 and its subsidiary legislation (the MGA's regulatory-framework page lists S.L. 583.3-583.12; the Gaming Authorisations Regulations are reported as S.L. 583.05, NOT 438.11 — S.L. NUMBER UNVERIFIED). THE SUBSTANTIVE CONCEPT SURVIVES: a controlled skill game is still a recognised category under Cap. 583 and MGA Directive 6 of 2018 ('Controlled Skill Games Ruling', 1 August 2018), which does exist. A skill game is a game for money or money's worth offered by distance communication whose result is determined by skill alone or predominantly by skill. THE TYPE 3 / TYPE 4 CHOICE IS A CHARACTERISATION WITH EU-WIDE CONSEQUENCES — AUDIT CORRECTION: the uncorrected brief said the 'likelier fit' for duels is Type 3 ('games of chance not played against the house and wherein the operator is not exposed to gaming risk' — P2P with a rake) without flagging that Type 3 is by definition a class of GAMES OF CHANCE. Filing for Type 3 concedes the product is a game of chance, which destroys the skill-predominance argument in Germany and Austria, weakens it in the Netherlands, and strengthens capture in Denmark, Sweden, Spain, Ireland and Italy. Type 3 and Type 4 are not interchangeable fallbacks. THE GAP: under Directive 6 of 2018, fantasy sports remains the only game the MGA has classified as a controlled skill game, so a trivia duel needs a fresh MGA determination and the burden of proving skill predominance is on the applicant — budget for a pre-application ruling request. COSTS (MGA guidance note, Dec 2024 v3, VERIFIED): application fee EUR 5,000; fixed annual licence fee EUR 10,000 if providing solely Type 4, otherwise EUR 25,000 for a B2C gaming service licence; Type 4 compliance contribution min EUR 5,000 / max EUR 500,000 at 0.50% rising to 2.00% by band; Type 3 min EUR 25,000 / max EUR 500,000 at 4.00% tapering to 0.40%; minimum share capital EUR 40,000 for Types 3 and 4, EUR 100,000 for Types 1-2. GAMING TAX — AUDIT FLAGGED AS POSSIBLY FABRICATED; EDITOR VERIFIED IT IS REAL: from 1 OCTOBER 2026 (A FUTURE DATE as at 14 September 2026) the single 5% rate is replaced by 15% of aggregate gaming revenue for Type 1 and 10% for Types 2, 3 and 4, with 5% retained for controlled gaming premises and junkets; the device-based levy is abolished and a higher fixed studio broadcasting levy introduced. MATERIAL AND PREVIOUSLY MISSED: for distance gaming the taxability test changes from the player's PHYSICAL PRESENCE in Malta to whether the player is established, has a permanent address and/or usually resides in Malta — the old 'only players physically in Malta' shorthand no longer states the rule. THE LEGAL NOTICE NUMBERS (reported as LN 84/2026 and LN 86/2026) REMAIN UNVERIFIED. A separate VAT change also takes effect 1 October 2026 — out of lane. CEILING: an MGA licence does NOT passport. Bill 55 (2023) inserting Art. 56A into the Gaming Act — requiring Maltese courts to refuse recognition of foreign judgments against MGA licensees — drew a Commission letter of formal notice, and in Case C-440/23 (16 April 2026) the CJEU held a member state may prohibit online gambling licensed elsewhere and may allow players to recover losses. LEAD TIME UNVERIFIED — no MGA service-level commitment exists; consultancy estimates of 4-6 months are marketing material.

    Gaming Act (Cap. 583), enacted by Act XVI of 2018, repealing the Lotteries and Other Games Act (Cap. 438) and its subsidiary legislation INCLUDING S.L. 438.11 — CITATION CORRECTED PER AUDIT (legislation.mt). Gaming Authorisations Regulations (reported S.L. 583.05) — REGULATION AND SECTION NUMBERS UNVERIFIED. Gaming Licence Fees Regulations S.L. 583.03; Gaming Tax Regulations S.L. 583.10, amended by the Gaming Tax (Amendment) Regulations 2026 in force 1 October 2026 (PwC Malta, MGA announcement, MTCA press release — VERIFIED AS TO SUBSTANCE, LN NUMBERS UNVERIFIED). MGA Directive 6 of 2018. Fee figures VERIFIED from the MGA 'Licence Fees and Taxation' guidance note, December 2024 v3. Regulator: Malta Gaming Authority.

  • Member state of identification for VAT OSS — a real choiceCLEAR

    A non-EU seller registering for non-Union OSS picks ONE member state to file through and reports all EU digital sales in a single return. The choice determines your filing language, portal, audit relationship and practical support quality, and is worth deliberating rather than defaulting. No physical presence in the EU is required for the non-Union scheme. Registration fees and lead times: UNVERIFIED. [AUDIT NOTE] The choice does NOT affect whether VAT is due — Art 58 puts the place of supply at the consumer's member state and Art 59c's EUR 10,000 threshold is confined to EU-established sellers, so a non-EU seller owes destination VAT from the first euro regardless of which state it files through.

    Council Directive 2006/112/EC Arts 358a-369k (non-Union scheme); Art 58 place of supply; Art 59c threshold (EU-established sellers only)

  • NetherlandsLICENCE-REQUIRED FOR MODEL B; MODEL C CONTESTED AND THE APPELLATE WEATHER RUNS AGAINST SKILL CLAIMS

    AUDIT CORRECTION — THE NETHERLANDS IS LESS FAVOURABLE THAN THE UNCORRECTED BRIEF IMPLIED. Art. 1(1)(a) Wet op de kansspelen prohibits, without a licence, giving an opportunity to compete for prizes where winners are determined by a chance mechanism on which participants generally cannot exert 'overwegende invloed' (decisive influence). A genuine behendigheidsspel is outside the Act. The uncorrected brief presented this as a live 50/50 by setting the Hoge Raad's 1998 poker ruling against a 2010 Hague District Court decision the other way. THAT 2010 DECISION WAS REVERSED ON APPEAL by the Gerechtshof Den Haag, and Dutch higher courts have consistently maintained that poker is a kansspel under the Wok. The honest framing: the 'overwegende invloed' test has been applied AGAINST skill claims at appellate level, so the Netherlands is a contested market for Model C, not a Camp 1 safe harbour. Wok statutory text was NEVER FETCHED by researcher or auditor (SECONDARY). Current licensing: Wet Kansspelen op afstand; new policy rules BVRKOA 2026 in force 1 January 2026; the September 2021 cohort of licences EXPIRES 1 OCTOBER 2026 — A FUTURE EVENT as at the date of this correction; licences run a maximum of 5 years; applicants must submit an exit plan and a change-notification plan. The KSA's 2026 Toezichtagenda prioritises illegal supply, working through an alliance with payment service providers, social media and hosting companies — a grey-market launch will be found. AUDIT ADDITIONS: the Gedragscode promotionele kansspelen governs promotional games outside the Wok; the Netherlands operates an untargeted-gambling-advertising ban; player-fund segregation is a licence condition. ALL THREE UNVERIFIED AS TO DETAIL. The KSA's 'Leidraad beoordeling kansspelen' could not be retrieved (404), and the KSA's current position on virtual currencies and paid skill games specifically is UNVERIFIED. Fees and lead times UNVERIFIED.

    Wet op de kansspelen Art. 1(1)(a) — SECONDARY (KSA and law-firm restatements; statutory text not fetched by researcher or auditor). Gerechtshof Den Haag reversal of the 2010 Rechtbank 's-Gravenhage decision — per audit; CITATION UNVERIFIED.   • PolandPROHIBITED FOR MODEL B; MODEL C ARGUABLY OUTSIDE GRY HAZARDOWE BUT CRIMINALLY RISKY

  • SpainLICENCE

    SKILL PREDOMINANCE IS NO DEFENCE ONCE A CHANCE ELEMENT IS PRESENT — WHICH IS NARROWER THAN 'NO PURE-SKILL EXEMPTION'. AUDIT CORRECTION: Art. 3(a) Ley 13/2011 defines 'juego' as any activity in which money or economically valuable objects are risked on uncertain future results 'DEPENDIENTES EN ALGUNA MEDIDA DEL AZAR' — dependent TO SOME DEGREE ON CHANCE — permitting transfer between participants, 'con independencia de que predomine en ellos el grado de habilidad, destreza o pericia de los jugadores o sean exclusiva o primordialmente de suerte, envite o azar'. The 'independencia de que predomine la habilidad' clause means skill-predominance is no defence once chance is present; it does NOT capture a contest with no chance element at all. The uncorrected brief's flat 'Spain expressly has NO pure-skill exemption' overstated this. For FACT//DUEL the practical answer is unchanged — the random question draw supplies the chance element — but the reasoning must be stated correctly. SECOND AUDIT CORRECTION: THE GENERAL-LICENCE CATEGORIES (apuestas, rifas, concursos, otros juegos) SIT IN ARTICLE 10, NOT ARTICLE 9. 'Concursos' is defined at Art. 3(e) as offers made through media (TV, radio, internet) requiring an economic outlay with prizes determined by chance, competition or knowledge tests — WHETHER AN APP-BASED DUEL FITS THAT DEFINITION IS UNTESTED and is a specific question for Spanish counsel, which the uncorrected brief never asked. Art. 2.2 excludes only games or competitions 'de puro ocio, pasatiempo o recreo que constituyan usos sociales' with no economically evaluable transfers, and excludes promotional combinations with random elements. A general licence must be held before the matching singular licence can be sought; general licences are awarded through a public convocatoria, not on demand. AUDIT ADDITIONS: Real Decreto 958/2020 severely restricts gambling advertising; player-winnings withholding tax and gambling VAT treatment change prize-pool economics and require payout-layer product changes; player-fund segregation is a licence condition. ALL THREE UNVERIFIED AS TO DETAIL. The DGOJ was consulting on amendments to Ley 13/2011 as at July 2026 (SECONDARY). Fees and lead times UNVERIFIED. VERIFICATION STATUS: PRIMARY TEXT CHECKED (BOE consolidated text).

    Ley 13/2011, de 27 de mayo, de regulacion del juego, Arts. 1, 2, 3, 10 (BOE-A-2011-9280, consolidated text fetched 14 Sep 2026) — ARTICLE 10 CORRECTED FROM ARTICLE 9 PER AUDIT; Art. 3(e) 'concursos' definition added per audit. Real Decreto 1614/2011 on licences, authorisations and registers. Real Decreto 958/2020 (advertising) — UNVERIFIED. Regulator: Direccion General de Ordenacion del Juego (DGOJ).

  • SwedenLICENCE-REQUIRED — REVERSED BY THE AUDIT FROM THE UNCORRECTED BRIEF'S 'OUTSIDE GAMBLING LAW'

    THE MOST CONSEQUENTIAL SUBDIVISION REVERSAL IN THIS CORRECTED EDITION. The uncorrected brief placed Sweden in the 'outside gambling law, no licence' camp in its headline and Model C analysis, on the strength of a Spelinspektionen page about competitions decided by a prestationsmoment — while its own subdivision row simultaneously said 'licence-required'. A founder reading the headline would have launched paid contests in Sweden unlicensed. CORRECTED POSITION: Spellagen defines KOMBINATIONSSPEL as 'spel dar deltagarna har chans att vinna ett pris och dar sannolikheten att vinna beror pa en kombination av skicklighet och slump' — a game where participants have a chance to win a prize and the probability of winning depends on a COMBINATION OF SKILL AND CHANCE — and kombinationsspel is a licensable form of spel, structurally identical to the Danish kombinationsspil the brief already treated as fatal (definition verified against the Spellag text, 14 Sep 2026). The Spelinspektionen carve-out is strict on its own wording: it exempts only competitions where the winner is decided by a performance element 'UTAN INSLAG AV LOTTNING ELLER SLUMP' — WITHOUT any element of drawing lots or chance, not merely 'predominantly skill'. A randomly drawn question set introduces exactly the inslag av slump that defeats it. ON ITS OWN FACTS FACT//DUEL'S PAID DUEL IS A CANDIDATE SWEDISH KOMBINATIONSSPEL REQUIRING A SPELINSPEKTIONEN LICENCE. A Swedish Model C would need an identical fixed question set for every entrant, no random matching, and no chance-introducing timer — and even then a Swedish opinion. Other exemptions at 3 kap. 4-5 §§ Spellagen: FREE-TO-ENTER GAMES (3 kap. 4 § — 'licens kravs inte for spel dar deltagande inte kraver betalning av en insats'), private play between close connections, combination games in periodicals/radio/TV, and municipal non-profit lotteries. Model B requires a Spelinspektionen licence. AUDIT ADDITION: player-fund segregation is a Swedish licence condition — DETAIL UNVERIFIED. An amending act SFS 2025:767 exists and ITS CONTENT IS ENTIRELY UNVERIFIED — it could affect this position. Fees and lead times UNVERIFIED. VERIFICATION STATUS: PRIMARY TEXT CHECKED for the kombinationsspel definition and the no-stake exemption.

    Spellag (2018:1138), in force 1 January 2019 — kombinationsspel definition and 3 kap. 4-5 §§ exemptions, verified against the Spellag text 14 Sep 2026 (riksdagen.se / svenskforfattningssamling.se). Spelforordning (2018:1475). Spelinspektionen guidance page 'Nar kravs det inte licens?' fetched 14 Sep 2026 — NOTE the guidance is narrower than the uncorrected brief read it. SFS 2025:767 — CONTENT UNVERIFIED. Regulator: Spelinspektionen.

  • Sweden — CPC enforcement precedentUNSETTLED

    The CPC Network's coordinated action announced alongside the Key Principles on 21 March 2025 targeted Star Stable Entertainment AB, a Swedish game operator, over direct exhortation to children in advertisements and time-limited pressure techniques in a virtual-currency economy. This is the live example of how the Key Principles are actually enforced — as a coordinated CPC action against a single operator, with commitments sought. Read it as the enforcement template for any coin economy aimed at or reaching minors. Note the enforcement value is independent of whether the Key Principles are binding: a CPC coordinated action is itself the punishment regardless of the eventual legal answer.

    CPC Network coordinated action, announced 21 March 2025 (European Commission news release)

    NEEDS FIRST Frozen product spec — timer, question draw, RAKE STRUCTURE (operator-funded fixed pool vs commission on a player-funded pot), prize structure, currency flows

    THIS DOCUMENT IS A COMPLIANCE CHECKLIST, NOT LEGAL ADVICE. Every Model C verdict turns on a skill-versus-chance assessment that only a local regulator or court can settle, fact-specific to the exact duel format. CORRECTED PRIORITY ORDER: Germany (largest market, best carve-out, and the § 33d GewO question that the uncorrected brief missed entirely — highest downside if wrong), Sweden (the audit reversed this market from 'exempt' to 'licence likely required' — confirm before any Nordic plan), Ireland (new regime, clearest capture of skill, and every Irish date in this document is unverified), Spain and Italy (skill expressly captured; Italy also needs the concession fee confirmed before it is worth an opinion at all), Netherlands (carve-out available but appellate authority runs against skill claims), Austria (reform details entirely unverified), France (confirm the geo-block; do not seek a route in).

Stage 2 — Licences, registrations, notifications 20

  • Choose the MGA licence type: Type 4 (controlled skill game) if the MGA will classify a knowledge duel as one; Type 3 (P2P, operator takes commission) if it will not — AND UNDERSTAND THAT THIS CHOICE IS AN EU-WIDE CHARACTERISATION, NOT A FORM-FILLING DECISION

    ISSUER
    Malta Gaming Authority
    COST
    Fixed annual licence fee: EUR 10,000 if providing SOLELY Type 4 services; EUR 25,000 for a B2C Gaming Service Licence otherwise (incl. Type 3). Minimum share capital: Type 4 EUR 40,000, Type 3 EUR 40,000, Types 1-2 EUR 100,000
    LEAD TIME
    UNVERIFIED
    APPLIES TO
    B, C

    NEEDS FIRST MGA application accepted; game design documentation demonstrating that skill determines the outcome alone or predominantly

    AUDIT CORRECTION — THE UNCORRECTED BRIEF RECOMMENDED TYPE 3 AS THE 'LIKELIER FIT' WITHOUT FLAGGING THE CONSEQUENCE. Type 3 is, on the MGA's own definition, a class of GAMES OF CHANCE ('games of chance not played against the house and wherein the operator is not exposed to gaming risk'). Applying for Type 3 concedes that the product is a game of chance — which destroys the skill-predominance argument in Germany and Austria, weakens it in the Netherlands, and strengthens capture in Denmark, Spain, Ireland, Sweden and Italy. Type 3 and Type 4 are NOT interchangeable fallbacks. If the EU strategy depends on Model C being outside gambling law anywhere, do not file for Type 3. Type 4 = controlled skill games. CRITICAL GAP (unchanged by the audit): under Directive 6 of 2018 (Controlled Skill Games Ruling, 1 August 2018) FANTASY SPORTS IS THE ONLY GAME THE MGA HAS CLASSIFIED as a controlled skill game; a trivia duel needs a fresh MGA determination, so budget for a pre-application ruling request. The burden of proving skill predominance is on the applicant. The reg. 8 Gaming Authorisations Regulations cross-reference could not be verified against primary text by either the researcher or the auditor; the auditor notes the Gaming Authorisations Regulations are S.L. 583.05 — REGULATION NUMBER UNVERIFIED.

    ISSUER

  • DO NOT run Model C in Germany unlicensed on the skill-predominance analysis alone — get a German opinion that covers BOTH § 3(1) GlueStV AND § 33d GewO

    ISSUER
    Gemeinsame Gluecksspielbehoerde der Laender (GGL), Halle (Saale), for GlueStV permissions; the competent Land/municipal trade authority (Gewerbebehoerde) for a § 33d GewO Erlaubnis
    COST
    UNVERIFIED for both the GGL permission and the § 33d GewO Erlaubnis

    UNVERIFIED
    APPLIES TO
    B, C

    NEEDS FIRST German legal opinion on (a) whether chance predominates in your specific duel format and (b) whether § 33d GewO applies to a purely online offering

    THIS ITEM IS THE MOST CHANGED IN THE DOCUMENT. The uncorrected brief's advice was 'run Model C in Germany unlicensed'. That advice is WITHDRAWN. CORRECTED PICTURE: (1) GlueStV 2021 permission classes are NOT limited to sports betting (§ 21), online poker (§ 22b), virtual slot machines (§ 22a), lotteries and horse betting — the GGL's own legal-basis page states GlueStV 2021 made licensable virtuelle Automatenspiele, Online-Poker UND ONLINECASINOSPIELE, and online casino games are authorised by the individual LAENDER under their own concession procedures, not by the GGL. There is still no class that fits a P2P knowledge stake, so Model B cannot be licensed in its current shape, but the word 'unlicensable' was overstated — restructuring into a licensable class is not categorically foreclosed. (2) Model C that escapes § 3(1) GlueStV does NOT thereby escape German permission requirements: § 33d GewO requires an Erlaubnis for the commercial operation of 'andere Spiele mit Gewinnmoeglichkeit' — other games with a chance to win that are not Gluecksspiel — with a Bundeskriminalamt Unbedenklichkeitsbescheinigung as a precondition and refusal mandated where the applicant lacks reliability (provision verified at gesetze-im-internet.de, 14 Sep 2026). ITS APPLICATION TO PURELY ONLINE OFFERINGS IS CONTESTED and is the exact question the German opinion must answer. (3) GET THE OPINION ON THE 5/7/10-SECOND TIMER AND THE RANDOM QUESTION DRAW before relying on the skill analysis at all. Statutory evaluation of GlueStV 2021 is due by 31 December 2026 under § 32 (section number SECONDARY); a successor treaty would not take effect before 2027-2028 (SECONDARY, trade press). No 2025 or 2026 amendment to GlueStV 2021 appears on the GGL's legal-basis page.

  • Apply for a Spanish DGOJ general licence in the 'concursos' (or 'otros juegos') category plus the matching singular licence

    ISSUER
    Direccion General de Ordenacion del Juego (DGOJ), Spain
    COST
    UNVERIFIED
    LEAD TIME
    UNVERIFIED — general licences are granted through a public convocatoria, not on demand; timing depends on the DGOJ opening a round
    APPLIES TO
    B, C

    NEEDS FIRST General licence must be held before the corresponding singular licence can be requested

    CORRECTED ON TWO POINTS. (1) The uncorrected brief said 'Spain expressly has NO pure-skill exemption'. That overstates Art. 3(a) Ley 13/2011, which requires the uncertain future results to be 'dependientes EN ALGUNA MEDIDA DEL AZAR' — dependent to some degree on chance. The 'con independencia de que predomine... el grado de habilidad' clause means skill-predominance is no defence ONCE A CHANCE ELEMENT IS PRESENT; it does not capture a contest with no chance element at all. For FACT//DUEL the practical answer is the same (random question draw supplies the chance element) but the reasoning must be stated correctly. (2) THE GENERAL-LICENCE CATEGORIES (apuestas, rifas, concursos, otros juegos) SIT IN ARTICLE 10, NOT ARTICLE 9 — the uncorrected brief's authority line was wrong. 'Concursos' is defined at Art. 3(e) as offers made through media (TV/radio/internet) requiring an economic outlay with prizes determined by chance, competition or knowledge tests; WHETHER AN APP-BASED DUEL FITS THAT DEFINITION IS UNTESTED AND UNVERIFIED and is a specific question for Spanish counsel. Art. 2.2 excludes only games of puro ocio, pasatiempo o recreo constituting usos sociales with no economically evaluable transfers. AUDIT ADDITION: Spain's Real Decreto 958/2020 imposes severe gambling advertising restrictions, and player-winnings withholding tax and gambling VAT treatment change prize-pool economics — both UNVERIFIED as to detail and both capable of making a licensed Spanish product unmarketable. The DGOJ was consulting on Ley 13/2011 amendments as at July 2026 — SECONDARY.

    CORRECTED. Wok Art. 1(1)(a): gambling exists where winners are determined by a chance mechanism on which participants cannot generally exert decisive influence ('overwegende invloed'); a genuine behendigheidsspel falls outside. The uncorrected brief presented this as a live 50/50 by citing a 2010 Hague District Court decision against the 1998 Hoge Raad poker ruling. AUDIT CORRECTION: the 2010 decision was REVERSED ON APPEAL by the Gerechtshof Den Haag and Dutch higher courts have consistently held poker to be a kansspel.   • Apply for a Danish Spillemyndigheden licence for any paid contest with Danish players

    ISSUER
    Spillemyndigheden, Denmark
    COST
    UNVERIFIED
    LEAD TIME
    UNVERIFIED
