    NEEDS FIRST None identified

    Spilleloven § 5 defines 'kombinationsspil' as activities where the chance of winning depends on 'en kombination af faerdighed og tilfaeldighed' — skill AND chance combined — and these require a licence. Only games decided purely by skill fall outside the Act. A trivia duel with randomly drawn questions is a textbook kombinationsspil. Spillemyndigheden's public guidance: charge for participation and you need permission; make it free and you do not. Danish statutory text was fetched from danskelove.dk. NOTE THE READ-ACROSS THE UNCORRECTED BRIEF MISSED: Sweden has the structurally identical kombinationsspel concept, which is why Sweden has been moved out of the 'no licence' camp in this corrected edition.

  • Apply for a Spelinspektionen licence for any paid contest with Swedish players — SWEDEN IS A LICENCE MARKET FOR THIS PRODUCT, NOT AN EXEMPT ONE

    ISSUER
    Spelinspektionen, Sweden
    COST
    UNVERIFIED
    LEAD TIME
    UNVERIFIED
    APPLIES TO
    B, C

    NEEDS FIRST Swedish legal opinion on whether the duel is a kombinationsspel

    AUDIT-DRIVEN REVERSAL — THIS ITEM REPLACES THE UNCORRECTED BRIEF'S PLACEMENT OF SWEDEN OUTSIDE GAMBLING LAW. Spellagen defines kombinationsspel as 'spel dar deltagarna har chans att vinna ett pris och dar sannolikheten att vinna beror pa en kombination av skicklighet och slump' — a game where participants have a chance to win a prize and the probability of winning depends on a COMBINATION OF SKILL AND CHANCE — and kombinationsspel is a licensable form of spel (definition verified against the Spellag text, 14 Sep 2026). The Spelinspektionen guidance the uncorrected brief relied on exempts only competitions decided by a prestationsmoment 'utan inslag av lottning eller slump' — WITHOUT any element of drawing lots or chance. A randomly drawn question set is exactly that element. A Swedish Model C would therefore need either a Spelinspektionen licence or a format with an identical fixed question set for every entrant, no random matching and no chance-introducing timer, and even then a Swedish opinion. Other exemptions at 3 kap. 4-5 §§ Spellagen: free-to-enter games (no stake), private play between close connections, combination games in periodicals/radio/TV, and municipal non-profit lotteries. An amending act SFS 2025:767 exists and ITS CONTENT IS UNVERIFIED — it could affect this position.

  • Build to the AMLR (Regulation (EU) 2024/1624) now — it applies directly from 10 July 2027 and gives the first EU-wide definition of a gambling service

    ISSUER
    National AML supervisors; Anti-Money Laundering Authority (AMLA), Frankfurt
    COST
    UNVERIFIED (compliance programme cost)
    LEAD TIME
    Applies from 10 July 2027 (Art. 90)
    APPLIES TO
    B, C (and Model A only if 'entries' carry a prize of value)

    NEEDS FIRST Identity/KYC stack, transaction monitoring, attribution of transactions to a customer before identity verification

    CORRECTED ON THREE POINTS, TWO OF THEM CITATION ERRORS IN AN ITEM THE UNCORRECTED BRIEF LABELLED 'VERIFIED PRIMARY TEXT'. (1) THE CDD PROVISION IS ARTICLE 19(5), NOT ARTICLE 20(5). Art. 19 ('Application of customer due diligence measures'), para. 5: 'providers of gambling services shall apply customer due diligence measures upon the collection of winnings, the wagering of a stake, or both, when carrying out transactions amounting to at least EUR 2 000'. Article 20 is the article listing the CDD MEASURES themselves; Art. 19(5) cross-refers to 'Article 20(1), point (a)'. Recital 64 confirms the threshold can be met by several linked smaller transactions. (2) THE ART. 4 EXEMPTION IS NOT THE CLOSED DOOR THE BRIEF DESCRIBED. The brief truncated Art. 4(1) mid-sentence. The text continues after 'online gambling services or sport betting services' with: 'other than: (i) online gambling services operated by the State... (ii) online gambling services the organisation, operation and administration of which is regulated by the State', and recital 63 confirms the carve-back. An operator inside a state-regulated online regime is NOT automatically outside the exemption. Art. 4 exemptions also require a Member State risk assessment and (per recital 63) a Commission approval/rejection step. The flat statement 'FACT//DUEL under Model B or C cannot be exempted' is WITHDRAWN. (3) AMLR CAPTURE OF MODEL C IS NOT SETTLED. Art. 2(1)(12) defines a gambling service as one 'which involves wagering a stake with monetary value in GAMES OF CHANCE, including those with an element of skill'. The chance limb is still required; 'including those with an element of skill' extends the definition to mixed games, it does not abolish it for skill-predominant ones. A Model C contest with genuinely no chance element is ARGUABLY NOT an AMLR gambling service — and the brief cannot simultaneously argue that Model C is chance-free enough to escape § 3(1) GlueStV and that it is an AMLR game of chance. CONTESTED; resolve it with counsel, and note that the safe engineering assumption is still to build the KYC and transaction-attribution stack, because retrofitting it is far more expensive than building it. Art. 3, point (3)(g) makes providers of gambling services obliged entities.

  • Register for VAT non-Union OSS (or Union OSS if you incorporate in the EU) BEFORE the first EU coin/subscription sale

    ISSUER
    Tax authority of one chosen EU member state of identification (e.g. Irish Revenue, Dutch Belastingdienst); framework: Council Directive 2006/112/EC Arts 358a-369k
    COST
    No EU registration fee known; UNVERIFIED. Compliance-provider cost UNVERIFIED
    LEAD TIME
    UNVERIFIED
    APPLIES TO
    A, B, C, D

    NEEDS FIRST Legal entity; decide member state of identification

    CRITICAL AND ROUTINELY MISSED. [CORRECTED] The obligation follows from the VAT Directive itself, not from case law: Art 58 puts the place of supply of an electronically supplied service at the consumer's member state, Art 73 makes the taxable amount the full consideration, and the EUR 10,000 micro-threshold in Art 59c applies ONLY to EU-ESTABLISHED sellers — so a non-EU seller owes VAT from the first euro. CJEU C-472/24 MB 'Zaidimu valiuta' (5 March 2026) is supporting, not load-bearing, and is narrower than the original brief said: the taxable person was a THIRD-PARTY TRADER reselling RuneScape gold on a secondary market, not a publisher issuing its own currency. The Court held that exchanging real currency for units usable only inside an online game is not exempt under Art 135(1)(e) and is not a voucher under Art 30a, so Art 73 general rules apply. Extending that to a first-party issuer's own coin sale is a reasonable inference, NOT a holding; the single-purpose voucher analysis under Art 30a(2) for a first-party issuer was not before the Court. Take the conservative position (VAT at the coin sale, on full consideration) and document it as an inference. Displayed coin prices must be VAT-inclusive for consumers and the rate varies by member state — see the geo-blocking checklist item before varying price per country.

  • Designate a DSA Article 13 legal representative and notify the Digital Services Coordinator

    ISSUER
    Digital Services Coordinator of the member state of designation; framework: Regulation (EU) 2022/2065 (DSA) Art 13 — DSA fully applicable 17 Feb 2024
    COST
    UNVERIFIED
    LEAD TIME
    UNVERIFIED
    APPLIES TO
    A, B, C, D — only if you are an 'intermediary service' (see notes)

    NEEDS FIRST Scoping decision on whether you are a hosting service

    SCOPE FIRST, THEN APPOINT. Today FACT//DUEL is almost certainly outside the DSA: all state is local, there is no storage of user-provided information. The moment you add accounts you will store player-chosen display names, duel room codes and any chat — that is 'storage of information provided by a recipient', i.e. a HOSTING service under DSA Art 3(g)(iii), which triggers Arts 11, 12, 14 and 16 including the Art 13 representative (who can be held personally liable for your non-compliance). You are very likely NOT an 'online platform' under Art 3(i), because a 1v1 duel is not 'dissemination to the public' — interpersonal 1:1 communication is expressly outside. Consequence: the Section 3 duties (Arts 20-28, including Art 28 minor protection) do not apply, and Art 19 exempts micro/small enterprises from Section 3 anyway. If you ever add public leaderboards with user-authored text, global chat, or user-generated question packs, you become an online platform and this analysis flips.

  • If any payment instrument you issue is used within a limited network, notify at the EUR 1 million threshold

    ISSUER
    National competent authority; PSD2 Art 37(2); EBA Guidelines EBA/GL/2022/02 on the limited network exclusion, applicable 1 June 2022
    COST
    No fee identified; UNVERIFIED
    LEAD TIME
    UNVERIFIED
    APPLIES TO
    A, B, C

    NEEDS FIRST Transaction volume monitoring instrumented in the coin ledger

    If you rely on the PSD2 Art 3(k) limited-network exclusion, Art 37(2) requires a notification to your competent authority when the total value of payment transactions over any period shorter than 12 months exceeds EUR 1,000,000. The EBA guidelines say the notification is filed once, with a fresh one if the information changes substantially. Instrument your coin ledger to measure this from day one — a threshold you cannot measure is a threshold you will breach silently.

    LEAD TIME
    UNVERIFIED
    APPLIES TO
    C primarily; B unlikely to qualify

    NEEDS FIRST Per-market characterisation opinion; documented risk assessment of your product

    [AUDIT ADDITION — the original brief said the AMLR ends forum-shopping of the definition, which OVERSTATES uniformity]. AMLD4 Art 2(2) expressly permits member states, OTHER THAN FOR CASINOS and following an appropriate risk assessment, to 'exempt, in full or in part, providers of certain gambling services' from AML obligations on the basis of proven low risk, with notification to the Commission; the AML package preserves an equivalent option. Consequence: whether a given paid-entry skill contest is an AML obliged entity remains a PER-MARKET answer both today and after 10 July 2027. This is the most plausible escape hatch for Model C and the original brief omitted it entirely. Action: for each launch market, establish (a) has the state exercised the Art 2(2) exemption, (b) for which categories of gambling service, (c) does a fixed-prize paid-entry trivia contest fall within the exempted category, (d) what risk assessment or notification is needed from you. Do not assume portability between states.

  • Document a DAC7 scoping conclusion (likely out of scope) and keep it on file

    ISSUER
    Council Directive (EU) 2021/514 (DAC7), amending Directive 2011/16/EU; reporting to the tax authority of the member state of registration
    COST
    n/a if out of scope
    LEAD TIME
    Days
    APPLIES TO
    A, B, C, D — assess, probably not report

    NEEDS FIRST Final product spec

    DAC7's four 'Relevant Activities' are exhaustive: rental of immovable property, personal services, sale of (tangible) goods, and rental of any mode of transport. Prize money and gaming winnings are not among them, and players in a duel are not 'Sellers' providing a personal service to a customer for consideration. So on the current design, none of Models A-D triggers DAC7 reporting. WRITE THE CONCLUSION DOWN. It would change if you ever add a creator marketplace (user-authored question packs sold to other players), paid coaching, or any revenue-share payout to individual content providers — at which point you become a reporting platform operator with annual reporting. Confirm with a tax adviser against your chosen member state's implementation; there is no Commission guidance specifically addressing gaming prizes. Annex V Section I(A)(8) not read directly — UNVERIFIED.

  • [AUDIT ADDITION] Take a documented position on the VAT gambling exemption and on the taxable amount for any wagering leg

    ISSUER
    Council Directive 2006/112/EC Art 135(1)(i); CJEU C-38/93 Glawe and CJEU C-377/11 International Bingo Technology; per-member-state conditions and limitations
    COST
    UNVERIFIED (tax adviser)
    LEAD TIME
    UNVERIFIED
    APPLIES TO
    B, C (and A only if any staking is added)

    NEEDS FIRST Per-market gambling characterisation opinion

    [AUDIT ADDITION — the original brief tested only Art 135(1)(e) and wrongly called Model C's VAT treatment 'settled']. Art 135(1)(i) exempts 'betting, lotteries and other forms of gambling, subject to the conditions and limitations laid down by each Member State'. If a member state characterises your paid-entry timed duel or your staked coin play as gambling, the entry fee or stake may be VAT-EXEMPT in that state and outside your OSS return — with knock-on input-VAT recovery consequences and a different cash-flow model. You cannot call the gambling characterisation unsettled for AML and settled for VAT: the same characterisation drives both. SEPARATELY, the Glawe line (C-38/93; C-377/11 International Bingo Technology) can make the taxable amount for a wagering supply the OPERATOR'S RETAINED MARGIN rather than gross stakes — a materially different VAT base for any Model B/C money-play variant. Both points must be settled per market with a tax adviser, and settled BEFORE you model revenue.

  • Accept and design around the fact that there is no EU gambling licence and no mutual recognition — budget for per-member-state licensing, not one EU authorisation

    European Commission (DG GROW) / CJEU
    COST
    n/a (structural decision)

    APPLIES TO
    B, C

    The Commission's online gambling page states that there is no sector-specific EU legislation in the field of gambling services and that EU countries are autonomous in how they organise gambling services. CJEU Case C-440/23 — AUDIT FLAGGED THIS AS POSSIBLY FABRICATED; THE EDITOR CHECKED AND THE CASE IS REAL. It is European Lotto and Betting Ltd and Deutsche Lotto- und Sportwetten Ltd, judgment of 16 April 2026, following AG Emiliou's opinion of 4 September 2025 (CELEX 62023CC0440). Holdings as reported across GTG Advocates, Gecic Law, Mondaq, BSP and AGB: EU law does not preclude a member state prohibiting online gambling services authorised in another member state; member states may attach civil-law consequences including nullity of contracts and restitution of lost stakes; a player's restitution claim is not an abuse of EU rights. The underlying facts were Malta-licensed operators serving German players between June 2019 and July 2021. The judgment text itself was not fetched from curia.europa.eu — the holdings are corroborated across multiple independent practitioner sources but remain SECONDARY as to exact wording.

  • Decide the launch shape FIRST: Model A/D (no gambling licence anywhere) vs Model C (split 27 ways, and not where you thought) vs Model B (do not attempt in the EU)

    Recommended EU sequencing: ship Model A + Model D EU-wide now with zero gambling licensing (subject to the Model A conditions — no randomised paid rewards anywhere, Belgium especially); treat Model C as a licensed-market product state by state; drop Model B for the EU. CORRECTED: the sweepstakes structure fails in the EU not because 'no consideration gateway exists' (several states DO exempt genuinely free-to-enter games — Spellagen 3 kap. 4 § is explicit) but because no member state accepts a DUAL paid-plus-free entry route as cleansing the paid route, and France defeats it in terms via L320-1 CSI. A genuinely free-only product is a different and possibly viable design; a paid product with a free side door is not.

  • Geo-block France for any paid-entry contest or staking product, and geo-block it hard (IP + payment BIN + device)

    ISSUER
    ANJ (Autorite Nationale des Jeux)
    COST
    engineering cost only
    LEAD TIME
    n/a
    APPLIES TO
    B, C

    NEEDS FIRST Server-authoritative geolocation at account and transaction level

    CORRECTED RATIONALE. L320-1 CSI catches operations offered to the public to create the hope of a gain due EVEN PARTIALLY to chance where a financial sacrifice is required, and adds that the prohibition covers games whose operation rests on the players' savoir-faire. France therefore catches any paid contest with ANY chance element — it does NOT prohibit skill contests as a class (paid chess, bridge and quiz competitions continue lawfully). The geo-block conclusion is unchanged for FACT//DUEL because randomly drawn questions and reflex timers supply the chance element, but the reason matters: a fully deterministic, fixed-question, no-timer format is not automatically illegal in France and would need French counsel rather than a blanket assumption. The video-game competition carve-out at Arts. L321-8 to L321-11 CSI requires PHYSICAL PRESENCE of participants. ANJ licences exist only for sports betting, horse betting and online poker. L320-1 WORDING WAS NEVER FETCHED FROM LEGIFRANCE (403) BY EITHER THE RESEARCHER OR THE AUDITOR — confirm the consolidated wording with French counsel.

  • Run the Model C skill-predominance test as a DESIGN requirement, not a legal afterthought — lengthen timers, enlarge the question set, fix the prize pool, and log the evidence

    ISSUER
    Self-imposed (evidence for DE/AT/NL regulators, a Swedish or Danish licence application, and an MGA Type 4 determination)
    COST
    engineering cost only
    LEAD TIME
    Before any paid launch
    APPLIES TO
    C

    The variables that decide whether chance predominates: (1) timer length — 5 seconds converts knowledge into reflex and randomness; (2) question randomisation — a random draw per duel injects chance the German, Austrian, Dutch, Swedish and Danish tests all weigh; (3) sample size — one duel over a handful of questions has high variance, a 50-question fixed set does not; (4) opponent matching — random matching adds chance, skill-banded matching removes it; (5) an identical question set for all entrants in an expedition removes draw-luck entirely and is a hard requirement for any Swedish or French argument. Keep per-player telemetry showing that higher-knowledge players win at a rate materially above chance; in Malta the burden of proof is expressly on the applicant.

  • DECIDE THE RAKE STRUCTURE BEFORE ANY LEGAL OPINION IS COMMISSIONED — operator-funded fixed prize pool vs commission on a player-funded pot

    ISSUER
    Founder / product (AUDIT ADDITION — absent from the uncorrected brief)
    COST
    n/a (structural decision, affects unit economics)
    LEAD TIME
    Before local counsel is instructed
    APPLIES TO
    B, C

    AUDIT ADDITION, AND A BIGGER LEVER THAN THE TIMER. The uncorrected brief never analysed whether FACT//DUEL takes a commission from a player-funded pot or pays a fixed announced prize pool from its own funds. Taking a rake from a player-funded pot is what makes the product a Type 3 P2P game in Malta and a 'stake' under most EU definitions. Paying a fixed, announced, operator-funded prize pool that does not depend on the number of entrants — which is exactly what Model C is defined as — removes or weakens the stake limb on the player side in several states and is the difference between licensable and out-of-scope in Germany, Austria and the Netherlands. Model C as defined in this project (fixed prize pool announced in advance, funded by the operator, NOT dependent on entrant numbers) is the favourable structure; any drift toward pot-splitting converts it into Model B. Note that the entry FEE may still constitute a stake in states whose test is 'is an Entgelt demanded for a Gewinnchance' (Germany) regardless of who funds the pool — CONTESTED and a question for each local opinion.

  • Comply with the CPC Network in-game virtual currency principles and prepare for the Digital Fairness Act

Q4 2026 IS RETAINED. As at 14 September 2026 the proposal has therefore NOT yet been tabled; the reported scope (dark patterns, addictive design, loot boxes, virtual currencies, influencer marketing, personalised pricing) and the reported possibility of an outright loot-box ban or a parental-consent requirement for minors are SECONDARY and pre-legislative.

  • Do NOT rely on an MGA licence to reach other EU markets — it does not passport, and C-440/23 now makes the downside a restitution liability

    ISSUER

    n/a

    There is no mutual recognition of gaming licences in EU law. Malta's Bill 55 (2023), inserting Art. 56A into the Gaming Act, requires Maltese courts to refuse recognition of foreign judgments against MGA licensees; the Commission issued a letter of formal notice on Art. 56A. CJEU Case C-440/23 (European Lotto and Betting Ltd and Deutsche Lotto- und Sportwetten Ltd), judgment 16 April 2026, held that EU law does not preclude a member state prohibiting online gambling services authorised in another member state, that member states may attach civil-law consequences including contract nullity and restitution of lost stakes, and that a player's restitution claim is not an abuse of EU rights. THE AUDIT FLAGGED THIS CASE AS POSSIBLY FABRICATED; THE EDITOR VERIFIED IT EXISTS AND THE HOLDINGS ARE AS DESCRIBED, corroborated across GTG Advocates, Gecic Law, Mondaq, BSP and AGB. The judgment text was NOT fetched from curia.europa.eu, so exact wording remains SECONDARY. Bill 55 does not shield you from a German, Dutch or Austrian claim.   • Show the real-money price alongside every coin price, at the undiscounted rate

    ISSUER
    CPC Network (national consumer authorities coordinated by the European Commission), Key Principles on In-game Virtual Currencies, 21 March 2025 — Principles 1 and 2 — SOFT LAW
    COST
    n/a
    LEAD TIME
    Weeks (UI work)
    APPLIES TO
    A, B, C

    NEEDS FIRST Coin-to-euro reference rate per member state

    [RE-LABELLED — ENFORCEMENT POSTURE, NOT A STATUTORY OBLIGATION]. Legal basis the CPC Network cites: UCPD Arts 6(1)(d) and 7, CRD Art 6(1)(e). The underlying misleading-omission analysis is real law; the specific prescription is the Network's reading of it, and the Key Principles expressly state they do not bind national authorities or the Commission and that non-compliance is not necessarily unlawful. Video Games Europe and EGDF have publicly disputed parts of the document. Build to it anyway — it is the declared posture of the authorities who would open the file, and a live CPC coordinated action against Star Stable Entertainment AB was announced the same day. The stated rule: the price must be the objective cost of buying the required coins IN FULL, without quantity discounts or promos, regardless of how the player actually obtained the coins. Principle 2 also says do not run several stacked currencies or force multi-step currency exchanges. Your current 0/10/25/50/100 stake ladder and Expedition POINTS tier are fine today, but the instant coins are purchasable you must not let points and coins interconvert in a way that obscures the euro cost.

  • Let players buy the exact amount of coins they need; never force an over-purchase bundle

    ISSUER
    CPC Network Key Principles, Principle 3 (legal basis asserted: UCPD Arts 5, 8, 9) — SOFT LAW
    COST
    n/a
    LEAD TIME
    Weeks
    APPLIES TO
    A, B, C

    NEEDS FIRST Coin shop design

    [RE-LABELLED — ENFORCEMENT POSTURE]. The two named practices to avoid are (i) offering coins only in bundles that mismatch the price of purchasable content, and (ii) denying the player the ability to choose the amount. This is the single most common design pattern in the market and the CPC Network has singled it out as likely to distort economic behaviour. Design your shop with an arbitrary-amount option from day one — retrofitting it after launch is far more expensive than building it.

  • Build a 14-day withdrawal and refund flow, and decide your position on UNSPENT coins

    ISSUER
    Directive 2011/83/EU Arts 9-16 (STATUTORY); CPC Network Key Principles, Principle 5 (SOFT LAW, contested)
    COST
    n/a
    LEAD TIME
    Weeks
    APPLIES TO
    A, B, C, D

    NEEDS FIRST Coin ledger that distinguishes spent from unspent balance

    [SPLIT INTO HARD AND SOFT]. HARD LAW: CRD Art 9 gives a 14-day withdrawal right on a distance contract, and CRD Art 16(1)(m) lets you extinguish it for digital content only if you run consent + acknowledgement of loss of the right in a click SEPARATE from the buy button and send an Art 8(7) durable-medium confirmation. SOFT AND CONTESTED: the CPC Network's further position is that in-game virtual currency is NOT itself digital content, so the Art 16(1)(m) waiver cannot cover the coin purchase and unspent coins must be cash-refundable within 14 days. Named practices to avoid: 'exempting the purchase of in-game virtual currency, which is not digital content, from the right of withdrawal' and 'denying consumers' right to withdraw... for any in-game virtual currency that remains unused'. This premise is a CPC legal theory that Video Games Europe/EGDF dispute and on which GERMAN COURTS HAVE GONE THE OTHER WAY, upholding properly-constructed waivers for virtual currency. Practical build: track FIFO coin lots with purchase timestamps and refund the unspent remainder of any lot younger than 14 days. Cheaper than litigating it. Do not record it as settled law.

  • Rewrite terms of service to remove unfair terms flagged by the CPC Network, and to comply with DCD Art 19 on modifications

    ISSUER
    CPC Network Key Principles, Principle 6 (asserted basis: UCTD Directive 93/13/EEC Art 3(1) and (3)) — SOFT LAW; PLUS Directive (EU) 2019/770 (DCD) Art 19 — HARD LAW [AUDIT ADDITION]
    COST
    UNVERIFIED (counsel time)
    LEAD TIME
    UNVERIFIED
    APPLIES TO
    A, B, C, D

    NEEDS FIRST Draft ToS

    [CORRECTED — the binding constraint on post-launch coin rebalancing is DCD Art 19, which the original brief never cited]. DCD Art 19 governs when a trader may MODIFY digital content or a digital service during a period of continuous supply: the contract must allow it, there must be a valid reason, it must be at no additional cost, the consumer must be informed in advance on a durable medium, and where the modification adversely affects access to or use of the content the consumer gets a FREE TERMINATION RIGHT (with DCD Arts 16-18 reimbursement). DCD Arts 6-8 set conformity and Arts 14-18 the remedies; the 'legal guarantee of conformity' that CPC Principle 4 requires you to disclose pre-contractually comes from this directive. SEPARATELY, CPC Principle 6 names as terms to avoid: unilateral rights to cease/modify/withdraw purchased content or purchased virtual currency; terms denying or limiting statutory rights; unilateral removal of purchasable features players expected to persist; A UNILATERAL RIGHT TO CHANGE THE VALUE OF THE VIRTUAL CURRENCY; and banning/suspending accounts or confiscating purchased content with no way to contest. Design the economy knowing you cannot silently devalue coins people paid for. Also: DELETE any EU ODR-platform link from your template terms — Regulation (EU) No 524/2013 was repealed by Regulation (EU) 2024/3228 effective 20 July 2025 and the platform is switched off. ADR disclosure duties under Directive 2013/11/EU survive.

  • Turn real-money spending OFF by default and ship functional parental controls

    ISSUER
    CPC Network Key Principles, Principle 7 (asserted basis: UCPD Arts 5-8 and Annex I point 28) — SOFT LAW, but Annex I point 28 is hard law
    COST

    LEAD TIME

    APPLIES TO
    A, B, C, D

    NEEDS FIRST Account system

    The CPC Network's explicit action point: 'by default disabling the possibility of spending real-world money in the video game if the video game is not exclusively limited to an adult audience'. It also says ANY game not exclusively intended for adults should expect a significant under-18 player base, and that age gates do NOT discharge your responsibility. Two stings that bite this product hard: UCPD Annex I point 28 is a BLACKLISTED practice in the directive itself (direct exhortations to children to buy) and needs no CPC theory to be unlawful; and the guidance treats high-spending 'whales' as themselves a VULNERABLE group likely to struggle with impulse control or gambling disorders, so a business model built on whale extraction is assessed against a stricter fairness threshold. FACT//DUEL's juice/streak/escrow loop is precisely the design vocabulary this principle is aimed at.

  • Choose and document an age-assurance method proportionate to the model

    ISSUER
    EDPB Statement 1/2025 on Age Assurance (adopted Feb 2025); European Commission age-verification blueprint / 'mini-wallet'; Commission Guidelines on the protection of minors under DSA Art 28 (July 2025)
    COST
    UNVERIFIED
    LEAD TIME
    UNVERIFIED
    APPLIES TO
    A, B, C, D — proportionate to risk; strictest for B and C

    NEEDS FIRST DPIA; decision on whether you are an 'online platform' under DSA Art 3(i)

    [CORRECTED STATUS OF THE BLUEPRINT]. Version 1 of the Commission's age-verification blueprint was published 14 July 2025 and the five pilot member states (Denmark, France, Greece, Italy, Spain) are confirmed. The original brief's claim that the Commission 'declared the blueprint feature-ready on 15 April 2026' is UNTRACEABLE and has been REMOVED. What is documented: the Commission released an ENHANCED SECOND VERSION on 10 October 2025, describing the blueprint as 'now being thoroughly pilot tested' and saying 'the first customised apps are expected to be published early 2026'; no 'feature-ready' declaration exists in any reachable source. Current readiness as at Sept 2026: UNVERIFIED. Substance unchanged: the EDPB's ten principles require the LEAST intrusive effective method against five criteria — accuracy, reliability, robustness, non-intrusiveness, non-discrimination — and distinguish self-declaration, age estimation and age verification. Self-declaration is defensible for Model A/D; it is not defensible for Model B and is doubtful for Model C. Integrating against the EUDI Wallet specs is the strategically correct bet, but adopting the app is not itself a compliance discharge. Separately, GDPR Art 8 sets the age of consent for information-society services at a default of 16 with member states free to lower it to 13 — the parental-consent age literally changes at the border (see subdivisions).

  • Obtain a regulatory perimeter opinion on whether holding player balances makes you a payment institution or an EMI

    ISSUER
    National competent authority (e.g. Central Bank of Ireland, BaFin, DNB, CSSF, MFSA) — via counsel
    COST
    UNVERIFIED
    LEAD TIME
    UNVERIFIED
    APPLIES TO
    B mandatory; C strongly recommended; A only if you ever add coin transferability or third-party acceptance

    NEEDS FIRST Final product spec including the exact money-in and money-out flows

    THE DECISION POINT OF THIS WHOLE ANALYSIS. [CORRECTED TEST]. Two limbs of EMD2 Art 2(2) must BOTH be satisfied: (i) electronically stored monetary value representing a claim on the issuer, issued on receipt of funds for the purpose of making payment transactions, AND (ii) accepted by a natural or legal person OTHER THAN the issuer. EBA Q&A 2022_6336 (Final, 17/01/2025, citing CJEU C-661/22) says limb (ii) requires 'the transferability and voluntary acceptance of electronic money as a separate monetary asset, and not simply as the reception by the payee of funds resulting from redeemed e-money'. CONSEQUENCE: coins that only you accept, that no one else accepts, and that players cannot transfer are NOT e-money — even if you redeem them yourself. Redeemability is evidence of a payment purpose under limb (i), not a standalone trigger; Art 11 redemption at par is an obligation that follows from already being an issuer. What genuinely engages limb (ii): player-to-player staking or gifting, transferable balances, acceptance by any third-party merchant, a priceable grey market. For the money-OUT leg, CJEU C-51/25 Betaal Garant Nederland (16 July 2026) helps: fund transfers made only to deliver a primary non-payment service are not themselves a payment service, and a credit transfer requires the intermediary to hold the payer's payment account. But the Court expressly did not address MONEY REMITTANCE (PSD2 Art 4(22), Annex I point 6), practitioners call the ruling fact-specific, and two further CJEU references are pending. Do not lean on Betaal Garant as a licence substitute. Instruct counsel on the PSD3 transition too: an EMI licence granted in 2027 requires re-application within 24-30 months.

  • Stand up AML/CFT capability NOW under national AMLD4/AMLD5 law if you pursue Model B or C — then re-paper to the AMLR by 10 July 2027

    ISSUER
    National AML supervisors and FIUs under national transpositions of Directive (EU) 2015/849 as amended by Directive (EU) 2018/843 — IN FORCE SINCE 26 June 2017; from 10 July 2027, Regulation (EU) 2024/1624 (AMLR) applies directly, supervised by national supervisors and the new Anti-Money Laundering Authority (AMLA)
    COST
    UNVERIFIED
    LEAD TIME
    UNVERIFIED — but must precede launch, not follow it
    APPLIES TO
    B mandatory TODAY; C to be determined per member state, TODAY

    NEEDS FIRST Identity verification, transaction monitoring, record-keeping infrastructure, and a per-market answer on whether you are an obliged entity

    [CORRECTED — THIS WAS THE MOST DANGEROUS ITEM IN THE ORIGINAL BRIEF, WHICH GAVE A 2027 START DATE]. AML obligations for gambling service providers are ALREADY LIVE across the EU. AMLD4 Art 2(1)(3)(f) has made 'providers of gambling services' obliged entities EU-wide since the 26 June 2017 transposition deadline, and AMLD4 Art 3(14) already defines gambling services in materially identical words to the AMLR: 'a service which involves wagering a stake with monetary value in games of chance, including those with an element of skill such as lotteries, casino games, poker games and betting transactions that are provided at a physical location, or by any means at a distance, by electronic means or any other technology for facilitating communication, and at the individual request of a recipient of services'. So the AMLR did NOT give the first EU-wide definition and 10 July 2027 is NOT the date AML first bites — it is the date a directly-applicable regulation replaces the national regime. Launching Model B in late 2026 with no AML programme would be unlawful in most member states on day one. Live obligations: customer due diligence, transaction monitoring, FIU suspicious-transaction reporting, record-keeping, and registration/licensing with the national AML supervisor. CDD TRIGGER [audit addition, now researched]: transactions of EUR 2,000 or more, on collection of winnings, wagering of a stake, or both, in a single operation or several linked operations — AMLD4 Art 11(d), carried into AMLR Art 19 (exact AMLR paragraph number UNVERIFIED); the AMLR also expects monitoring BELOW the threshold to catch structuring. Whether Model C's paid-entry contest is inside 'games of chance... including those with an element of skill' is unresolved and needs counsel per market — see the low-risk-exemption item below.

  • Confirm the AI Act does not apply, and re-test the conclusion if you add any AI

First, if you add AI-driven personalised offers, dynamic difficulty tuned to spending, or emotion/behaviour inference aimed at maximising stake size, test against the Art 5 PROHIBITED practices — Art 5(1)(a) manipulative or deceptive techniques materially distorting behaviour and causing significant harm, and Art 5(1)(b) exploitation of vulnerabilities including age — in force since 2 February 2025, highest fine tier. A gambling-adjacent product with whale-targeted personalisation is close to that line.   • Confirm MiCA does not apply

    ISSUER
    Regulation (EU) 2023/1114 (MiCA); ESMA/EBA and national competent authorities
    COST
    n/a
    LEAD TIME
    Hours
    APPLIES TO
    A, B, C, D — not applicable on current design

    NEEDS FIRST Confirm no DLT or blockchain in the coin ledger

    MiCA's crypto-asset definition turns on the value being transferable and stored using distributed ledger technology or similar technology. A centrally-ledgered coin balance in your own database is not a crypto-asset and MiCA does not apply. Exact article reference and any in-game carve-out recital: UNVERIFIED. This conclusion breaks immediately if anyone proposes putting coins on-chain or making them tradeable — at which point you would face BOTH MiCA and (for a redeemable, transferable coin) the e-money-token regime, which is the worst of all worlds. Note the overlap with the corrected EMD2 test: on-chain tradeability is precisely what satisfies EMD2 Art 2(2) limb (ii).

  • Ship DSA baseline compliance if and when you become a hosting service

    ISSUER
    Regulation (EU) 2022/2065 (DSA) Arts 11, 12, 14, 16 (NOT Art 15 — see notes); supervised by the Digital Services Coordinator of your member state of establishment or of your legal representative
    COST
    n/a
    LEAD TIME
    UNVERIFIED
    APPLIES TO
    A, B, C, D — only once accounts introduce stored user-provided information

    NEEDS FIRST Art 13 legal representative designated; DSA scoping decision made

    [CORRECTED — ART 15 REMOVED]. Baseline duties that apply regardless of Section 3 exemptions: Art 11 single point of contact for authorities, Art 12 point of contact for users, Art 14 terms and conditions written clearly including your content-restriction policies, and — for hosting services — Art 16 notice-and-action mechanisms for illegal content. ART 15 TRANSPARENCY REPORTING DOES NOT APPLY TO YOU: Art 15(2) provides that Art 15(1) 'shall not apply to providers of intermediary services that qualify as micro or small enterprises as defined in Recommendation 2003/361/EC and which are not very large online platforms within the meaning of Article 33'. This brief assumes micro/small status elsewhere (it relies on Art 19 for exactly that), so listing Art 15 among size-blind baseline duties was a misstatement that would have generated unnecessary annual reporting work. Because you are very likely not an 'online platform' (no dissemination to the public), Arts 20-28 do not apply, and Art 19 would exempt you from Section 3 anyway as a micro/small enterprise, with a 12-month grace period after you outgrow that status.   • Track the Digital Fairness Act and design to its expected requirements now

    ISSUER
    European Commission (proposal expected H2 2026); NOT YET LAW
    COST
    n/a
    LEAD TIME
    Adoption unlikely before 2027; application later — all timing UNVERIFIED
    APPLIES TO
    A, B, C, D

    NEEDS FIRST Coin economy design decisions not yet locked

    COMMENTARY AND TRADE PRESS, NOT LAW. The DFA is trailed as a single instrument covering dark patterns, addictive design, loot boxes, virtual currencies, influencer marketing and unfair personalised pricing. Two expected provisions land directly on this product: an obligation to display the real-world monetary value of in-game purchasable items even when priced in virtual currency (which the CPC Key Principles already assert as current law — itself contested), and possibly an outright loot-box ban or a parental-consent requirement for minors, with the Netherlands reported to be pushing for a full ban. Practical advice: if you build the real-money price display and the exact-amount coin purchase now, DFA compliance is close to free later; if you build a stacked dual-currency economy with randomised paid rewards, you are building a product you may have to dismantle.   • Obtain member-state gambling counsel in every launch market before Model B or C

    ISSUER
    National gambling regulators (out of this lane)
    COST
    UNVERIFIED
    LEAD TIME
    UNVERIFIED
    APPLIES TO
    B, C

    NEEDS FIRST Final product spec including stake mechanics and prize determination

    There is no EU gambling directive. Gambling is reserved to member states subject only to the TFEU free-movement rules (Arts 49 and 56) as policed by the CJEU, which permits national restrictions on public-interest grounds. That means 27 separate answers on whether a timed trivia duel for money is gambling, a skill competition, or a prize contest — and a favourable answer in Malta tells you nothing about Germany, the Netherlands or Poland. Horizontal EU law CANNOT resolve this. Instruct the SAME counsel on three linked questions at once, because one characterisation drives all three: gambling licensing, AML obliged-entity status (live today, not 2027), and VAT Art 135(1)(i) exemption.

  • [AUDIT ADDITION] Comply with Directive (EU) 2019/770 (Digital Content Directive) across conformity, modification and termination

    ISSUER
    Directive (EU) 2019/770 — transposition 1 July 2021, applicable to contracts from 1 January 2022; enforced through national contract law and national consumer authorities
    COST
    UNVERIFIED (counsel time)
    LEAD TIME
    Weeks
    APPLIES TO
    A, B, C, D

    NEEDS FIRST Draft ToS; product spec for coins, cosmetics and any subscription

    [AUDIT ADDITION — the single largest gap in the original brief's consumer-law layer: the DCD was never cited once]. This is the core contract-law instrument for exactly this product. Arts 6-8: objective and subjective conformity requirements for digital content and digital services. Arts 14-18: remedies for non-conformity, price reduction, and reimbursement on termination. ART 19 IS THE ONE THAT CONSTRAINS YOUR ECONOMY: it governs when a trader may modify digital content or a digital service during continuous supply — permitted only where the contract allows it, there is a valid reason, it is at no extra cost, and the consumer is informed in advance on a durable medium; and where the modification ADVERSELY AFFECTS ACCESS TO OR USE of the content, the consumer gets a free termination right. This, not UCTD Art 3 alone, is the hard-law limit on post-launch coin rebalancing, cosmetic removal and feature withdrawal. The 'legal guarantee of conformity for digital content and services' that CPC Principle 4 requires you to disclose pre-contractually comes from here. Get your ToS drafted against the DCD, not just against a US-style EULA template.

  • [AUDIT ADDITION] Scope the European Accessibility Act and set a trigger alarm at the microenterprise threshold

    ISSUER
    Directive (EU) 2019/882 (European Accessibility Act) — requirements APPLY SINCE 28 June 2025; enforced by national market surveillance authorities with fines
    COST
    UNVERIFIED
    LEAD TIME
    Months if retrofitted — design it in instead
    APPLIES TO
    A, B, C, D — exempt today only as a microenterprise service provider

    NEEDS FIRST Headcount and turnover tracking; accessibility audit of the app shell

    [AUDIT ADDITION — not mentioned once in the original brief]. The EAA expressly covers e-commerce and consumer-facing digital services, i.e. websites and mobile apps used to conclude consumer contracts — which is exactly what a coin shop or subscription checkout is. Art 4(5) exempts MICROENTERPRISES providing services, which is why you can ignore it today. The exemption evaporates at 10 staff or EUR 2m annual turnover. Retrofitting WCAG-level accessibility into a heavily animated, timer-driven, juice-and-ceremony game UI after launch is expensive: timed inputs, motion, colour-coded answer shapes and audio-cued ceremonies are all EAA-relevant. FACT//DUEL already has reduced-motion support, which is the right direction. Action: put a headcount/turnover trigger in the compliance calendar, and build the checkout flow to accessibility standards from day one even while exempt.

  • [AUDIT ADDITION] Fix the governing-law and jurisdiction clauses: a US or offshore choice of law does NOT displace EU consumer protection

    ISSUER
    Regulation (EC) No 593/2008 (Rome I) Art 6(1)-(2); Regulation (EU) No 1215/2012 (Brussels Ia) Arts 17-19
    COST
    UNVERIFIED (counsel time)
    LEAD TIME
    Days
    APPLIES TO
    A, B, C, D

    NEEDS FIRST Draft ToS

    [AUDIT ADDITION — the original brief asserted throughout that the CRD, UCPD, UCTD and DCD bind a non-EU trader without ever explaining why]. Rome I Art 6(1) applies the law of the consumer's habitual residence to a contract with a trader who 'by any means, directs' commercial activities to that member state; Art 6(2) permits a choice of law but provides that it 'may not... have the result of depriving the consumer of the protection afforded to him by provisions that cannot be derogated from by agreement' under that home law. Brussels Ia Art 18 lets the consumer sue in the courts of their own domicile and restricts where you can sue them. CONSEQUENCE: a US- or offshore-law ToS template does not solve the EU problem, and a founder handed one may believe it does. If you accept EUR pricing, localise, or market into the EU, you are 'directing activities'. Have the ToS reviewed on the assumption that 27 sets of mandatory consumer rules apply to the parts that cannot be contracted away.

  • [AUDIT ADDITION] Put a CRD Art 8(2) 'order with obligation to pay' button on EVERY purchase surface, not just the subscription confirm

    ISSUER
    Directive 2011/83/EU (CRD) Art 6 pre-contractual information and Art 8(2) confirm-button labelling; Art 8(7) durable-medium confirmation
    COST
    n/a
    LEAD TIME
    Days (UI work)
    APPLIES TO
    A, B, C, D

    NEEDS FIRST Checkout design

    [AUDIT ADDITION — the original brief applied Art 8(2) only to Model D]. Art 8(2) requires that, for a distance contract concluded by electronic means placing the consumer under an obligation to pay, the trader make the consumer explicitly acknowledge that the order implies an obligation to pay, and that the order button be labelled unambiguously ('order with obligation to pay' or equivalent). THE SANCTION IS NOT A FINE: if you fail, the consumer is NOT BOUND BY THE CONTRACT. That is direct revenue reversal on every coin purchase, not a paperwork risk. It applies to every coin-purchase button in Models A, B and C exactly as it does to a subscription confirm. Pair it with Art 6 pre-contractual information on the same screen and Art 8(7) durable-medium confirmation after purchase.

  • [AUDIT ADDITION] Publish e-Commerce Directive provider identification and label commercial communications

    ISSUER
    Directive 2000/31/EC (e-Commerce Directive) Arts 5, 6 and 10, as implemented nationally
    COST
