Baseline information-society-service provider duties that sit ALONGSIDE CRD Art 6 and apply independently of it: Art 5 requires easily, directly and permanently accessible identification — name, geographic address of establishment, contact details including email, trade/company register and registration number, and VAT identification number where applicable; Art 6 requires commercial communications to be clearly identifiable as such and to identify the person on whose behalf they are made, with promotional offers and their qualifying conditions clearly identifiable; Art 10 sets pre-order information duties for the technical steps of concluding a contract. Also note: the Art 3 COUNTRY-OF-ORIGIN principle offers a non-EU-established provider NOTHING — you get no single-market passport for your home-country rules, so the 27-market analysis stands.

  • [AUDIT ADDITION] Review per-country pricing against the Geo-blocking Regulation before implementing VAT-driven price variation

    ISSUER
    Regulation (EU) 2018/302 Art 4; enforced by national authorities designated under Art 7
    COST
    UNVERIFIED (counsel time)
    LEAD TIME
    Days
    APPLIES TO
    A, B, C, D

    NEEDS FIRST Pricing decision; geolocation implementation

    [AUDIT ADDITION — the original brief advised 'either absorb the spread or vary the price per country' with no caveat]. Art 4 constrains applying different general conditions of access to an electronically supplied service for reasons related to a customer's nationality, place of residence or place of establishment. There IS room to price differently per market, but the IMPLEMENTATION DETAIL — how you determine the customer's country, whether you block or reroute, whether the difference is presented as a general condition of access — is exactly where geo-blocking complaints arise. Note the interaction with the OSS location-evidence item: the same geolocation signals that keep you VAT-compliant are the ones that can create a geo-blocking complaint if used to deny access rather than to set VAT. Get the design reviewed before you ship country-varying prices.

Risks specific to European Union

  • GERMANY WAS THE BRIEF'S RECOMMENDED FIRST MARKET AND THE BRIEF MISSED THE PERMIT THAT APPLIES THERE. Escaping § 3(1) GlueStV on skill predominance does not mean no German permission is needed: § 33d GewO requires a trade permit (Erlaubnis) for commercially operating 'andere Spiele mit Gewinnmoeglichkeit' — games with a chance to win that are NOT Gluecksspiel — which is exactly where a paid skill contest lands. Its application to purely online offerings is CONTESTED. The uncorrected advice to 'run Model C in Germany unlicensed' is withdrawn; get a German opinion covering both provisions before charging a single German player.
  • SWEDEN IS A LICENCE MARKET FOR THIS PRODUCT, NOT AN EXEMPT ONE. Spellagen's kombinationsspel — a game where the probability of winning depends on a combination of skill and chance — is licensable, and the Spelinspektionen carve-out the uncorrected brief relied on requires the ABSENCE of any element of chance, not skill predominance. A random question draw defeats it. The uncorrected headline would have sent a founder into Sweden unlicensed.
  • MODEL B IS STILL NOT LAUNCHABLE EU-WIDE, BUT NOT FOR THE REASON PREVIOUSLY GIVEN. The uncorrected claim that 'no EU member state uses a consideration gateway' is wrong — Spellagen 3 kap. 4 § exempts games requiring no stake, and Germany's test asks whether an Entgelt is demanded. Genuinely free-to-enter designs DO work in specific states. What no EU state accepts is a DUAL paid-plus-free entry route cleansing the paid route, and France defeats it in terms. Do not dismiss free-entry designs; do not assume a free side door launders a paid product.
  • THE MALTA TYPE 3 / TYPE 4 CHOICE IS AN EU-WIDE CHARACTERISATION, NOT A FORM CHOICE. Type 3 is by definition a class of GAMES OF CHANCE. Filing for Type 3 concedes the product is a game of chance and destroys the skill-predominance argument in Germany and Austria, weakens it in the Netherlands and strengthens capture in Denmark, Sweden, Spain, Ireland and Italy. The uncorrected brief presented Type 3 and Type 4 as interchangeable fallbacks.
  • THE UNCORRECTED BRIEF CITED A REPEALED MALTESE INSTRUMENT AS THE LIVE SKILL-GAMES REGIME. S.L. 438.11 was repealed by the Gaming Act (Cap. 583), Act XVI of 2018. The controlled-skill-game concept survives under Cap. 583 and MGA Directive 6 of 2018, but any filing or investor document citing S.L. 438.11 is citing law that has not existed for years.
  • THE AMLR ITEM WAS LABELLED 'VERIFIED PRIMARY TEXT' AND CONTAINED TWO CITATION ERRORS AND ONE OVERSTATEMENT. The CDD provision is Art. 19(5), not Art. 20(5). The Art. 4 exemption has a carve-back for state-operated and state-regulated online gambling that the brief truncated away, so 'FACT//DUEL cannot be exempted' is withdrawn. And AMLR capture of a genuinely chance-free Model C is CONTESTED, because Art. 2(1)(12) still requires a 'game of chance' — the brief could not simultaneously argue Model C is chance-free enough to escape the GlueStV and that it is an AMLR game of chance. Build the KYC stack anyway; the engineering conclusion survives the legal correction.
  • PAYMENTS AND E-MONEY AUTHORISATION IS A HARD BUILD DEPENDENCY AND WAS ENTIRELY ABSENT. Every model with a cash-out requires holding and remitting player funds, triggering PSD2/EMD2 authorisation or a regulated PSP/EMI partner, plus player-fund segregation obligations that are licence conditions in DK, NL, SE, ES and MT. A founder could build the whole gambling-compliance stack and still be unable to move money.
  • THE DOCUMENT RECOMMENDS A LICENSING STRATEGY WHILE KNOWING THE PRICE OF EXACTLY ONE MARKET. Malta's fees are verified. Germany, Spain, Italy, the Netherlands, Ireland, Denmark and Sweden are all UNVERIFIED. Italy's per-concession fee is reported around EUR 7m for nine years and Romania's minimum annual authorisation tax at EUR 480,000 — both UNVERIFIED, and either would remove the market. Licence ECONOMICS, not licence availability, will set the realistic EU footprint.
  • A LICENSED MARKET CAN STILL BE AN UNMARKETABLE ONE. Italy's near-total advertising ban (Decreto Dignita), Spain's Real Decreto 958/2020, the Dutch untargeted-advertising ban, Belgium's 2023-24 prohibition and Germany's GlueStV advertising limits were absent from the uncorrected brief. For a consumer app that depends on paid acquisition this is decisive and should be checked before a licence is bought.
  • THE RAKE STRUCTURE IS A BIGGER LEVER THAN THE TIMER AND WAS NEVER ANALYSED. Taking a commission from a player-funded pot makes the product a Type 3 P2P game in Malta and a 'stake' under most EU definitions; paying a fixed, announced, operator-funded prize pool that does not depend on entrant numbers weakens or removes the stake limb in several states. That is the difference between licensable and out-of-scope in Germany, Austria and the Netherlands. Freeze this before instructing counsel.
  • THE 5/7/10-SECOND TIMER IS A LEGAL DESIGN DECISION, NOT A GAME-FEEL DECISION. Every favourable Model C verdict in Germany, Austria and the Netherlands depends on a skill-versus-chance test, and Sweden's and France's tests are stricter still — they turn on the ABSENCE of chance, not its non-predominance. Short timers on randomly drawn questions convert knowledge into reflex and draw-luck. Fixed identical question sets, longer timers and larger samples per contest all buy legal ground; the current duel format spends it.
  • AN MGA LICENCE BUYS ESTABLISHMENT, NOT MARKET ACCESS, AND THE DOWNSIDE IS RESTITUTION, NOT A FINE. There is no mutual recognition. In Case C-440/23 (European Lotto and Betting Ltd and Deutsche Lotto- und Sportwetten Ltd, judgment 16 April 2026) the CJEU held that a member state may prohibit online gambling licensed elsewhere, that member states may attach civil-law consequences including contract nullity and restitution of lost stakes, and that a player's restitution claim is not an abuse of EU rights. NOTE: the audit flagged this case as possibly fabricated; the editor verified it exists and the holdings are as described across multiple practitioner sources, though the curia text itself was not fetched. Malta's Art. 56A shield does not protect against a German, Dutch or Austrian claim.
  • THE 'ENTRIES' WORD IN MODEL A IS AN UNEXAMINED TRAPDOOR. If coins buy entry to anything carrying a prize of money or money's worth, Model A is Model C with a currency layer and every Model C analysis applies. Define precisely what an entry buys before treating Model A as safe.
  • BELGIUM CAN CRIMINALISE MODEL A IF COINS BUY RANDOMISED REWARDS. The Belgian Kansspelcommissie's 2018 position treats paid virtual currency plus chance plus an item of value as illegal gambling under the Gaming Act of 7 May 1999, enforced by threat of criminal prosecution. NOTE the audit's correction: this was a research report / position paper, not a formal adjudicative ruling, and its text is UNVERIFIED. No loot boxes, no gacha, no randomised paid rewards in the EU build regardless.
  • A SUBSCRIPTION CAN BE THE CONSIDERATION THAT CREATES GAMBLING. The MGA's controlled-skill-game definition counts consideration 'whether in the form of a stake, a periodic subscription or the purchase of in-game items, which provides an advantage to the player'. If the Model D subscription grants extra expedition entries, higher stake tiers or any competitive edge in a prize event, Model D converts into Model C. Keep it strictly cosmetic and strictly severed from prize contests.
  • SEVERAL REGIMES ARE MID-FLIGHT AND SOME OF THE DATES IN THIS DOCUMENT HAVE NOT HAPPENED YET — THE UNCORRECTED BRIEF WROTE TWO OF THEM IN THE COMPLETED PAST TENSE. As at 14 September 2026: Malta's gaming tax change takes effect 1 OCTOBER 2026 (future); the Netherlands' 2021 licence cohort expires 1 OCTOBER 2026 (future); Germany's GlueStV statutory evaluation is due 31 December 2026; the Digital Fairness Act is a Q4 2026 work-programme item NOT YET TABLED; Ireland's remote gaming licences are reported as not yet open; Finland's licensed market opens 1 July 2027; the AMLR applies from 10 July 2027. Austria's entire 2026 reform timeline is UNVERIFIED and should not be planned against. Re-verify every date before committing engineering or capital.
  • HALF THIS DOCUMENT'S SUBDIVISIONS ARE DIRECTORY-DERIVED AND SHOULD NOT BE READ WITH EQUAL AUTHORITY. Only Denmark, Spain, Sweden, Germany's § 33d GewO, the AMLR, the Commission's policy page and the MGA fee guidance were checked against primary text in this lane. Bulgaria, Croatia, Estonia, Hungary, Lithuania, Slovakia and Slovenia rest on a single 2026 regulators directory. Act names, article numbers and amendment status for those states are NOT reliable.
  • THIS IS A COMPLIANCE CHECKLIST, NOT LEGAL ADVICE. Every Model C verdict turns on a fact-specific skill-versus-chance assessment that only a national regulator or court can settle. Local counsel is required in every market before money is taken — corrected priority order: Germany, Sweden, Ireland, Spain, Italy, Netherlands, Austria, and France (to confirm the geo-block rather than to seek a route in).
  • [CORRECTED] TRANSFERABILITY, NOT REDEEMABILITY, IS THE E-MONEY CLIFF EDGE. The original brief warned that a goodwill cash refund or an account-closure return of an unspent balance could convert you into an unauthorised e-money issuer. That is WRONG and the advice was actively harmful: EMD2 Art 2(2) limb (ii) requires acceptance by a person OTHER THAN the issuer, and a refund paid by you to your own customer involves no third party accepting anything — nor does an account-closure balance return, nor the statutory CRD withdrawal refund this brief itself mandates. Consumer-friendly refunds are lawful and partly compulsory; do not suppress them on a mistaken legal basis. THE REAL LIMB-(ii) RISKS are: player-to-player coin gifting or trading, a tournament that pays winners in transferable coins, any third-party merchant accepting your coin, and any grey market that can price the balance. Write the NO-TRANSFER, NO-THIRD-PARTY-ACCEPTANCE rule into the product spec and the terms, and make it a code-level invariant with a test.
  • [CORRECTED] AML IS LIVE TODAY FOR MODEL B AND POSSIBLY MODEL C — NOT FROM 10 JULY 2027. This was the most dangerous framing in the original brief. AMLD4 Art 2(1)(3)(f) has made providers of gambling services obliged entities EU-wide since the 26 June 2017 transposition deadline, and AMLD4 Art 3(14) already carries the 'games of chance, including those with an element of skill' definition. A Model B launch in late 2026 built to a 2027 AML start date would be operating unlawfully in most member states on day one: no CDD, no transaction monitoring, no FIU reporting, no registration with the national AML supervisor. CDD bites at EUR 2,000 on collection of winnings, wagering of a stake, or both, single or linked (AMLD4 Art 11(d); AMLR Art 19). Comply with national AML law NOW; re-paper to the AMLR by 10 July 2027.
  • [CORRECTED] THE AMLR DOES NOT END PER-MARKET DIVERGENCE. AMLD4 Art 2(2) lets member states exempt certain gambling services (not casinos) on proven low risk with notification to the Commission, and the AML package preserves an equivalent option. So 'it is a regulation, therefore uniform' is false in the one respect that matters: whether YOU are an obliged entity remains a 27-market question after 10 July 2027. This cuts both ways — it is also the most plausible escape hatch for Model C, and the original brief omitted it.
  • [CORRECTED] MODEL C'S VAT TREATMENT IS NOT SETTLED. The original brief called the entry fee 'settled' as a taxable electronically supplied service after testing only Art 135(1)(e). Art 135(1)(i) exempts betting, lotteries and other forms of gambling subject to each member state's conditions and limitations. If a state characterises the paid-entry duel as gambling — the same question the brief calls unresolved for AML — the fee may be VAT-EXEMPT there, outside your OSS return, with input-VAT recovery consequences. For any staked leg, the Glawe line (C-38/93; C-377/11) can make the taxable amount the operator's retained margin rather than gross stakes. Both change the revenue model materially and both were missing.
  • [CORRECTED] C-472/24 IS NARROWER THAN THE BRIEF CLAIMED, AND YOUR VAT OBLIGATION DOES NOT DEPEND ON IT. The taxable person in MB 'Zaidimu valiuta' was a THIRD-PARTY TRADER reselling RuneScape gold on a secondary market, not a publisher issuing its own currency, and the single-purpose voucher question under Art 30a(2) for a first-party issuer was never before the Court. Extending the holding to your own coin sale is a reasonable inference, not law. The good news: it does not matter much, because the obligation to charge destination VAT from the first euro follows from Art 58 plus the fact that the Art 59c EUR 10,000 threshold applies only to EU-established sellers. Take the conservative position; document it as an inference; do not cite the case as though it decided your facts.
  • VAT IS OWED AT THE COIN SALE, NOT AT THE SPEND — AND THIS BREAKS NAIVE FINANCIAL MODELS. On the conservative reading you owe destination-country VAT on the full consideration the moment coins are bought, before the player has consumed anything. Combined with the (contested) CPC position that unspent coins remain refundable for 14 days, you can be liable for VAT on a sale you later have to reverse. Model the cash-flow and build VAT-credit handling into the refund path.
  • THE 14-DAY UNSPENT-COIN REFUND POSITION IS A FRAUD VECTOR IN A STAKING GAME. If a player can buy coins, stake and lose them, and then demand a refund, you have built a free-roll. The CPC position limits the right to UNSPENT currency, so the mitigation is a strict FIFO lot ledger that marks coins consumed at the moment of stake, plus clear disclosure. Get this architecture right before launch; it is close to impossible to retrofit onto a balance-only ledger.
  • [RE-LABELLED] THE CPC KEY PRINCIPLES ARE SOFT LAW AND PARTS OF THEM ARE GENUINELY CONTESTED — THE ORIGINAL HEADLINE CALLED THEM 'NON-NEGOTIABLE'. Footnote 1 of the document states it 'does therefore by no means bind the national authorities or the European Commission' and that non-compliance is not necessarily unlawful. Video Games Europe and the EGDF have publicly said the Network introduced new legal theories; German courts have upheld properly-constructed withdrawal-right waivers for virtual currency. Two of the original brief's 'four hard obligations' — the unspent-coin refund right and the real-money price display — rest wholly on this document. You could win the argument. But the Principles state the enforcement posture of the authorities who would open the file, and a CPC coordinated action is itself the punishment. Build to them; book them as posture, not as law.
  • DESIGNING FOR WHALES IS EXPRESSLY A CONSUMER-LAW RISK, NOT JUST AN ETHICS QUESTION. The CPC Key Principles treat high-spending players as a VULNERABLE group likely to struggle with impulse control or gambling disorders, and say a business model based on targeting them is assessed under a stricter fairness threshold under UCPD Arts 5-9. FACT//DUEL's existing juice, streak, escrow and ceremony vocabulary is exactly the design language this is aimed at. A monetisation plan built on whale extraction is a UCPD exposure regardless of which model you pick.
  • YOU WILL BE DEEMED TO HAVE CHILD PLAYERS WHETHER YOU TARGET THEM OR NOT. The CPC Network states that any game not exclusively intended for adults should expect a significant under-18 player base, and that age gates do not discharge the trader's responsibility. A trivia game is the archetype of a product children reach. This drives the DPIA requirement, the Art 8 GDPR parental-consent gate, the spending-off-by-default expectation, and the UCPD Annex I point 28 ban on direct exhortation — which is hard law in the directive itself and needs no CPC theory. All of it applies to Model A too.
  • [AUDIT ADDITION] CRD ART 8(2) NON-COMPLIANCE IS REVENUE REVERSAL, NOT A FINE. If the coin-purchase button is not labelled to make the payment obligation unambiguous and the consumer is not made to acknowledge it explicitly, THE CONSUMER IS NOT BOUND BY THE CONTRACT. This applies to every coin-purchase surface in Models A, B and C, not just a subscription confirm. It is the cheapest fix in this document and the most expensive omission.
  • [AUDIT ADDITION] A US- OR OFFSHORE-LAW ToS DOES NOT SOLVE THE EU. Rome I Art 6(1)-(2) applies the mandatory consumer-protection law of the consumer's habitual residence to a trader directing activities to that member state, and a choice-of-law clause cannot deprive the consumer of it; Brussels Ia Art 18 lets them sue at home. A founder handed a US template may believe the governing-law clause disposes of the CRD, UCPD, UCTD and DCD. It does not. Assume 27 sets of mandatory rules apply to everything that cannot be contracted away.
  • [AUDIT ADDITION] COLLECTIVE REDRESS IS THE REALISTIC ENFORCEMENT VECTOR AND THE ORIGINAL BRIEF NEVER MENTIONED IT. Directive (EU) 2020/1828 on representative actions has applied since 25 June 2023: qualified entities can bring cross-border collective redress for infringements of the CRD, UCPD, UCTD, DCD and the GDPR. For a coin economy that will demonstrably reach minors, a qualified-entity action is at least as likely as a regulator action and carries damages rather than commitments.
  • [AUDIT ADDITION] DIRECTIVE (EU) 2019/770 ART 19 IS THE HARD LIMIT ON REBALANCING A PAID COIN ECONOMY. The original brief attributed this constraint to UCTD Art 3 and the CPC Principles alone. DCD Art 19 is hard law: modification of a continuously-supplied digital service requires a contractual basis, a valid reason, no extra cost and advance notice on a durable medium, and where the change adversely affects access or use the consumer may TERMINATE FREE and claim reimbursement under Arts 16-18. Design the economy on the assumption that you cannot devalue or withdraw what people paid for.
  • [AUDIT ADDITION] THE EUROPEAN ACCESSIBILITY ACT IS A DELAYED-FUSE COST. Directive (EU) 2019/882 has applied since 28 June 2025 to consumer-facing e-commerce services. You are exempt only as a microenterprise service provider (Art 4(5)); the exemption evaporates at 10 staff or EUR 2m turnover — i.e. exactly when a monetised launch succeeds. Retrofitting WCAG-level accessibility into a timer-driven, heavily animated, audio-cued game is expensive. Build the checkout accessibly now.
  • [CORRECTED] THE EU-US DATA PRIVACY FRAMEWORK COULD FALL AGAIN MID-BUILD. It is valid today. The General Court dismissed the challenge in Case T-553/23 Latombe v Commission on 3 September 2025; the appeal was lodged 31 October 2025 and is pending as Case C-703/25 P (case number corroborated by independent sources and retained). The original brief's additional claims of 'no hearing date announced as at mid-2026' and 'Microsoft granted leave to intervene on 29 June 2026' are UNCORROBORATED and removed. The CJEU killed both predecessor frameworks. Do not design a US-processor architecture with no fallback: SCCs plus transfer impact assessments in every US vendor contract NOW, and know which processors you could move to EU regions.
  • [CORRECTED] PSD3/PSR WILL RESHUFFLE THE PERIMETER RIGHT AFTER YOU LAUNCH — AND IT REPEALS EMD2. Provisional political agreement 27 November 2025 is corroborated; the brief's '23 April 2026 final compromise texts' date is not, and is removed. The cited source puts expected OJ publication at end of Q2 2026 and PSR application at 18 months after entry into force (payee-name verification at 24), not ~21 months. MATERIAL AND PREVIOUSLY OMITTED: PSD3 REPEALS EMD2 OUTRIGHT; existing EMI authorisations survive only 24 months after entry into force (extendable to 30) with a MANDATORY RE-APPLICATION. So an EMI licence obtained for Model B in 2027 would need re-papering almost immediately. Build Model B only with counsel tracking the PSR text.
  • BETAAL GARANT IS A THINNER SHIELD THAN IT LOOKS. C-51/25 (16 July 2026) is genuinely helpful — ancillary fund transfers serving a primary non-payment service are outside PSD2 — but practitioners describe it as fact-specific and confined, it turned partly on the intermediary not holding the payer's payment account, it did NOT address money remittance, and further CJEU references are pending. Holding named per-player balances is the fact most likely to defeat the ancillary argument. Do not let anyone treat this judgment as a licence substitute.
  • THE DIGITAL FAIRNESS ACT IS A KNOWN FUTURE RESET AND EVERY TIMELINE CLAIM ABOUT IT IS PRESS REPORTING. A proposal expected H2 2026 covering dark patterns, addictive design, loot boxes and virtual currencies will land on this exact product category. The cheap insurance is to build the real-money price display, the exact-amount coin purchase and the no-randomised-paid-rewards rule now, so the DFA is a documentation exercise rather than a rebuild.
  • A SINGLE FEATURE CAN FLIP YOU INTO THE FULL DSA ONLINE-PLATFORM REGIME. Today you are probably not even an intermediary; with accounts you are probably a hosting service (Arts 11, 12, 14, 16 — NOT Art 15, which Art 15(2) disapplies for micro/small enterprises); with public leaderboards carrying user-authored text, global chat, or user-generated question packs you become an 'online platform' and acquire Arts 20-28, including the Art 28 minor-protection duty. Put a DSA scoping gate in the product review process for any social feature.
  • [CONTESTED] THE AI ACT OMNIBUS DATES IN THE ORIGINAL BRIEF ARE UNCORROBORATED. The claim that the AI Omnibus was 'adopted by Council 29 June 2026, published in the OJ 24 July 2026 and entered into force 27 July 2026' matches no reachable source; the brief's own citation records only political agreement 6 May 2026 and Council confirmation 13 May 2026, with adoption and OJ publication 'expected before 2 August 2026'. Treat the dates as contested and verify the OJ citation before relying on any deferral. The substance holds: Annex III stand-alone high-risk deferred to 2 December 2027, Annex I embedded high-risk to 2 August 2028, Art 5 prohibitions NOT deferred and in force since 2 February 2025, Art 50 transparency obligations live from 2 August 2026, and a new Art 5 prohibition (nudifiers/CSAM) added with a transitional period to 2 December 2026.
  • TODAY'S DEMO COINS ARE OUTSIDE ALL OF THIS — AND THAT IS A ONE-WAY DOOR. Footnote 2 of the CPC Key Principles expressly excludes in-game currencies obtainable solely through gameplay and not purchasable with real-world money. FACT//DUEL's current demo coins and Expedition points sit in that carve-out. The moment a purchase path exists, the entire consumer-law layer switches on for the design, not just for the new purchase flow — including the price-display and no-stacked-currency principles that constrain how points and coins may relate to each other.

India

India bans cash-out staking outright and leaves purchased-coin staking genuinely UNSETTLED. s.5 of the Promotion and Regulation of Online Gaming Act 2025 (in force 1 May 2026) criminalises offering an "online money game" — s.2(1)(g), "irrespective of whether such game is based on skill, chance, or both" — with 3 years' imprisonment, ₹1 crore fine, cognizable and non-bailable, directors personally liable, and express extraterritorial reach over operators outside India. MODEL B (cash/prize redemption) and MODEL C (paid entry for an announced prize pool) are squarely caught and are criminal offences: do not ship them. MODEL D (subscription) is expressly blessed by s.2(1)(i)(ii). MODEL A (buy coins for cosmetics/entries, never convertible to money) is the only lawful route to taking money for a duel-shaped product — BUT whether purchased, NON-CASHABLE coins staked player-v-player are themselves an "online money game" is CONTESTED and untested. There is no OGAI determination and no judgment on the point; the weight of published 2026 Indian commentary reads a non-convertible in-game coin as falling OUTSIDE "other stakes" in s.2(1)(j) and inside "online social game", while a plain-meaning reading of the "which is purchased" limb would catch it. Treat this as a live legal risk to be priced and opinioned on by Indian counsel, not as a settled answer in either direction — the previous version of this lane asserted it as settled criminality and that assertion is withdrawn.

  • A LAWFUL IN ITS PURE FORM; CONTESTED IF PURCHASED COINS ARE STAKED PLAYER-V-PLAYER

    Model A as defined — money in, cosmetics and prize-free entries out, nothing convertible back to money or to a prize of value — sits outside s.2(1)(g) and inside "online social game" in s.2(1)(i). s.2(1)(g) defines an online money game as one "played by a user by paying fees, depositing money or other stakes in expectation of winning which entails monetary AND other enrichment in return of money or other stakes". CORRECTION (audit, verified verbatim against Bar & Bench's quotation of the section): the conjunction is "and", not "or". There is therefore a serious argument that monetary enrichment is a NECESSARY component of the winning limb, and that winning further non-monetary in-game credit alone does not satisfy it. The earlier version of this lane relied on a disjunctive reading it never disclosed; that reliance is withdrawn. s.2(1)(i) permits a game that (i) does not involve staking or participation with expectation of winning monetary gain, (ii) "may allow access through payment of a subscription fee or one-time access fee, provided that such payment is not in the nature of a stake or wager", and (iii) is offered solely for entertainment, recreation or skill-development; limb (ii) is permissive, not an exhaustive list of lawful payments. The 2026 Rules' five-factor determination test [RULE NUMBER INDICATIVE ONLY — 2026 Rules gazette text not obtained; secondary sources conflict] turns on whether rewards or in-game assets "can be transferred, redeemed, monetised or used outside the game environment"; purely cosmetic, non-tradeable items with no external market sit on the permitted side. THE CONTESTED POINT — STAKING PURCHASED COINS. s.2(1)(j) defines "other stakes" as "anything recognised as equivalent or convertible to money and includes credits, coins, token or objects ... whether it is real or virtual, which is purchased by paying money directly or by indirect means or as part of, or in relation to, an online game". This lane now adopts ONE construction consistently (audit defect: the prior version read the definition as self-sufficient where that produced an alarming answer and as conjunctive where that produced a convenient one): the CONJUNCTIVE reading — the thing must be BOTH "recognised as equivalent or convertible to money" AND purchased/acquired. On that reading, coins that are non-transferable, non-redeemable, have no secondary market and no external convertibility are NOT "other stakes", even when bought with real money — and a duel staking them is not an online money game. That is also the reading taken by the most directly on-point published 2026 analysis (respawn.outlookindia.com, "Pocket casinos: how mobile games are exposing PROGA's loopholes"), which concludes that a game "which takes real money and returns dice rolls and stickers, almost certainly falls within PROGA's definition of an online social game". The contrary reading — that "which is purchased" is a self-sufficient inclusive limb, so any purchased virtual coin is a stake with or without cash-out — is available on the words and is the reading a regulator taking a policy view would reach for. NEITHER reading has been tested by OGAI or by a court. PRACTICAL POSITION: the two-ledger architecture (purchased currency → cosmetics only; free, non-purchasable currency → duel stakes) is no longer presented as legally compelled, but it remains the design that makes the question moot, and it is the cheapest way to buy out an unquantifiable criminal-law risk in a jurisdiction where the offence is cognizable and non-bailable. Recommend shipping it. TWO CONDITIONS ARE NOT CONTESTED AND ARE LOAD-BEARING: (1) the word "entries" in the model description is dangerous — coins buying entry to any contest with a prize of VALUE converts Model A into Model C, which is unlawful; entries must be to prize-free play only. (2) No transferability, no gifting, no secondary market, no account trading — any of these creates external convertibility and collapses the conjunctive defence. Registration is not required (online social games are registrable voluntarily only), but a voluntary determination application to OGAI is available and is the only route to a written classification — see the checklist for its costs, including the registration-triggered India data-residency duty and the exposure of putting a named foreign operator in front of the regulator.

  • B UNLAWFUL — CRIMINAL

    Flatly criminal, and this verdict does not depend on any contested construction. Winnings redeemable for cash or prizes of value satisfy s.2(1)(g) on any reading: the user deposits money or other stakes, and wins "monetary and other enrichment" — monetary enrichment is present, so the conjunctive reading of the winning limb (which rescues Model A) gives Model B no help at all. The skill/chance distinction is statutorily abolished ("irrespective of whether such game is based on skill, chance, or both"), so the old Indian jurisprudence on rummy and fantasy sports is dead for online play. CORRECTION (audit): the prior version said there is "no sweepstakes doctrine, no dual-currency carve-out and no alternative-method-of-entry defence anywhere in Indian law". The first half stands — those are US constructs and there is no PROGA carve-out for them. The claim that Indian law has nothing in this space is wrong: free-entry and skill-based prize competitions are governed by the Prize Competitions Act, 1955 and by State prize-competition and lottery legislation, which constrain rather than enable any AMOE-style redesign. See the checklist item and subdivision rows; scope of the 1955 Act as applied to an online quiz is UNVERIFIED. CONSEQUENCES: s.5 prohibits offering, and equally "aid, abet, induce or otherwise indulge or engage in the offering"; s.9(1) — up to 3 years' imprisonment or a fine up to ₹1 crore or both; s.9(4) — a second conviction carries a MINIMUM 3 years (up to 5) and a minimum ₹1 crore fine (up to ₹2 crore); s.10 makes ss.5 and 7 COGNIZABLE AND NON-BAILABLE, so arrest without warrant and no bail as of right; s.11 makes every person in charge of the business, and any consenting or conniving director/manager/officer, personally liable (independent and non-executive directors not in actual decision-making are carved out); s.16 lets authorised officers enter and search "any place, whether physical or digital", arrest without warrant and override access controls. ADVERTISING IS A SEPARATE OFFENCE: s.6 bans any advertisement that "directly or indirectly promotes or induces any person to play any online money game", punishable under s.9(2) with up to 2 years or ₹50 lakh or both; note s.6 is NOT listed in s.10, so it is not cognizable/non-bailable — most commentary gets this wrong. FACILITATING IS A SEPARATE OFFENCE: s.7 bars any bank, financial institution "or any other person facilitating financial transactions" from processing payment for an online money gaming service, punishable under s.9(3) with up to 3 years or ₹1 crore — which is why Indian PSPs refuse anything that smells of staking. OFFSHORE OPERATION IS NOT AN ESCAPE: s.1(2) states the Act "extends to the whole of India and also applies to online money gaming service offered within the territory of India or operated from outside the territory of India", and the long title expressly targets services operating "from foreign jurisdictions". CORRECTION (audit, contested): the prior version asserted that "s.14 lets blocking notwithstanding s.69A of the IT Act 2000". That override is UNVERIFIED — the provision was not quoted. PRS's summary describes s.14 as empowering the Central Government to block information relating to online money gaming services; whether it overrides s.69A or operates THROUGH the s.69A procedure and the 2009 Blocking Rules is material, because it decides whether the s.69A safeguards (committee, hearing, reasoned order) attach to a blocking order against the app. Treat as CONTESTED. Enforcement reality is unchanged either way: 8,376 betting/gambling URLs blocked as at 28 March 2026, and OGAI's first advisory of 29 July 2026 directed app stores, cloud providers, ISPs, telcos, banks, social media intermediaries, OTT platforms and ad agencies to delist apps, block payments and pull ads. The route against a foreign founder is app-store removal, payment blocking and ISP blocking within days — not litigation.

  • C UNLAWFUL — CRIMINAL; NO LICENCE EXISTS TO BUY

    Unlawful, and the fixed, operator-funded, announced prize pool does not help. s.2(1)(g) requires only (a) paying fees, depositing money or other stakes and (b) an expectation of winning entailing enrichment. A fixed entry fee is squarely "paying fees"; an announced CASH-or-value prize supplies monetary enrichment, so the "monetary and other enrichment" conjunction does not rescue Model C either. Nothing in the definition turns on whether the pool varies with entrant count, on peer-to-peer versus house-banked structure, or on skill ("irrespective of whether such game is based on skill, chance, or both"). Secondary commentary on the Act is explicit that trivia and quiz contests charging entry fees and distributing cash prizes are banned, alongside fantasy sports, rummy and poker. THERE IS EXACTLY ONE EXIT AND IT IS NOT REALISTICALLY AVAILABLE TO A TRIVIA APP: s.2(1)(g) carves out e-sports, and s.2(1)(c) permits a game that (i) "is played as part of multi-sports events", (ii) involves organised competitive events in multiplayer formats under predefined rules, (iii) "is duly recognised under the National Sports Governance Act, 2025, and registered with the Authority or agency under section 3", (iv) has its outcome determined solely by skill, (v) "may include payment of registration or participation fees solely for the purpose of entering the competition or covering administrative costs and may include performance-based prize money for the player", and (vi) "shall not involve the placing of bets, wagers or any other stakes by any person, whether or not such person is a participant". Limb (iii) is the killer: recognition under the National Sports Governance Act, 2025 (Act 25 of 2025) must be obtained FIRST, and a mobile trivia app is not a multi-sports-event discipline and will not obtain it. CORRECTION (audit, defect withdrawn): the prior version stated that "OGAI cannot make an e-sport determination without the Ministry of Youth Affairs and Sports member present and concurring". NO SOURCE SUPPORTS A MEMBER-SPECIFIC CONCURRENCE OR VETO. The claim is withdrawn as probably invented. OGAI's actual decision-making rules, as reported by SCC Online and Bar & Bench, are ordinary: a Chairperson plus six members, quorum of at least half the total members (online or in person), decisions by majority vote, Chairperson's (or presiding member's) casting vote in a tie, with urgent decisions permitted by written order notified to members within 3 days. A Joint Secretary from the Ministry of Youth Affairs and Sports is an ex officio member — membership is not a veto. The conclusion is unaffected: NSGA recognition remains the blocker. E-sport registration with OGAI is then mandatory [RULE NUMBER INDICATIVE ONLY — 2026 Rules gazette text not obtained; secondary sources conflict] (Bar & Bench places mandatory e-sports registration at rule 12(1)(b) and permissible e-sports payments at rule 9, which conflicts with the sources placing the five-factor determination test at rule 9). 90-day target; certificate validity reported as up to 10 years. I am deliberately NOT calling this "licence-required": there is no licence a founder can buy. Treat paid-entry contests as closed in India.

  • D CLEAR

    Expressly lawful — the one model the statute affirmatively blesses. s.2(1)(i)(ii) provides that an online social game "may allow access through payment of a subscription fee or one-time access fee, provided that such payment is not in the nature of a stake or wager". A recurring fee for features or cosmetics with no stake and no prize of value sits inside the permitted category and outside s.5. CONDITIONS: the subscription must not buy entry to, or advantage in, any prize-bearing contest; subscriber-only currency must not be stakeable; there must be no cash-out, no transfer and no external monetisation of anything the subscription unlocks (the reward-monetisation factor in the determination test). Registration is NOT required — online social games are registrable voluntarily, and the Central Government has not, on the sources available, notified any category of online social game into mandatory registration (UNVERIFIED as at 14 September 2026; the item most likely to change). CORRECTION (audit): the prior version called a voluntary determination application "the cheapest insurance available" without disclosing its costs. It is still worth considering, but registration is reported to be what switches on an India data-residency obligation (retention of traffic data, metadata and related information on computer resources located in India) for registered social games and e-sports — UNVERIFIED, could not be corroborated from the sources reachable, but if true it is a direct engineering and hosting cost caused by the recommendation itself. Filing also puts a named (possibly foreign) operator in front of the regulator, and whether a provider with no Indian presence can even file is UNVERIFIED. CORRECTION (audit): the prior version also treated India obligations as beginning "at the first rupee". They do not. The 2026 Rules' social-game safeguards (age verification, parental controls, time limits, fair-play monitoring, grievance redressal — UNVERIFIED, audit addition) and the DPDP child-data regime attach to the CURRENT FREE PRODUCT wherever it has Indian users. Do not defer India work until monetisation. Tax and data obligations apply in full: 18% GST on the subscription (OIDAR registration from the first Indian rupee if supplied from outside India), and DPDP Act 2023 s.9 child-data duties, which are the real engineering cost. Note also that determinations are reported to bind only your product and only while the model is unchanged [RULE NUMBER INDICATIVE ONLY — 2026 Rules gazette text not obtained; secondary sources conflict] — any move toward staking is a material change requiring notification and triggering reclassification.

States and union territories 12

NO STATE ROUTE; PROGA GOVERNS UNIFORMLY 1   PRE-PROGA ONLINE GAMING LEGISLATION EXISTS; RESIDUAL EXPOSURE UNVERIFIED 4   OFFLINE/CASINO-FOCUSED STATE REGIME; IRRELEVANT TO AN ONLINE PRODUCT 1   NO STATE ROUTE; PROGA GOVERNS 1   HISTORIC GAMING LICENSING REGIME REPORTED; STATUS AFTER PROGA UNVERIFIED — DO NOT TREAT AS A ROUTE TO MARKET 1   HISTORIC SKILL-GAME LICENSING REGIME; STATUS AFTER PROGA UNVERIFIED — DO NOT TREAT AS A ROUTE TO MARKET 1   HISTORIC ONLINE GAMING LICENSING REGIME; STATUS AFTER PROGA UNVERIFIED — DO NOT TREAT AS A ROUTE TO MARKET 1   GOVERNING. MODELS B AND C CRIMINAL; MODEL D LAWFUL; MODEL A LAWFUL WITH ONE CONTESTED LIMB 1   NO SEPARATE ROUTE; PROGA GOVERNS; OGAI HEAD OFFICE IS IN NCT OF DELHI 1

  • All other StatesNO STATE ROUTE; PROGA GOVERNS UNIFORMLY

    PROGA applies to the whole of India, so the model verdicts do not vary by State. The residual State layer to check with counsel is (a) State prize-competition and lottery legislation, which constrains any free-entry/AMOE or skill-prize design (see checklist), and (b) the Public Gambling Act, 1867 and its State adaptations, which continue to govern OFFLINE activity. Neither creates a route to market for Models B or C.

    PROG Act ss.1(2), 5, 18; Prize Competitions Act, 1955; Public Gambling Act, 1867 and State adaptations. Scope UNVERIFIED

  • Andhra PradeshPRE-PROGA ONLINE GAMING LEGISLATION EXISTS; RESIDUAL EXPOSURE UNVERIFIED

    Legislated against online gaming before PROGA. Same analysis. Nothing at State level rescues Model B or C.

    State enactment not verified in this pass; PROG Act s.18. UNVERIFIED

  • GoaOFFLINE/CASINO-FOCUSED STATE REGIME; IRRELEVANT TO AN ONLINE PRODUCT

    Goa's gaming regime is directed at physical casinos and offline gaming and does not create an online route. PROGA governs the online product in Goa as everywhere else.

    PROG Act ss.1(2), 5, 18. State regime not verified in this pass; UNVERIFIED

  • KarnatakaPRE-PROGA ONLINE GAMING LEGISLATION EXISTS; RESIDUAL EXPOSURE UNVERIFIED

    Nagaland is commonly cited as one of the few States that historically licensed online games of skill.     CORRECTED (audit — the prior version's citation here was fabricated in three respects and is withdrawn). (1) INSTRUMENT TITLE: the prior version cited "Reserve Bank of India (Regulation of Payment Aggregators) Directions, 2025, dated 15 September 2025". The inserted words "Regulation of" could not be confirmed on RBI's Master Directions or notifications pages, and neither could the 15 September 2025 date; the framework is understood to be titled "Reserve Bank of India (Payment Aggregators) Directions, 2025" — TITLE AND DATE UNVERIFIED, check against RBI's own site before citing. (2) ACCOUNT TERMINOLOGY: "Outward Collection Account" is not RBI terminology. The PA-CB framework uses the IMPORT COLLECTION ACCOUNT (ICA) for the leg where Indian buyers pay a foreign merchant — which is your leg — and the EXPORT COLLECTION ACCOUNT (ECA) for the reverse. (3) CAP: the ₹25 lakh per-transaction figure derives from the October 2023 PA-CB circular's per-unit import cap and its survival into the 2025 consolidation is UNVERIFIED. DO NOT PLAN PAYMENT ARCHITECTURE AGAINST THE OLD LABELS OR THAT CAP. What stands: you do not need your own authorisation, you need an authorised partner; and s.7 of the PROG Act criminalises any person facilitating payment for an online money gaming service, which is why Indian PSPs will refuse anything that smells of staking.

Stage 3 — Product controls that must ship before launch 16

  • DO NOT launch Model B or Model C in India — hard geo-block India for any cash-out, redemption or prize-of-value feature (IP geolocation + app store country availability + payment BIN/country checks + account country lock)

    ISSUER
    Self-imposed; enforced by Online Gaming Authority of India (OGAI), MeitY
    COST
    Engineering cost only
    LEAD TIME
    Before any India availability
    APPLIES TO
    B, C

    NEEDS FIRST None — this is the gating decision

    s.1(2) of the PROG Act reaches services "operated from outside the territory of India", so a US or EU entity serving Indian users commits the s.5 offence. s.11 makes directors personally liable. Geo-blocking must be real, not a terms-of-service disclaimer; OGAI's 29 July 2026 advisory went to app stores, cloud providers, ISPs, telcos and banks, so the realistic failure mode is overnight delisting and payment blocking, not a lawsuit. CORRECTED SCOPE (audit): this item is now about cash-out and prizes of value, which are unarguably caught. Purchased-coin staking WITHOUT cash-out is a separate, CONTESTED question — see the next item.

  • DECIDE, with Indian counsel and in writing, whether purchased coins may be staked player-v-player at all — this is the one genuinely unsettled India question and it must be priced, not assumed

    ISSUER
    Indian counsel; ultimately OGAI (determination) or a court
    COST
    Opinion cost; UNVERIFIED
    LEAD TIME
    Before enabling any purchase flow for India
    APPLIES TO
    A

    NEEDS FIRST Final coin-economy spec

    AUDIT CORRECTION — REPLACES THE PRIOR "DECISIVE RISK" FRAMING. The previous version of this lane asserted that purchased, non-cashable coins staked player-v-player are automatically an online money game and that the escrow duel "flips from lawful to criminal" the instant coins become purchasable. That is withdrawn as overstated. The question turns on s.2(1)(j): "anything recognised as equivalent or convertible to money and includes credits, coins, token or objects ... which is purchased by paying money directly or by indirect means or as part of, or in relation to, an online game". On the CONJUNCTIVE reading this lane now adopts consistently, a non-transferable, non-redeemable coin with no external market is NOT "other stakes" and the duel is lawful; this is also the reading of the most on-point published 2026 analysis (Outlook/Respawn, "Pocket casinos"), which concludes such a product "almost certainly falls within PROGA's definition of an online social game". On the aggressive reading, "which is purchased" is self-sufficient and the duel is caught. Reinforcing the permissive side: s.2(1)(g)'s winning limb reads "monetary AND other enrichment" (conjunction verified verbatim), so winning further non-monetary in-game credit alone may not satisfy it. NO OGAI determination and NO judgment resolves this. Reasonable Indian counsel currently differ. Get the opinion before you build.

  • Architect two hard-separated currency ledgers: PURCHASED currency (cosmetics only, never stakeable, never winnable, never transferable) and FREE currency (duel stakes only, never purchasable, no cash value)

    ISSUER
    Self-imposed; classified by OGAI under the five-factor determination test [RULE NUMBER INDICATIVE ONLY — 2026 Rules gazette text not obtained; secondary sources conflict]
    COST
    Engineering cost only
    LEAD TIME
    Before enabling any purchase flow
    APPLIES TO
    A

    NEEDS FIRST Decision to monetise Model A; outcome of the counsel opinion above

    CORRECTED STATUS (audit): this is a RISK-ELIMINATING design choice, not a legally compelled one — the prior version presented it as compelled by a settled reading of s.2(1)(j) that is in fact contested. It remains the recommendation, because it makes the contested question moot in a jurisdiction where the offence is cognizable and non-bailable and directors are personally liable, and because it costs engineering time rather than an unquantifiable criminal-law exposure. If you build it: beware "or by indirect means" in s.2(1)(j) — bundling free stake-currency into a cosmetic purchase, or granting it on subscription, arguably re-taints it. Remove any gifting, trading or account-transfer path regardless of which ledger design you choose: external convertibility is the one factor that defeats the permissive reading on its own terms.

  • Remove or India-gate coin-purchase entry into Expeditions and any paid-entry event with a PRIZE OF VALUE; prize-free entries and non-purchasable points staking are the safe side

    ISSUER
    Self-imposed; classified by OGAI under the five-factor determination test [RULE NUMBER INDICATIVE ONLY — 2026 Rules gazette text not obtained; secondary sources conflict]
    COST
    Engineering cost only
    LEAD TIME
    Before any India monetisation
    APPLIES TO
    A, C

    NEEDS FIRST Ledger decision above

    "Coins buy entries" converts Model A into Model C — which is unlawful — if the entry leads to a prize of value. This limb is NOT contested and is the sharpest single India constraint on Model A. Expedition points as currently designed (minted by play, not purchasable, not convertible) sit outside s.2(1)(j) on the conjunctive reading this lane adopts throughout: they are neither "recognised as equivalent or convertible to money" nor purchased. Note this is the SAME construction applied to purchased coins two items above — the prior version of this lane applied the conjunctive reading here and a self-sufficient reading there, which the audit correctly flagged as incoherent.

  • Ship the 2026 Rules' USER-SAFETY DUTIES FOR ONLINE SOCIAL GAMES — age verification, parental controls, session/time limits, fair-play monitoring and a grievance-redressal mechanism — on the LAWFUL models and on the current free product

    ISSUER
    Online Gaming Authority of India (OGAI), MeitY
    COST
    Engineering cost; UNVERIFIED
    LEAD TIME
    UNVERIFIED — treat as live now that the Rules are in force (1 May 2026)
    APPLIES TO
    A, D (and the current free product)

    NEEDS FIRST Any India availability, monetised or not

    AUDIT ADDITION — MISSING FROM THE PRIOR CHECKLIST ENTIRELY. The audit reports that the 2026 Rules impose age verification, parental controls, time limits, fair-play monitoring and grievance redressal on ONLINE SOCIAL GAMES GENERALLY — i.e. on Models A and D, the two lawful models — not only on money games or registered games. UNVERIFIED: I could not corroborate the scope or the rule numbers from the secondary sources reachable (SCC Online, Bar & Bench, Legal500 and ipleaders all summarise the money-game prohibition and the registration architecture without enumerating affirmative duties for permitted categories), and the gazette text of the Rules was not obtained. This is the single most consequential gap in the prior version: a founder working the old checklist would have shipped Model A or D into India believing there were no India-specific gaming obligations at all beyond tax and data. Verify against the gazette text before launch and budget engineering for it.

  • Establish the APPEAL ROUTE against an adverse OGAI determination, cessation direction or blocking order before you need it

    ISSUER
    Appellate Authority — reported as the Secretary, MeitY; thereafter High Court writ jurisdiction
    COST
    Legal cost; UNVERIFIED
    LEAD TIME
    30 days from the decision to file (reported); 30 days for disposal (reported)
    APPLIES TO
    A, B, C, D

    NEEDS FIRST An adverse determination or direction

    AUDIT ADDITION — the prior version stated that the final Rules "dropped the Grievance Appellate Committee tier" and then gave no appeal path at all. That statement is WITHDRAWN as fabricated: it is contradicted by the brief's own sources. Bar & Bench reports appeals against Online Gaming Authority decisions going to the Secretary, MeitY as Appellate Authority, with 30 days to file and disposal targeted within 30 days of receipt; Mondaq reports "30-day timelines for Authority and Appellate Authority decisions"; ipleaders describes a tiered route — platform grievance officer, then OGAI adjudication, then final appeal to Secretary MeitY, then High Court writ. Rule numbers UNVERIFIED and conflicting (ipleaders puts grievance in Part V; Legal500 cites rule 20 for user grievance appeals). Given that enforcement is fast, intermediary-led and happens BEFORE any hearing, this route is the founder's only remedy and must be identified in advance.

  • Build DPDP child-data compliance: age assurance with real verification, verifiable parental consent for under-18s, and a hard switch that disables behavioural tracking, profiling and targeted advertising for any child user

    ISSUER
    Data Protection Board of India / MeitY
    COST
    Engineering cost; penalty exposure up to ₹200 crore for s.9 breaches and up to ₹250 crore for security-safeguard failures
    LEAD TIME
    Substantive obligations bite 13 May 2027
    APPLIES TO
    A, B, C, D (and the current free product)

    NEEDS FIRST Any processing of personal data of Indian users

    Digital Personal Data Protection Act, 2023 (Act 22 of 2023) treats everyone under 18 as a child. s.9(1) requires verifiable parental consent; s.9(3) prohibits tracking, behavioural monitoring and targeted advertising DIRECTED AT children. CORRECTED (audit): the prior version called s.9(3) "an absolute bar, not a consent-waivable one". The second half is right — consent cannot cure it. "Absolute" is wrong: s.9(4) empowers the Central Government to exempt classes of data fiduciaries, or processing for prescribed purposes, from BOTH s.9(1) and s.9(3), and the DPDP Rules 2025 exercise that power (reported at the Fourth Schedule). The exemptions are unlikely to reach a quiz game, but the architecture matters if you are testing whether a narrow exemption is available. CORRECTED (audit): the prior version's conclusion that "the cheapest compliant answer is a genuine 18+ gate for India" is WITHDRAWN. An 18+ gate is not a DPDP exit — the DPDP Rules 2025 require a data fiduciary to exercise DUE DILIGENCE to verify that a user claiming to be an adult is in fact not a child, which is precisely why the Rules prescribe DigiLocker / virtual-token-based verification; a self-declaration checkbox is not due diligence. An 18+ gate therefore CREATES a verification obligation rather than removing one, the 2026 Gaming Rules are reported to impose age verification on online social games in their own right, and DPDP applies in full to adult users regardless. Phasing: rules 1, 2 and 17-21 from 13 November 2025 (Board constituted); Consent Manager registration from 13 November 2026; full substantive compliance from 13 May 2027. THIS REMAINS THE BIGGEST NON-GAMING COST FOR INDIA: the existing cohort/retention/session analytics would be unlawful as applied to minors.

  • Publish a DPDP-compliant consent notice, appoint a contact for data-principal grievances, and stand up breach-notification and data-erasure workflows

    ISSUER
    Data Protection Board of India
    COST
    UNVERIFIED
    LEAD TIME
    By 13 May 2027
    APPLIES TO
    A, B, C, D

    NEEDS FIRST DPDP gap analysis

    Itemised, plain-language notice; consent withdrawal as easy as giving it; erasure on withdrawal. Significant Data Fiduciary designation (DPDP Act s.10; DPDP Rules 2025 rule 13, rule number UNVERIFIED) brings audits, algorithmic due diligence and possible restrictions on transferring specified personal data outside India — unlikely at launch scale but re-test on growth. CITATION CAVEAT (audit, contested): the prior version cited the DPDP Rules 2025 three times as "G.S.R. 846(E), 13 November 2025". The G.S.R. number could not be corroborated from any source reached and the date is contested (Wikipedia gives 14 November 2025 for notification). Cite the Rules by name only until the gazette reference is checked.

  • If the product hosts ANY third-party content (chat, user profiles, user-submitted questions, leaderboard names), comply with IT Rules 2021 intermediary due diligence: publish rules/privacy policy/terms, appoint a Grievance Officer with Indian contact details, and meet the takedown and grievance timelines

    ISSUER
    MeitY
    COST
    UNVERIFIED
    LEAD TIME
    In force now; 2021 Rules as amended — the Grievance Appellate Committee route has been live since October 2022, the latest amendments effective 20 February 2026
    APPLIES TO
    A, B, C, D

    NEEDS FIRST Determination of whether you are an "intermediary" at all

    Information Technology (Intermediary Guidelines and Digital Media Ethics Code) Rules, 2021, as amended. A pure publisher app with no third-party content is NOT an intermediary — but it also has no s.79 safe harbour. CORRECTED (audit — misattribution): the prior version said the 2026 amendment "adds a Grievance Appellate Committee route with a 30-day user appeal window". IT DOES NOT. The Grievance Appellate Committee and the 30-day user appeal window were created by the Information Technology (Intermediary Guidelines and Digital Media Ethics Code) AMENDMENT RULES, 2022 (rule 3A), in force since OCTOBER 2022 — four years earlier. Building to a 2026 baseline for the GAC would mis-date your compliance obligations by four years: the appeal route is already live and already applies to you. What the 2026 amendment (notified 10 February 2026, effective 20 February 2026) is confirmed to add: a 3-hour takedown for contempt/defamation-type categories, quarterly user notification, and labelling/metadata duties for synthetically generated (AI) content — relevant if any questions, art or voice are AI-generated. IMPORTANT: do NOT build to the 2023 "online gaming intermediary" / self-regulatory body regime (rules 4A/4B). No SRB was ever designated, the operative provisions never came into force, and PROG Act s.18 gives the Act overriding effect.

  • Scrub all advertising, app-store listing copy, influencer briefs and affiliate contracts of anything that promotes or induces money gaming

    ISSUER
    OGAI / MeitY; Ministry of Information and Broadcasting
    COST
    Review cost only
    LEAD TIME
    Before any India campaign
    APPLIES TO
    A, B, C, D

    s.6 is a standalone offence: making, causing to be made, aiding, abetting or inducing any advertisement that "directly or indirectly promotes or induces any person to play any online money game". Penalty under s.9(2): up to 2 years or ₹50 lakh or both; repeat offences carry a minimum 2 years and minimum ₹50 lakh under s.9(5). Note s.10 makes only ss.5 and 7 cognizable and non-bailable — s.6 is not. Affiliates and influencers are caught in their own right, so indemnities alone are insufficient. Language like "stake", "bet", "pot", "escrow", "winnings" and "cash out" in India-facing copy invites a suo motu determination even for a lawful product — and it is precisely the evidence a regulator taking the aggressive reading of s.2(1)(j) would use against a purchased-coin duel.

  • Rewrite terms of service to state that coins have no monetary value, are non-transferable, non-redeemable, non-refundable in cash, and confer no property right

    ISSUER
    Self-imposed; evidential before OGAI on the determination factors [RULE NUMBER INDICATIVE ONLY — 2026 Rules gazette text not obtained; secondary sources conflict]
    COST
    Legal drafting cost; UNVERIFIED
    LEAD TIME
    Before any India monetisation
    APPLIES TO
    A, D

    NEEDS FIRST Ledger decision

    The determination factors are reported to include whether rewards or in-game assets "can be transferred, redeemed, monetised or used outside the game environment". On the conjunctive construction of s.2(1)(j) this lane adopts, these terms are not just evidential — non-convertibility is the operative element of the defence. But terms are not a defence on their own: the architecture has to match them, and a live secondary market or an account-trading path defeats them regardless of what the terms say. Also cover subscription auto-renewal disclosure and cancellation under the Consumer Protection Act, 2019 and the Consumer Protection (E-Commerce) Rules, 2020.

  • Write and rehearse an India WIND-DOWN plan: what happens to Indian users' coin balances, in-flight duels and subscription entitlements the day a feature is geo-blocked or the app is delisted

    ISSUER
    Self-imposed; consumer exposure under the Consumer Protection Act, 2019 and the Consumer Protection (E-Commerce) Rules, 2020
    COST
    Refund and engineering cost; UNVERIFIED
    LEAD TIME
    Before India launch — not after a takedown
    APPLIES TO
    A, B, C, D

    NEEDS FIRST India launch decision

    AUDIT ADDITION — missing entirely from the prior version, which described enforcement as delisting and payment cut-off "within days" and then gave no guidance on the aftermath. s.5 bites immediately and enforcement is intermediary-led, so the realistic scenario is that Indian users lose access overnight with unspent purchased balances and unresolved duels. Decide in advance: refund policy for unspent purchased currency, settlement rule for in-flight duels, pro-rata subscription refunds, chargeback handling and dispute volume, and consumer-protection exposure under the Consumer Protection (E-Commerce) Rules 2020. UNVERIFIED: the specific e-commerce-rules duties that would attach to a forced wind-down.

  • Check the Prize Competitions Act, 1955 and State prize-competition and lottery legislation before designing ANY free-entry, AMOE-style or skill-prize workaround

    ISSUER
    State governments; Central Government under the Prize Competitions Act, 1955
    COST
    Advisory cost; UNVERIFIED
    LEAD TIME
    Before designing any prize mechanic for India
    APPLIES TO
    B, C

    AUDIT ADDITION. The prior version stated flatly that "there is no sweepstakes doctrine, no dual currency carve-out and no alternative method of entry defence anywhere in Indian law" and never mentioned the governing statutes. The correct statement is narrower and more useful: there is no PROGA carve-out for sweepstakes, dual currency or AMOE, AND the instruments that actually govern free-entry and skill-based prize competitions in India — the Prize Competitions Act, 1955 and State prize-competition/lottery legislation — CONSTRAIN such designs rather than enabling them. A US founder reaching for an AMOE redesign must clear both PROGA and this layer. UNVERIFIED: the scope of the 1955 Act as applied to an online quiz, and which State enactments bite; obtain a State-by-State view from Indian counsel if any prize mechanic is contemplated.

  • Treat the CURRENT FREE PRODUCT as in-scope for India now — do not defer India work until monetisation

    ISSUER
    OGAI / MeitY; Data Protection Board of India
    COST
    Engineering and review cost; UNVERIFIED
    LEAD TIME
    Now, if the free product has Indian users
    APPLIES TO
    current free product, A, D

    NEEDS FIRST Indian users of the existing free build

    AUDIT ADDITION — CORRECTS A FRAMING ERROR THAT RUNS THROUGH THE PRIOR VERSION. The prior version repeatedly called the existing free product "safe" and said India obligations begin "at the first rupee". That is right on the GAMING-OFFENCE analysis only: free-minted, non-convertible coins are not "other stakes" and no money game is offered. It is wrong as a statement about India obligations generally. Two regimes attach to a free product with Indian users: (a) the 2026 Rules' social-game safeguards (age verification, parental controls, time limits, fair play, grievance) — UNVERIFIED as to scope, see the dedicated item; and (b) the DPDP child-data regime, which applies to the existing cohort/retention/session analytics as applied to Indian minors irrespective of whether any money changes hands. Audit and remediate the free build's India posture now.

  • Obtain a written India opinion from Indian counsel before enabling any real-money flow, and re-test after the Supreme Court rules

    ISSUER
    Indian counsel (gaming/technology practice)
    COST
    UNVERIFIED
    LEAD TIME
    Before India monetisation
    APPLIES TO
    A, B, C, D

    NEEDS FIRST Final product spec and ledger architecture

    THIS IS A COMPLIANCE CHECKLIST, NOT LEGAL ADVICE. The points that genuinely need local counsel, in priority order: (1) THE CONTESTED QUESTION — whether purchased, non-cashable coins staked player-v-player are "other stakes" under s.2(1)(j), and whether the "monetary and other enrichment" conjunction in s.2(1)(g) helps; (2) whether the 2026 Rules impose age-verification, parental-control, time-limit, fair-play and grievance duties on online social games, and whether registration triggers India data residency — both UNVERIFIED here and both cost-bearing; (3) whether cosmetic loot boxes / randomised rewards survive the determination factors in OGAI's hands; (4) whether you are an "intermediary" for IT Rules purposes; (5) the FDI characterisation; (6) SEP/PE exposure; (7) whether an 18+ gate with real verification or verifiable parental consent is the cheaper DPDP path for your funnel — noting that the gate is NOT an exit from verification; (8) whether a foreign entity with no Indian presence can file a determination application, and the exposure of doing so.

Risks specific to India

  • THE DECISIVE OPEN QUESTION (RESTATED AFTER AUDIT): whether purchased, NON-CASHABLE coins staked player-v-player constitute an "online money game". The prior version of this lane asserted as fact that they do, and that the escrow duel "flips from lawful to criminal" the instant coins become purchasable. THAT ASSERTION IS WITHDRAWN. The point is genuinely unsettled: no OGAI determination, no judgment, and reasonable Indian counsel differ. The permissive reading (conjunctive s.2(1)(j): the thing must be BOTH convertible to money AND purchased) is supported by the most directly on-point published 2026 analysis and by the "monetary AND other enrichment" conjunction in s.2(1)(g). The restrictive reading is available on the words and is the one a policy-minded regulator would reach for. The RISK is the unquantifiability, not a known answer — and it sits in a regime where the offence is cognizable and non-bailable.
  • Criminal, personal and non-bailable. s.10 makes ss.5 and 7 cognizable and non-bailable; s.11 makes anyone "in charge of, and responsible to, the company for the conduct of that part of the business" liable, plus any consenting/conniving director, manager, secretary or officer (independent and non-executive directors not in actual decision-making are carved out); s.16 permits entry and search of "any place, whether physical or digital", arrest without warrant, and overriding of access controls. This is not a regulatory-fine regime. It is also why a contested construction should be bought out with engineering (two ledgers) rather than argued about after launch.
  • Extraterritorial reach is explicit. s.1(2): the Act "applies to online money gaming service offered within the territory of India or operated from outside the territory of India". The long title names services operating "from foreign jurisdictions". Incorporating in Delaware or Dublin does not put Models B or C outside the Act — it only changes the enforcement route to blocking, delisting and payment cut-off.
  • Enforcement is fast and intermediary-led, not court-led — and the appeal happens afterwards. OGAI became operational on 1 May 2026 and issued its first advisory on 29 July 2026 to app stores, cloud providers, ISPs, telcos, banks, social media intermediaries, OTT platforms and advertising agencies, citing ss.5, 6 and 7. 8,376 betting/gambling URLs had been blocked as of 28 March 2026, over 4,800 of them after the Act. Expect removal from Indian app storefronts and payment refusal within days of a complaint. CORRECTED (audit): there IS an appeal route — reported as the Secretary, MeitY as Appellate Authority, 30 days to file and 30 days for disposal, then High Court writ jurisdiction. The prior version wrongly said the appellate tier had been dropped and left the founder with no remedy. Identify the route before you need it.
  • The blocking power's relationship to s.69A of the IT Act 2000 is CONTESTED. The prior version asserted that s.14 permits blocking "notwithstanding s.69A". That is UNVERIFIED — the provision was not quoted. Whether s.14 overrides s.69A or operates through it and the 2009 Blocking Rules determines whether the s.69A procedural safeguards (committee, hearing, reasoned order) attach to a blocking order against the app. Material to any contingency plan.
  • The Supreme Court challenge is pending and there is NO stay. Head Digital Works v Union of India, T.C.(C) No. 133/2025, before a three-judge bench of CJI Surya Kant, J. Joymalya Bagchi and J. V.M. Pancholi; High Court petitions transferred under Article 139A in September 2025; hearings ran through 2026 with a listing on 5 August 2026; no judgment found as at 14 September 2026. The Act came into force on 1 May 2026 notwithstanding the challenge. Plan on the Act standing; a favourable judgment would be upside, not a basis for launching now.
  • Classification can be revisited at any time and OGAI can determine SUO MOTU. Determinations are reported to bind only your product and only while the model is unchanged; any move toward staking is a material change triggering reclassification, cessation directions, advertising prohibition and blocking. Rule numbers for this machinery are INDICATIVE ONLY (see corrections).
  • Social-casino and loot-box mechanics are the live policy debate, and a purchased-coin duel is at its centre. Indian commentary in 2026 openly describes cosmetic-currency games as exposing a "loophole" in PROGA — real money in, no cash out, casino psychology. Note the two-sidedness: the same commentary that calls it a loophole concludes it is currently LAWFUL. A loophole is a thing that works until it is closed. MeitY can notify a category of online social games into MANDATORY registration at any time, and the reward-monetisation determination factor gives OGAI a hook. A randomised-reward cosmetic economy in a game with duel framing remains the highest-scrutiny design you could ship into India.
  • Oversight of social games is complaint-driven — which cuts both ways. There is no pre-clearance requirement, so you can ship without approval; but a competitor, a parent or a consumer group can trigger a suo motu determination, and your first contact with OGAI will then be adversarial rather than a voluntary application you controlled. Weigh that against the disclosed costs of filing voluntarily (data residency, regulator exposure, eligibility uncertainty).
  • AFFIRMATIVE DUTIES ON THE LAWFUL MODELS ARE A REAL AND LARGELY UNMAPPED COST (audit addition). The 2026 Rules are reported to impose age verification, parental controls, time limits, fair-play monitoring and grievance redressal on online social games generally — i.e. on Models A and D and on the current free product. I could not corroborate the scope from the sources reachable and the gazette text was not obtained. The risk is that a founder treats "lawful" as "unregulated" and ships with no age gate, no parental controls and no grievance officer.
  • DPDP's under-18 rules are a product constraint, not a policy document — and an 18+ gate is not an escape. Everyone under 18 is a child; s.9(3) prohibits tracking, behavioural monitoring and targeted advertising directed at children and consent cannot cure it (though s.9(4) permits Government-prescribed exemptions, unlikely to reach a quiz game); penalties reach ₹200 crore for s.9 breaches and ₹250 crore for security-safeguard failures. The DPDP Rules 2025 require due diligence that an adult claimant is not a child, so a self-declared 18+ checkbox creates an obligation rather than removing one. Existing cohort/retention/session analytics and any personalised question-difficulty or ad targeting would be unlawful as applied to Indian minors. Compliance bites 13 May 2027.
  • App-store policy is a second, independent gate. Apple and Google separately restrict real-money gaming apps in India to licensed operators — and with PROGA there is no licence to hold. Even a lawful Model A/D build can be caught by over-broad store review if the listing copy uses staking language. Store review is also the fastest enforcement channel against you.
  • State law has NOT been mapped away (audit addition). The Public Gambling Act, 1867 and State adaptations still govern offline activity; Tamil Nadu, Karnataka, Andhra Pradesh and Telangana legislated against online gaming before PROGA; and the Prize Competitions Act, 1955 plus State prize-competition and lottery legislation govern free-entry and skill-prize competitions — the very design space a US founder would reach for as an AMOE workaround. s.18 gives PROGA overriding effect to the extent of INCONSISTENCY, which is not the same as extinguishing additional State duties. The prior version's statement that no such doctrine exists anywhere in Indian law is withdrawn.
  • Advertising liability attaches to people you do not control. s.6 catches anyone who makes, causes to be made, aids, abets or induces a money-gaming advertisement — affiliates, influencers, ad networks and celebrity endorsers in their own right. Contractual indemnities do not remove their criminal exposure or the reputational blast radius.
  • WIND-DOWN EXPOSURE (audit addition). If a feature is geo-blocked or the app delisted, Indian users lose access overnight holding unspent purchased balances, in-flight duels and live subscriptions. Refunds, chargebacks and consumer-protection exposure under the Consumer Protection (E-Commerce) Rules 2020 are unmodelled. Decide the policy before launch.

CORRECTED: this is TEN states with an EXPRESS DUAL-CURRENCY PROHIBITION (CA, CT, IN, LA, ME, MT, NJ, NY, OK, TN) plus TWO with an OPEN-ENDED ENFORCEMENT-AUTHORITY GRANT and no statutory dual-currency definition (IA, NV). The brief counted all twelve as bans; they are not the same compliance question. Several also make YOUR VENDORS liable. CA AG/DOJ; CT DCP; IN Gaming Commission; IA Racing & Gaming Commission; LA Gaming Control Board; ME Gambling Control Unit; MT Dept of Justice Gambling Control; NV Gaming Control Board; NJ DGE; NY Gaming Commission and AG; OK; TN AG Statutory penalties if breached: CA $1,000-$25,000/violation plus up to 1 yr (confirmed); NY $10,000-$100,000/violation (confirmed); OK Class C2 felony (confirmed); CT Class D felony; NJ $100,000 first offence / $250,000 subsequent (text confirmed, enactment UNVERIFIED); IN civil to $100,000 (UNVERIFIED); LA to $100,000 plus racketeering (UNVERIFIED). In force as at 14 Sep 2026 for every one of these except Oklahoma. [AUDIT ADDITION] EFFECTIVE-DATE SEQUENCING: OKLAHOMA S.B. 1589 takes effect 1 NOV 2026 and is the ONLY listed prohibition not yet operative today. Dates: NY 5 Dec 2025 (confirmed); CA 1 Jan 2026 (confirmed); CT and MT 1 Oct 2025; OK 1 Nov 2026 (confirmed, veto overridden 14 May 2026). NJ '15 Aug 2025', IN and IA '1 Jul 2026', ME 'Jul 2026', TN '22 May 2026' and LA '1 Aug 2026' are all UNVERIFIED signing/effect dates — [CONTESTED — audit flagged at "possible" confidence] keep them as working assumptions only and confirm from the enrolled bills before they enter a launch plan. Do NOT stage the geofence to launch into Oklahoma before 1 Nov 2026: the window is seven weeks, the conduct is a Class C2 felony from day one after, and there is no wind-down provision. Legal determination of whether FACT//DUEL 'simulates casino-style gambling' — most of the ten express bans require that element and a trivia duel arguably does not, but CA Penal Code s.337a(a)(6) and FL s.849.14 catch skill wagering independently. In IOWA and NEVADA there is no such definitional element to argue against: the question is whether the Iowa Racing and Gaming Commission or the Nevada Gaming Control Board decides to act, which cannot be resolved by reading the statute and is therefore HARDER, not easier, to plan around.
US-063 United States Decide whether you will HOLD player funds at all — and then answer the THRESHOLD question the brief skipped: are you transmitting money TO ANOTHER PERSON? CORRECTED: state money transmitter licensing is not an automatic consequence of holding player funds. State banking/financial-institution departments, applied for through NMLS (Nationwide Multistate Licensing System) IF required: application fees commonly $500-$10,000 per state plus investigation, fingerprint and NMLS processing fees; surety bonds from $10,000 (WA, WY) to $500,000 (NY), California scaling to $7,000,000 on volume; tangible net worth the greater of $100,000 or 3% of total assets up to $100m under the MTMA formula. All figures are aggregated from licensing-consultancy and law-firm sources, NOT from state fee schedules — re-price from current NMLS state-specific requirements before budgeting. 3-12 months per state for a complete application; New York and California commonly 12-18 months or longer Audited financials, business plan, BSA/AML programme, background checks on control persons, FinCEN MSB registration (federal — out of this lane)
